## R: red snow–road-keeper

### red snow
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  At this place, 6,050 feet high, may be seen small patches of that peculiar 1-celled plant, <i>Protococcus</i> or red snow. The presence of this growth is said to be an indication of the very permanent character of the snow on which it occurs. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/353</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

### red socks
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He is not the type, he insists, that files red socks in the red-sock drawer. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,A19</i>)

>  Over the years many New Zealanders contributed to the campaign to bring back the Cup with red socks, etc. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  All wore red socks in memory of their friend, who made lucky red socks a symbol of New Zealand's unified efforts during the 1995 America's Cup Challenge off San Diego. <i>(Dec 15, 2001,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  This time, Team NZ and Blake seemed much more the big guys, splashing about conspicuously in the local pond, buying in red socks from Korea. <i>(Mar 12, 2000,Dominion Sunday Times,A9</i>)

>  [Hdg] A red socks moment. <i>(Oct 7, 2004,Wellingtonian,47</i>)

>  Schnackenberg was the only central figure remaining from those glorious red sock days when the America's Cup was first declared New Zealand's cup. <i>(Dec 4, 2004,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  I sat in my red socks, alone in my retirement home room, happily absorbed in what I was seeing and hearing and life felt very flat when it was all over. <i>(May 2000,North & South,14</i>)

>  Jim Bolger, in his most natural television performance yet, ably fronted the red socks campaign. <i>(May 27, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  This coming America's Cup will be huge and, if the Kiwis keep busting their millennium masts at the current rate, we'll not only have to all wear Red Socks, but Red Knickers as well. <i>(May 8, 2002,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  It did cross my mind that it might have been a bit red socky in getting involved in the Olympic spirit. <i>(Jun 2000,Radio NZ / National Radio,Al Morrison</i>)

>  He's got up this sort of Dunkirk-Red Socks campaign to get public-spirited Kiwis to do their bit and Switch Off. <i>(Dec 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  [Hdg] Sir Michael to wear red socks for Team NZ. Smelly red socks hardly go with Sir Michael Fay's classic ensemble of navy blazer, blue plaid shirt and caramel trousers. <i>(Feb 15, 2003,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  Skipper Dean Barker will don lucky red socks and ensure the spirit of Sir Peter Blake remains aboard the Black Boat when Team New Zealand and Alinghi clash in the first America's Cup race on Saturday. <i>(Feb 9, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  The previous red-socks gimmick was innocent fun, but the "loyal" campaign is a shameful promotion. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  First it was the virgin in a condom. Now an artist has done something extraordinary with that symbol of yachting good luck, the red sock. He put it on the penis of a carved Maori warrior. <i>iBID.</i> F3 Pakeha artists are often pulled up for their appropriation of Maori imagery but now contemporary Maori artist Chris Bryant has appropriated that rather perverse icon of Kiwi culture, the red sock. <i>(Aug 27, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  New Zealanders felt they were part of it all and Blake's inspirational red socks campaign gave everyone a down home chance to get in behind. <i>(Sep 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,2</i>)

>  Whose idea was the America's Cup red sock campaign?... This was one of those promotional success stories that took off when Kiwis bought numerous red socks to suppport the New Zealand team on the water in 1995... English journalist Jane Redford casually remarked to someone that skipper Peter Blake always aseemed to be wearing red socks when the team did well. Some marketing whiz-kid at TVNZ put two and two together and came up with way more than four; thousands of pairs of patriotic pinky-warmers flew off the production line. <i>(Oct 3, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),92</i>)

>  Taylor said the Red Socks campaigns for Team New Zealand were typical of Sir Peter. "It touched the whole of New Zealand. Hundreds of people went out and bought red socks." <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Evening Post,17</i>)

### Red Squad
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was a natural progression from task force to Red Squad. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  The thousands of people who turned out at the Springbok tour demonstrations saw the effect of the psychological preparation of the Red Squad as they exercised the legitimate use of force in pre-emptive strikes against demonstrators. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### red sticker
 
 <i>v.</i> [to apply, or have applied, a red sticker; in Wellington this indicates the highest risk of earthquake damage, while in Chch it indicates the highest outcome of earthquake damage and renders a property unusable. The verb appears most often as red stickered. Abbreveiated to red, and redded]

>  An inspection lasting just 20 minutes has ended an "infuriating" three-month wait for the owners of Lyttelton homes incorrectly red-stickered in February. <i>(Jun 8, 2011,Press, The,1</i>)

>  "I understand the knee- jerk reaction of safety first, but three months to say, 'Well, you shouldn't have been redded in the first place', that's just crap." <i>(Jun 8, 2011,Press, The,1</i>)

>  Sixty-three properties on Christchurch's Port Hills have been red-stickered this week as a result of Monday's aftershocks. <i>(Jun 17, 2011,NZ Herald,internet</i>)

>  <b>Red stickered:</b> This block of Basin Reserve shops owned by the New Zealand Transport Agency is one of the first buildings in the city to be red stickered by Wellington City Council under its new policy to force owners to strengthen or demolish earthquake-prone buildings. <i>(Jul 2, 2011,Dominion Post,B14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [The label indicating highest level of earthquake damage and renders a building unusable; Christchurch origin but spreading to other centres where the indication is of risk rather than damage.]

>  The red sticker is not a demolition order. It means the building is either so damaged there is a risk of collapse or nearby unstable buildings pose a safety threat. Civil Defence figures show that as of Thursday, 42 buildings have been demolished, with 94 partially "deconstructed". <i>(Mar 19, 2011,Press, The,8</i>)

>  The large four-storey brick mill in Wise St, Addington, was built at the turn of the 20th century but yesterday its fate was decided with a red sticker. <i>(Mar 10, 2011,Press, The,6</i>)

>  To their immense credit, our trio of hard-hats were wonderfully kind and quick to check on our emotional state. The inevitable did happen. The red sticker was produced. The old brick lady was wrapped in red tape, the warning notice was stuck on the garage door and the neighbour's garden cordoned off. Our house was deemed unsafe to inhabit. .. The red sticker was a moment of clarity. The house was unsafe while the aftershocks were occurring; walk away. <i>(Sep 18, 2011,Press, The,10</i>)

>  Experts in related fields were doing preliminary assessments to ease the workload although they could not recommend a red sticker. <i>(Mar 8, 2011,Press, The,11</i>)

### red stickered
 
 <i>adj.</i> [having had a red sticker applied; this indicating the highest level of earthquake damage and rendering a property unusable; Christchurch earthquake context but extended to other places with implications of high risk as opp to damage already done.]

>  A meeting with other owners of red-stickered homes on April 20 put them in touch with geotechnical consultant David Bell. Bell was the only official able to give a ruling, and proved to be a "lifesaver". <i>(Jun 8, 2011,Press, The,1</i>)

>  However, two remain red-stickered because of separate, structural issues. <i>(Jun 8, 2011,Press, The,1</i>)

>  Blackhead: Three houses red-stickered because of slip damage. Road blocked and access for owners only. Te Awanga: More than a dozen houses and campground flooded. fourteen houses were red stickered because of flooding. <i>(May 7, 2011,Dominion Post,6</i>)

### red stripe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a top quality grade of animal sold for the butchery trade.

>  The addition of an extra buyer lifted values for red stripe ewes by a few cents per kg ... <i>(Oct 1, 1998,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  Prime sheep: Quality was also good for a red-stripe yarding of about 1590. <i>(Mar 19, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Environment) pateke [aka brown teal, see]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 98. ANAS CHLOROTIS. Gray. Red Teal. Pateke. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,36</i>)

>  <b>PEACHE</b> Our bag was 4 brace of swamp hens, 11/2 brace of Red teal, 1 brace of Blue duck and 1 brace of Grey duck. ... we shot 2 brace of Black teal ... <i>(Apr 10, 1876,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1876 Apr 10 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 43]

### red tussock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Chionochloa rubra</i>, the red tussock, is the species that imparts the rich, red-brown colour to areas bordering the Desert Road and other tussock grasslands. <i>(Aug 27, 1999,Dominion,14</i>)

>  Down there, webs of standing water transect a rippling carpet of red tussock, <i>Chionochloa rubra</i>... red tussock thrives on waterlogged or frosty flats... [29] In the wetter areas, such as Southland, red tussock raced across the low-lying hills. <i>Ibid.</i> 35 Red tussock, <i>Chionochloa rubra</i>.. grows to a metre high and is one of the most widely distributed tall tussocks, occurring from the volcanic plateau of the central North Island, through most of the South Island and into Stewart Island. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/26</i>)

>  Maori names for tussock varied from region to region. In one southern dialect, <i>wi</i> was used for silver tussock (furher north the same word was applied to rushes) and <i>patiti</i> seemed to be a general term for tall tussocks, with <i>maania</i> an alternative or the word for red tussock in particular. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

>  Some of these grasses, such as red tussock (<i>Chionochloa rubra</i>), can live for more than 100 years and reach 2 metres in height. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/36</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native tussock grass, Chionochloa rubra, usually grazed only by cattle.

>  The land is mainly easy rolling contour with some steep sidlings, covered generally by red tussock, fern and manuka scrub. <i>(Apr 1968,NZ Journal of Agriculture,55</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A big proportion of Ngamatea - the homestead end - is easy rolling country, heavily covered with red tussock two feet or so in height, and difficult country in which to get a clean muster. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,149</i>)

>  Red tussock is the least attractive to stock and is usually cropped at the tips of the leaves. <i>(1970,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Research 535</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Jack has seen photos of the Gentle Annie area in the 1890s, all in native red tussock with odd stands of bush. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,80</i>)

>  This red tussock, or snow grass, grows abundantly in the Mataura district, and would be used very extensively but for the fact that it is very stubborn to work, requiring a long treatment in the digester and a strong solution of soda, which reduces its bulk very much, making it more profitable to use made material, of which a good supply is now procurable. <i>(Feb 24, 1888,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  Even the humourously [sic] described duck ponds, swamps, and red tussock land off Taringatura would grow this fibre plant. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  This evening there was paper made in New south Wales from maize, and also a specimen of paper pulp from the red tussock grass of New Zealand, presented by Mr E. M'Glashan. <i>(Jun 20, 1874,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  In Southland, owing to the methods universally adopted from the Canterbury farmers, land that was covered with "red tussock," wet, cold and bleak, has been brought into cultivation. <i>(Sep 11, 1920,Grey River Argus,6</i>)

### red water fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Azolla rubra</i>

>  The talk was of Lake Kohangapiripiri beyond the lighthouse, of the yellow poppies that grow out of the black sand and look as delicate as paper, of the red water fern called <i>Azolla rubra</i>. <i>(Nov 27, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### Red, White and Blue
 
 <i>n.</i> a betting game played with darts thrown into a board with stripes of the three colours. Money is placed on a chosen colour [ca1935 Havelock Easter Sports]

>  <b>BELTON</b> [Sideshowman speaks] Can a man play Red, White and Blue tomorrow. <i>(1939,Outside the Law,204</i>) [NOTES: nf. P]

### red-bill
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  To the trout-fisherman wandering over the gravelly river beds, bird life just now will be found specially interesting, for Paradise ducks, and other ducks, red bills, and dotterel, and other birds, that usually are to be found along the rivers, are now nursing their young broods. <i>(Nov 12, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> [The] native rat still lingers here and there [in the central North Island], although it has disappeared on the coast; the native dog has become a mongrel; ducks, teals, the red-billed porphyrio or pukeko, a large bittern, a tern, two kinds of gulls, cormorants ... are found here.] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/362</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> <i>(1823,Journal,142</i>) [NOTES: [see quot quail: check =PUKEKO]]

>  The red-bill, <i>porophyrio melanotus </i>or <i>pukeko</i> of the natives. <i>(1851,Canterbury Papers,79</i>) [NOTES: 1849 March 10 Strange's Journal (Naturalist `Acheron') (1851 Canterbury Papers 79)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Red-bill, n bird-name given to </i>Estrelda temporalis, Lath. It is also applied to the <i>Oyster-catchers</i> (q.v.); and sometimes to the <i>Swamp-Hen</i> (q.v.). [Aust. contexts] <i>(1898,Austral English,383</i>)

>  <i>redbill n.</i> gregarious bird, the swamp-hen. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> short for red-billed gull [(obs)]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Seagulls and red-bills poised upon the flat tide water. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,132</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  HAEMATOPUS UNICOLOR, <i>Forst</i>. - Black Red-bill. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/126</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  Grey teal .. together with the red-bill or oyster-catcher, formed another part of the collection. <i>(Feb 15, 1873,Otago Witness,2</i>)

### red-billed gull
 
 <i>n.</i> Larus scopulinus, tarapunga

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The Capt & Gentlemen returned late from a shooting party & brought a large white Heron (<i>Ardea alba</i>), a white & brown Gull [267] (<i>Larus naevius</i>) [[ed. fn] <i>Larus scopulinus</i> of Descr. Anim., p.106; ... Now Red-billed Gull <i>Larus novaehollandiae scopulinus</i> Forster, 1844.] & a young Sky Lark (<i>Alaudaar vensis</i>). [ed. fn] <i>Alauda littorea</i> of Descr. Anim., p.90; ... Now the new Zealand Pipit <i>Anthus novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae</i> (Gmelin, 1789). <i>(Apr 25, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/266</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> LARUS SCOPULINUS. (MACKEREL- \{RED-BILLED\} GULL.) ...<i> Native name\{s\}.</i> Tarapunga, \{Makora, and Akiaki\}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,273</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 2/55}]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Akiaki, ... larus scopulinus red-billed gull. = tarapunga. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,9</i>)

>  RED-BILLED GULL, or AKIAKI (<i>Larus novae hollandiae</i>). <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/208</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> RED-BILLED GULL (<i>Larus novaehollandiae</i>), Tarapunga or Akiaki of the Maoris, is abundant throughout New Zealand, Australia, New Caledonia and South Africa. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>L[arus] novae hollandiae</i> ... Red-billed Gull, Jacky. TIAKI-AKI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,83</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Red-billed gull .. .. akiaki, tarapunga .. .. <i>Larus novaehollandiae</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> RED-BILLED GULL <i>Larus scopulinus Other names</i>: Tarapunga, Mackerel Gull, Jackie (Chathams). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,154</i>)

>  <b>53</b> 194. <i>Larus novaehollandiae scopulinus</i> Forster, 1844 <i>Red-billed Gull (Tarapunga)</i> New Zealand <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,1980</i>)

>  <i>Red-billed Gull Larus novaehollandiae scopulinus</i> FORSTER, 1844 ... The red-billed gull is one of the commonest gulls on the New Zealand coast; it rarely lives inland. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Silver gull, tarapunga, akiaki, mackerel gull, jackie. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,222</i>)

>  The red-billed or mackerell gull - .. the Maoris' tarapunga - is a smaller and prettier bird. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,38</i>)

### red-crowned parakeet
 
 <i>n.</i> a small parrot, Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae, in several subspecies, with red forehead and crown (also called powhaitere).

>  <i>221.b. Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae novaezelandiae</i> (Sparrman, 1787) <i>Red-crowned Parakeet (Kakariki)</i> New Zealand; North Island ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,59,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> ... Half Moon Bay straight out to the ... island of Herekopare, at one time a paradise for native birds, especially red-crowned parakeets. <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,146</i>)

>  <i>Red-crowned Parakeet Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) ... There are four subspecies of the red-crowned parakeet in New Zealand: the New Zealand red-crowned parakeet <i>Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae novaezelandiae</i>, the Kermadec parakeet <i>C.n. chathamensis,</i> and Reischek's parakeet <i>C.n. hochstetteri.</i> ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Kakariki, New Zealand parakeet, Antipodes red-crowned parakeet. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,248</i>)

>  <b>Moon</b> The red-crowned parakeet is becoming increasingly rare on the mainland, due to the fact that they often feed on the ground and are subject to predation by feral cats and stoats. <i>(2002,Photographic Guide to Birds of NZ, A,103</i>)

>  <i>Red-crowned Parakeet Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) ... There are four subspecies of the red-crowned parakeet in New Zealand: the New Zealand red-crowned parakeet <i>Cyanoramphus novaezelandiae novaezelandiae</i>, the Kermadec parakeet <i>C.n. chathamensis,</i> and Reischek's parakeet <i>C.n. hochstetteri.</i> ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Kakariki, New Zealand parakeet, Antipodes red-crowned parakeet. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,248</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 48. PLATYCERCUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Sparrm. Crimson-top Paroquet. Kaka[r]iki. P. Forsteri. FINSCH. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,19</i>)

>  Maori knew the three mainland parakeets - red-crowned, yellow-crowned and orange-fronted - collectively as kakariki. <i>(May 2001,NZ Geographic,51/10</i>)

### red-fronted parakeet
 
 <i>n.</i> kakariki

>  <b>BULLER</b> PLATYCERCUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (RED-FRONTED PARRAKEET.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Kakariki, Kakawariki, Powhaitere, Porere, and Torete ...[59] The Red-fronted Parrakeet is very generally dispersed over the whole country - but is more plentiful in the southern portion of the North Island than in the far north, where the yellow-fronted species predominates. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,58,1888 2nd edn 1/137 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Cyanorhamphus novaezealandiae Sparr. Red-fronted Parrakeet. >  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> It was a delight once again to see New Zealand forest unspoiled. Birds noted were Weka, Bellbird, Tui, Red-fronted Parakeet, ... Grey Warbler and Yellow-breasted Tit. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,100</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The red-fronted parakeet (<i>Cyanorhampus novaezelandiae .. ranges the North, South and Stewart Island forested hills ... Other subspecies occur on the Kermadecs, Chathams and Antipodes; the Macquarie parakeet has died out.</i> [Other species are known: the yellow-crowned <i>C.auriceps auriceps</i> (same distribution as the red-fronted), the rare <i>forbesi</i> (confined to Little Mangere in the Chathams), and the rare orange-fronted <i>C. malherbi</i> ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,290</i>)

>  47. <i>Platycercus novae-zealandiae</i>, Sparrm. Red-fronted Parroquet, Kakariki. - Years ago it was very common in the numerous Fagus woods in the Malvern district ... <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,480</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

### red-headed parakeet

### redback spider
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...it may be prudent to mention that the Australian red-back spider (<i>Latrodectus hasselti</i>) has become firmly established in New Zealand in the last couple of years, so we can deal with three closely related comb-footed spider species in the space of one story... In other words, the black widow is really nothing more than the American katipo, and the red-back is the Australian katipo. <i>(Dec 4, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  redback spider : a theridiid spider (<i>Latrodectus hasselti</i>) that is closely related to the American black widow and prob. identical with the katipo, has a venom which produces neurotoxic symptoms in man, and occurs in Australia, New Zealand, and the major islands of the East Indies - called also <i>red-streaked spider </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1900</i>)

### redbanded perch
 
 <i>n.</i> A fish distinguished by vertical brown bars on otherwise reddish body, found in NZ and southern Australian waters. [<i>Hypoplectrodes huntii</i>, previously <i>Ellerkeldia hunti</i>]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Redbanded perch Ellerkeldia hunti</i> New Zealand and southern Australia. ... Sometimes called the banded sea perch, which causes some confusion with the scorpaenid sea perches ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,85</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>REDBANDED PERCH Ellerkeldia huntii </i>... The redbanded perch is the smallest member of this family found in New Zealand ... Inshore specimens are orange on the back and whitish beneath with a green-tinged head. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,1984</i>)

### redfin bully
 
 <i>n.</i>

The redfinned bully is a handsome species, perhaps the most beautiful of New Zealand's freshwater fish. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,21</i>)

>  A survey of the Kaiwharawhara Stream near Ngaio has uncovered a range of freahwater fish .. Bluegill bullies, Eels, koaro, banded kokopu and redfin bullies were also found in the catchments. <i>(Jan 25, 2005,Independent Herald (Wellington),p9</i>)

>  [caption] <b>Under water:</b> Native fish found in the region's [Wellington] rivers and streams are, clockwise from top left, inanga, redfin bully, torrent fish, banded kokopu, giant kokopu and kaoro. <i>(May 7, 2005,Dominion Post,A21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

These are sometimes caught in large quantities by whitebaiters. The fishermen call them `Dan Doolin Spawn' or `Whalefeed'. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,189</i>)

### redshank
 
 <i>n.</i> (Occasionally) =willow weed (See [redroot](../R#redroot))

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WILLOW WEED (Epilobium sp[p].). - This is also called herb willow, swamp willow, and by confusion redshank and fireweed. It is a native that occasionally grows as a weed in or near ditches. The name is sometimes given to redshank [p.59], and it is true that both polygonum and epilobium grow in damp places, have willow-like leaves and are about 2 feet high. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,126,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>redshank [=] redroot: willow weed: pale willow weed [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,63</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> persicaria, spotted persicaria, knot weed, smart weed,=willow weed, lady's thumb, ? water pepper, ? walker's weed

Perhaps also it is this weed that on the west coast of the South Island is called water pepper or Walker's weed. It is an annual weed of gardens, crops, and wet pastures. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,59,1967 ed.</i>)

### reef
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Any expectations of great gold finding until the dry summer weather s [sic] decidedly premature, and even then `quartz reefing' will, I am convinced, be the main source of a gold export. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  Reef (r&emacr.f), v. i. (Mining) To be engaged in quartz reefing, that is, in mining for gold in a quartz reef. See under QUARTZ <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(goldminers')]

>  The Paparoa Range is swarming with men out prospecting for reefs, while some are busy testing reefs already taken up. <i>(Oct 29, 1896,Otago Witness,22</i>)

>  REEFTON, October 11. The manager of the New Big River mine reports that stoping was continued on the eastern and branch reefs as usual. <i>(Oct 14, 1908,Otago Witness,29</i>)

>  The Alexandra Company have, at a new level, cut the reef, which is about four feet six inches wide, and is, in the words of one of the party, looking well. <i>(Feb 22, 1872,Tuapeka Times,5</i>)

>  Lightband is out somewhere between the Para Para and Appoo's Flat; report says he has found a quartz reef. <i>(Jun 9, 1858,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  ... on the hill sides there are a number of large quartz reefs. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Farrell went down to the gorge to see the reefs; found any amount of specks to a shovel of dirt in several places, also found gold in the reef. <i>(Apr 6, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 6 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 405]

>  A main vein or lode. <i>Ibid.</i> 82 Five men's ground, ... Large reef and leaders. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,55</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> Our reef, or leader, measuring only four inches in thickness, a drive for a considerable distance following the course of the gold-bearing quartz had to be carried out, ... <i>(1874,Boy Travellers,262</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Some of the mines are wholly alluvial, or placer, diggings, others are wholly quartz-mines, which are called reefs in New Zealand and Australia, ... <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,215</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> term in gold-mining; a vein of auriferous quartz. Called by the Calfornian miners a vein, or lode, or ledge. In Bendigo, the American usage remains, the words reef, dyke and vein being used as synonymous, though reef is the most common. (See quotation, 1866.) In Ballarat, the word has two distinct meanings, viz. the vein, as above, and the bed-rock or true-bottom. quotations, ...1869 and 1874.) Outside Australia, a reef means `a chain or range of rocks lying at or near the surface of the water.' (Webster.') <i>(1898,Austral English,385</i>)

>  ~ n. a vein of gold-bearing quartz;-v.t. to work at a reef. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  ~ n. vein of auriferous quartz <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  reef n. vein of auriferous quartz <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Reefs: The quartz veins which were contained in the country rocks at Waihi. They were more correctly described as fissure veins; the term `reefs' is considered to be more appropriately applied to them than the term `lodes' formerly used. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),330</i>)

### reef  track
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; aka reef-track]

>  A promising reef track is now being followed. <i>(Nov 9, 1885,Inangahua Times,2</i>)

>  The contractors are making fair headway, following the reef track west. <i>(Mar 17, 1890,Inangahua Times,2</i>)

>  There is a reef track on both foot and hanging walls, with hard sandstone between them. <i>(Mar 27, 1905,Colonist (Nelson),4</i>)

>  For the week NNo, 3 level has been extended south 14ft, making a total of 56ft, now driven on the reef track. <i>(May 25, 1911,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  When they contain only small veinlets of quartz or merely bands of faulty pug, vein-fissures are referred to as `reef-track' by the miners. 1948 <i>Ibid.</i> 38 ... a crosscut put out eastward for 800ft, from which three reef-tracks were explored. 1948 <i>Ibid.</i> 58 The Blackwater company also explored the ground north of their boundary ... but found merely `reef-tracks' <i>(1948,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 42 (NS)/18</i>) [NOTES: (Gage: Reefton)]

### reef bottom
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  The wash-dirt is of rich yellow color, and a hard reef bottom; the ground in some places is very good to drive in, but in other claims a drift exists which will cause the occupants to slab the hole. <i>(May 10, 1862,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  THE ACRE CREEK RUSH. .. "The only party who have as yet tried to or got gold are the prospectors, who have driven a tunnel from the creek, in an easterly direction, into the terrace for 180ft on top of the ordinary blue reef bottom covered with a thin clayey crust of a chocolate colour. ..." <i>(Jul 8, 1887,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  As a rule, the coarse and nuggety gold is confined to a reef bottom, while the false bottom of whatever description carries fine gold. <i>(Jun 29, 1899,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  This dredge eventually proved that the bed of the Molyneux River was practically lined with gold, one week's return being 1189 oz. and another week 1158 oz., but the gold was found to be the richest in the jointy reef-bottom and crevices of the river bed. <i>(Jun 9, 1927,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  The basement rock is known to alluvial miners as 'reef bottom'; ... The 'reef bottom' may be slate and sandstone, as in Victoria; mica-schist, as in Otago; or granite, as in some parts of New South Wales and Queensland. <i>Ibid.</i> 42 Towards the dip it passes off the schist or `reef' bottom on to the lignite series. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 5 (N.S.)/37</i>) [NOTES: (Park: Cromwell)]

### reef country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The discovery of reefs at Mahakipawa and Waikakaho is about to give an impetus to the reef country at Wakamarina. ... <i>(Oct 25, 1888,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Golden Fleece Mine: The only point now being attacked is in No.15 level, which is extended 89 feet - a portion is in a likely reef country - but which has exposed nothing of real importance. <i>(Apr 20, 1908,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  They are of opinion that it will prove a payable reef country, but will await finer weather before going in again. <i>(Jun 4, 1895,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  "..Nevertheless, any amount of similar reef country is still open to the miner and small capitalist." <i>(Dec 16, 1865,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  REWARD FUND. (<i>To the Editor.</i>) Sir,- Allow me to explain a paragraph which appeared in your issue of this morning to the effect that I offered to contribute GBP200 towards GBP1000 as a reward fund for the discovery of a payable gold-bearing quartz reef country in the County of Westland, south of the Teremakau. <i>(Jul 5, 1872,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Excellent reef country is being penetrated. <i>(Aug 1, 1912,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> Similarly in the Lyell, Antonio Zala had discovered a reef in 1869 ... Naturally, this led to a renewed search for gold ..., and an endeavour to trace the gold back to `reef country'. <i>(1976,Difficult Country,107</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> These fields - or reefs, as they are called - are at present worked by many thousands of digers ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,230</i>)

### reef fish
 
 <i>n.</i> (figurative)

>  [He] has been overseas some of the time, running foreign exchange dealing rooms in Hong Kong and Los Angeles, and the term "reef fish" is new to him. I have to explain that it's a derisive term coined by David Lange to describe the behaviour of the money markets... Lange once referred to market operators as demented reef fish - a term inspired by the aberrant behaviour of tropical fish which dart backwards and forwards en masse in a manner that defies rational explanation. <i>(Nov 27, 1993,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  Highly addictive, with low unit cost, [crack] makes an attractive retail opportunity for resting reef fish or person with experience in the hospitality or service industry. <i>(Dec 2, 1993,City Voice,2</i>)

>  In Mr Lange's book, this is an individual who, like the "demented reef fish" of the money markets, reacts mindlessly to conform with everyone else's mindless reaction <i>(Mar 24, 1996,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Those who feel appalled at the influence the so-called reef-fish - the financial market traders - have over our economy will not be reassured by the tale of [this] man. <i>(Mar 31, 1996,Sunday Star Times,E4</i>)

>  As the currents of the market-place permeate through New Zealand society, it is the voters who have become the new generation of reef fish. <i>(May 27, 1996,Dominion,2</i>)

>  It may even be that the reef-fish who temporarily converge upon [new restaurants] are essential, providing a kick-start for a relatively out-of-the-way new venture. <i>(Oct 19, 1999,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  Since when did the reef fish in the share market attest to anything, I was told, in stern tones. <i>(Dec 4, 1996,Norwester,14</i>)

>  <b>Colin James</b> I've been talking to some of the reef fish this morning. <i>(Dec 11, 1996,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  Last week distress signals were beginning to show; the Reserve Bank hung out a flag, the reef fish shoaled, and interest rates headed north. <i>(Dec 4, 1994,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  What I saw happen here.. was a sort of reef-fish mentality taking over the public debate. <i>(Aug 22, 1997,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Once again, TVNZ's trustworthy reporter Ewart Barnsley intoned ominously as he watched the financial reef fish swirling in yet another frenzy of self-interest over the dollar, home mortgage rates - life itself. <i>(Aug 6, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94 ed.</i>)

>  In South Africa that commercialism is much more up front than in New Zealand and players' agents and various other rugby reef fish are given fairly short shift by the new coach. <i>(Mar 15, 2002,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  [Lange's] characterisation of foreign exchange dealers as "demented reef fish", and of rival political operatives as "poll-driven fruitcakes" have entered the New Zealand lexicon. <i>(Feb 26, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  A decade or so ago, Burge had his bright star, Krondorf, whisked from under his nose by stockmarket reef sharks... <i>(May 25, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  These are things which strike fear into the hearts of many who run the economy today, particularly those David Lange once described as financial reef fish... Financial reef fish.. point out that the country's comparatively stable economic outlook is based on three fundamental policies - the Reserve Bank Act, the Employment Contracts Act and the Fiscal Responsibility Act. <i>(Oct 1995,Metro,88</i>)

>  WHITE'S REEF GOLD MINING COMPANY (LIMITED). <i>(Feb 17, 1888,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  In other words, from the distributed river-gold the geologist works back to the reef gold in the hills. <i>(Sep 12, 1925,NZ Truth,17</i>)

>  `That alluvial gold is of a higher quality than reef-gold from the same neighbourhood.' <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/402</i>)

### reef heron
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Egretta sacra sacra</i>, also called <i> blue heron </i>, occas, <i> blue reef heron </i>(see b. and c. below). See also (blue) crane, matuku-moana. [AND <i> Egretta sacra </i> of coastal mainland Austr. and elsewhere 1848.]

>  <i> Ardea sacra </i>, Gml. Blue, Purple, or Reef heron, Matuku. - May be observed alighting on reefs immediately on the ebb of the tide so that the birds stand in the water. <i>(1885,NZ Journal of Science,557</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>POWELL</b> REEF HERON (<i> Demigretta sacra </i>), Matuku-moana of the Maoris, is a graceful dark slaty grey bird with long beak and legs of bright yellow. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,83</i>)

>  94. Egretta sacra sacra (Gmelin, 1789) <i> Reef Heron (Matuku-moana)</i> Asia to Australia, eastern Polynesia and New Zealand. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,35,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Matuku, n. ... 2. Demigretta matook, blue heron </i>; a shore bird. = <i> matuku-moana ... m&amacr.tukutuku. ... 1. Reef heron (blue heron).= matuku, 2.:</i> a wading bird with long legs, neck and beak. <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,195</i>)

>  <i> Reef Heron Egretta sacra sacra </i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i> Blue reef heron, eastern reef heron, blue crane, blue heron, matuku-moana.</i> ... Reef herons in New Zealand are at the southeastern limit of their mostly tropical range, and for that reason alone may never be common. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,132</i>)

>  Bird enthusiasts are in for a treat with a good chance of seeing .. shags, white-fronted terns, reef herons and oystercatchers ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 8</i>)

### reef in with
 
 <i>v.</i> to throw in with (a person); join with [is this a coinage or nonce use?] [an Italian fisherman speaks]

>  <b>GRACE</b> reef in alonga you an' Rock Codda. <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,I</i>)

### reef off
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> The landlady was slightly mad ... She kept reefing off about how she was going to do this and going to do that to the house ... <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,230</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [reef fish (fig.)](../R#reef-fish-(fig.)))

>  A decade or so ago, Burge had his bright star, Krondorf, whisked from under his nose by stockmarket reef sharks... <i>(May 25, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

### reef snapper
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [snapper](../S#snapper),[reef](../R#reef))

>  <b>WILSON</b> Red snapper or sometimes reef snapper, is a name given to those fish which have spent a good part of their time in rough-bottom areas; they are a deeper, golden red than the normal open-water snapper. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,227</i>)

### reef-fish
 
 <i>v.</i> (figurative) [cf. reef fish n. (figurative)]

>  We said he would reef-fish the financial markets and frighten off investment. <i>(Oct 18, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### reef-fish swings of sentiment
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The Privy Council judges aren't swayed by "the reef fish swings of sentiment to which we are prone", as Franks put it. <i>(Aug 3, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

### reef-fishlike
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  We all go reef-fishlike whenever we read that the local worthies consider the time may have come to jettison the trains and make everybody come in by road. <i>(Jan 28, 2002,Dominion,11</i>)

### reefer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The death is announced of William Williams, who was a pioneer gold reefer. <i>(Sep 30, 1902,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  The inquest on the body of William Davies was held at the Reefers' Rest, Deep Creek, on Tuesday last, the 23rd instant, before Mr John Allen, Coroner. <i>(Jan 26, 1883,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  Reefi.er (-&etilde.r), n. (Mining) A quartz reefer. See under QUARTZ <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  As a reefing country, I am convinced that the small portion of land now open is wonderfully rich. Let the reefers once obtain machinery, and depend upon it the yield will be enormous. <i>(Oct 27, 1867,Trip to the Thames Gold-fields,29,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> A digger comes up here [to Grahamstown], say from the West Coast,/ To go out prospecting, .../ .../ Discovers a reef, and his fortune is made./ .../ He rides in a carriage, race-horses he buys,/ And the people who fawn on this reefer the most,/ Would have turned up their nose at him on the West Coast./ <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,24</i>) [NOTES: (Gold) Also in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 55]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Joseph Berry wrote in the Cromwell Argus:/ Gone is the old `forty-niner',/ Gone, like the days that are past,/ Gone is the sturdy old reefer,/ Gone to his haven at last! <i>(1982,Ghost Towns of NZ,46</i>)

### reefing
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining] (See [also quartz-reefing](../A#also-quartz-reefing))

>  As a reefing country, I am convinced that the small portion of land now open is wonderfully rich. Let the reefers once obtain machinery, and depend upon it the yield will be enormous. <i>(Oct 27, 1867,Trip to the Thames Gold-fields,29,1978 ed.</i>)

>  As a reefing country, I am convinced that the small portion of land now open is wonderfully rich. Let the reefers once obtain machinery, and depend upon it the yield will be enormous. <i>(Oct 27, 1867,Trip to the Thames Gold-fields,29,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOLLOWAY</b> The rising reefing district of Lyell. <i>(Jun 24, 1874,Journal of a Visit,121</i>)

>  ... the oldest reefing-district in New Zealand [is] ... Coromandel. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,362</i>)

>  1890 Mines Report 157 in 1948 NZ Geol. Survey Bull. No 42 (N.S.)/39 <i>(1890,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 42 (N.S.)/39</i>) [NOTES: (Gage, Reefton)]

>  Stone is found in detached blocks along the line of reefing country for 600ft. to 700ft. and runs in narrow scams rich in gold ... . <i>(1890,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 42 (N.S.)/39</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Mines Report 157 in 1948 NZ Geol. Survey Bull. No 42 (N.S.)/39 (Gage, Reefton)]

>  ... a central belt of reefing-country. <i>(1890,Papers Read at the Mining Conference,33</i>)

>  The Try Fluke vein or branches of this vein carrying payable values, were soon traced into the Mariposa, Red Mercury, Great Mercury, and other claims, and it was therefore considered that here at least, a permanent reefing-field existed. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,4(N.S.)/16</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  An application for a licensed holding at Mahakipawa has been lodged at the Warden's office. It is north of the Bright Smile and west of the Welcome claims. The name is the Success Coy.; area, 30 acres; capital, GBP10,000; mode of working, quartz reefing by machinery; term, 21 years; day of hearing, 21st prox.; James R. Brimor and party. <i>(Oct 31, 1888,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> As to reefing, the efforts to prospect have been largely sporadic, and ... there is a feeling that this main source of Westland's gold supply has never been truly tried. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,88</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Quartz crushing for gold ... [or] `reefing' <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,95</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> We must sell out the business and go reefing. <i>(1879,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,164</i>)

>  Reefing is being carried out at a number of places. <i>(1906,Mining Handbook,5</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Reefing is made more difficult by the bush cover over most of the hill country and no doubt many rich reefs have thus been passed over. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,21</i>)

### reefing country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is good reefing country in the crosscut now. <i>(Jun 14, 1904,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  For this week the western crosscut from No 3 level south has been extended 16ft; total 112 ft through good reefing country with more leaders than previously. <i>(Dec 22, 1911,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  Mr. Bleazard had charge of this party of seven men, and remained with them in Coromandel for about three months, at his own charge, while giving his time for nothing. On his return to Auckland, he gave a written report to the treasurer, Captain Salmon, the purport of which was that Coromandel would in all likelihood prove a good quartz reefing country, but would never be worth for alluvial diggings. <i>(Jun 24, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  As a reefing country the Inangahua bids fair to be unsurpassed. <i>(Nov 9, 1870,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Dunn.. is satisfied that the country.. is both an alluvial field and good reefing country. <i>(Sep 26, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### reefing district
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  According to promise, I send you a few lines about our Thames goldfield. .. There is no doubt about this being a rich reefing district, but you must wait patiently until the stone can be crushed before you have gold in any quantity sent to town. <i>(Sep 16, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Great attention is now being given to the Paparoa reefing district. <i>(Oct 14, 1897,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  Everything seems to point to the fact that the time is not far distant when a valuable reefing district will be opened up in the neighbourhood of Browning's Pass. <i>(May 2, 1905,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  No great importance need be attached to the fact that the quartz is rather peculiar looking and very different in quality and appearance from any seen in the Inangahua or any other known reefing district in New Zealand or Australia, because Nature shows diverse methods in the composition of her auriferous lodes. <i>(Oct 29, 1910,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

### reefing field
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This little game has been enacted on every reefing field, but yet people do not take warning. <i>(Mar 3, 1883,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  This is the primary and essential difference between such a reefing field as that at the Thames and an ordinary alluvial digging. <i>(Sep 28, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Vigorous prospecting will now follow, and as all such legitimate enterprise is being liberally subsidised by the Hon. Mr Cadman- who, by the way, has become a fast favourite among the diggers here - the chances are that next summer may see a permanent and payable reefing field opened up in this locality. <i>(Jul 19, 1894,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  That formation in a reefing field means a prolonged life of exploration, of working activity, of steady gold returns depending upon the length of the "makes" or "runs" of gold in the reefs operated upon. <i>(Aug 4, 1923,NZ Truth,13</i>)

### reefing locality
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When on the ground Mr Travenor also went to see most of the now deserted mines in the neighbourhood of Macetown .. and expressed himself surprised that such mining chances were allowed to lie unimproved ; and it will only depend upon the success of the works now in progress at the Premier whether or not Macetown will come once more to the fore as a reefing locality. <i>(Mar 31, 1892,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  This is a very satisfying result, as, in addition to being encouraging to the enterprising prospectors, it is another proof of the richness of the Rough Ridge as a reefing locality. <i>(Jan 1, 1881,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  I am personally acquainted with many of the old Coromandel miners, whose faces I meet in the various reefing localities. <i>(Sep 20, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### reefy
 
 <i>adj.</i> pertaining to a quartz-REEF [goldmining]

>  What little prospects have been got are not of an alluvial character at all, but a light reefy nature. <i>(Aug 29, 1867,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  They found what they term a "reefy rock" about two feet in thickness. <i>(Feb 22, 1872,Tuapeka Times,5</i>)

>  .. for instance, one hole may show a grayish drift from under the surface soil to the washdirt, another yellow clay mingled with small quartz, and the next a brown reefy formation, and so on. <i>(Jul 25, 1885,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  .. but in two or three driblets of creeks coming out from Arm Chair mountain (and evidently on "the strike" of Bradcock's reef) we got several very nice prospects of course [sic] reefy gold. <i>(Mar 5, 1898,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  The gold won from Glasseye Creek [Mt. Radiant] was for the most part of a coarse `reefy' nature, the particles generally varying in weight from 0.5 (half)gr. to 5 dwt. <i>(1910,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 11 (N.S.)/37</i>) [NOTES: (Webb)]

### reference sire
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stud stock breeding] a select and closely studied ram or bull used for artificial insemination.

>  Some reference sires are used in successive years to create genetic links, which allows EBVs to be compared across years. Other reference sires are replaced by superior rams, identified by their across-flock EBVs. Thus the reference sires have a dual role, creating genetic links across flocks and years and producing more rapid dissemination of genetic improvement. <i>(Sep 27, 2002,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### reformative detention
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) [also as RD below]

>  At the other end of the scale is the indeterminate sentence of preventive detention. A sentence of the same name existed in Britain from 1908 to 1967. Introduced in New Zealand in 1954 to replace the sentence of reformative detention (1910) and declaration as an habitual offender and habitual criminal (1906), it remains very much alive here. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (See [also refreshment rooms](../A#also-refreshment-rooms))

>  The recently erected Refreshment Room by Bealey Flat. which has been completed by the Railway Department, was handed over to the proprietor, Mr. John Brake, last week, and was opened on Saturday evening last by a splendid ball at which the whole of the community was entertained. <i>(Sep 1, 1914,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Early this morning a fire broke out in the refreshment room at Te Aute, and the whole of the station buildings and post and telegraph offices were destroyed. <i>(Oct 11, 1895,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  You'd have a problem finding a cup of tea at the Taumaranui railway station - the refreshment rooms were closed down in 1975. <i>(Sep 28, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  A PASSENGER was left behind in a railway refreshment room recently. He got new bread in a sandwhich and swooned. <i>(Sep 12, 1929,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> One or two faces, wanner than the rest, had grown familiar to me during innumerable excursions to railway refreshment rooms the day before. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,175</i>) [NOTES: From R.A. Copland (1977, first published in Listener 1950) :Yes, to the Very End]

 <i>n.</i>

>  On arriving at the site, your rego sticker is checked against a database... <i>(Aug 20, 2002,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  The tyres are down to the canvas and they have no rego or warrant. <i>(Jan 17, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> motor-vehicle licence plate number [Austral. abbreviation of 'registration']

>  I then left but as I was not happy I recorded the rego of the red car. <i>(Sep 17, 2003,Norwester,1</i>)

### regrassing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [He] began regrassing at the rate of 100 hectares a year. <i>(May 16, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  Staggers is a problem for his stock in the autumn and he is part-way through a regrassing programme using novel endophyte ryegrass, though he says he still has reservations about the benefit of regrassing. <i>(Mar 14, 2003,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

### Rehab
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr Saleman said his father went through "rehab" after the war and became a carpenter, mechanic and ran his own stevedoring company. <i>(Mar 16, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

>  `Now, chaps, what about a spot of Rehab.?' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,16</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> `This block's a Soldiers' Settlement. You know the sort of thing. Under Rehab.' I didn't know till he explained. Rehab meant the Rehabilitation of Returned Soldiers, a Government department. It advanced money to buy farms ... <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [incl. attrib. uses]

>  A Rehab. officer outlined the scheme; he offered opportunities for learning carpentering, ... and a host of other trades. <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,5</i>)

>  `I had a bursary,' said the Grim Dig. `Reading, writing and rehab. - every soldier's right.' <i>(Mar 25, 1959,Upper Hutt Leader,5</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I was living in Christchurch at different times, at varsity on Rehab [no stop] for a while. <i>Ibid.</i> 146 -Rehab was the caper you jokers. Got on the carpentry course. Gone into building. <i>Ibid.</i> 163 While the other Rehab jokers got through I was doing everything but swotting. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,28</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `There's a dozen jobs waiting to walk into. I can even apply for my rehab and crash out on my own.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,50</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> When he came out of the army he got a Rehabilitation grant to finish his B.A. The clerk at the Rehab. office advised him to do this. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,26</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Rehab, ... was a common word in the years following the Second World War for `rehabilitation', referring to loans, bursaries and other help given to returned soldiers <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,172</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... he'd never really thought of himself as a soldier or an ex-soldier even though he'd had two years of it before ... eventually going farming on rehab money. <i>(1969,Green River,22</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Well, the Rehab people were sympathetic to my request. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,156</i>)

>  rehab (farm sold on favourable terms to returning soldier) <i>Ibid..</i> 6 We lived out in the wop-wops then - on a rehab place, miles from town up a metal road. Apart from a mate on the next farm I didn't have much to do with anyone outside the family. There was the Plunket Nurse, of course, when the baby was little. And the Bon Brush man, and a few other salesmen. <i>(1988,University Entrance Board Bursaries Exam</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the Rehabilitation Department, which was set up to help servicemen returned from World War II. [ORIGIN: abbreviation, Formed [?d] and ceased as a separate department [?d]: also freq. attrib. or in combin. REHAB. DEPARTMENT, -LOAN (special low interest loans to servicemen), -MONEY etc; and used a a noun. `Rehab. is fixing me up with a loan' or, esp., `I got my loan under Rehab.' referring to the Department or its officers or policies in general. Contract with NZ WW1 repat qv, and British and Austr. Repat. WW2 (1952 Apr 28 Bluey and Curly, Austr cartoon, use repat. (R.Mason). The abbrev. is the usual or expected one: (cf Part: rehab. official abbrev. of rehabilitation ward or department in a hospital since ca1945)]

>  The smaller ones [sc. builders], however, are shying away.. from "rehab. jobs". <i>(Aug 6, 1947,NZ Observer,11</i>)

>  Most rehab farmers did make some sort of a fist of it. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> My rehab. housing loan and part of my capital has made it possible. <i>(1960,Tangahano,255</i>)

### Rehab  bursary
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The others made good use of their Rehab Bursary, made up for the years in the services or behind the wire in the stalag, but I was a washout. <i>Ibid.</i> 239 Blew my back pay on God knows what. Loused up my Rehab Bursary. Never even sent for my campaign medals. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,37</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> While on a Rehab Bursary he gained his Masters Degree. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,16</i>)

### rehab block/ rehabilitation block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm, composed of marginal land or from a large privately-owned property, for which returned servicemen ballot.

>  <b>FEARON</b> He built his house and woolshed on a flat section of land above the river, and in the 1920s procured an extra portion of land from the 'Rehab' block lying to the west ... <i>(1980,Te Wharau,182</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> Rollet lived in an Army hut close to the Tyneside homestead - as did the other single men who had drawn Rehab. blocks. After extensive repairs and replacement of the rabbit fence around all the Rehab blocks .. a programme of poisoning was embarked on. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,189</i>)

>  When the men came home from World War I, they were given the rehabilitation blocks. <i>(Dec 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

### rehab farm
 
 <i>n.</i> [WW1]

>  Dave McGovern took over until after W.W.I. when.. a returned serviceman was settled on it as a Rehab. farm. <i>(1987,Alfredton,315</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> I sent the local secretary of the RSA ten pounds towards expenses in getting the man secure on his farm, which was a rehab. Farm. <i>(1974,Sugarbag Years,48</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [WW2]

>  We saw his name in the paper for winning a Rehab. Farm ballot. <i>(Mar 1954,Landfall,8(1) No. 29 : 18</i>)

>  They were breaking in their 526 hectare rehab farm - Anerley - nearly 70km east of Masterton, <i>(Jun 24, 1983,Dominion,P6</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm for which returned soldiers ballot.

>  <b>PEAT</b> The real managers of this 'rehab' farm were the rabbits. <i>(1992,Falcon and the Lark, The,46</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> They were mostly rehabilitation farms and the majority were not dedicated farmers but were placed by the government on unsuitable country where they eked out a peasant lifestyle until mortgages and other miseries drove them to walk off. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,42</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Lindsay Ferguson offered me a job with him on a rehab farm at Tetley Brook. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,155</i>)

>  After returning from war duty, Mr Luxton drew a ballot for a rehab farm at Okato. [rehabilitation farm] <i>(Nov 29, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### Rehab farmer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Most rehab farmers did make some sort of a fist of it... I fully agree.. about the rehab farmers' wives. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Mac, a canny rehab. farmer, took his truck into Invercargill, let loose his two prize dogs, and said, "Now enjoy yourself, have a good time, but be back in two hours or no more leave." <i>(1990,Soldier Country,141</i>)

>  Till the 1980s, a generation of people unique to Australia and New Zealand owned the land. They were rehab farmers, granted their land from a grateful nation for deed and suffering endured in two world wars. <i>(Apr 4, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),25</i>)

### Rehab League
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ?Rehab League, for rehabilitation of handicapped accident victims The Rehab League [title] I am writing to express concern about the article on the Rehabilitation League NZ (Inc) published in the April issue of your magazine. <i>(Jun 1987,NZ Outlook,5</i>)

### Rehab loan
 
 <i>n.</i> [also Rehabilitation loan below]

>  Our little nest egg grew slowly and 1950 we had the opportunity to buy a furnished house in Miramar on a Rehab Loan available to returned servicemen. <i>(Apr 2002,NZ Memories,35/48</i>)

>  With the help of a rehab loan, I belatedly sat for and obtained my engineering qualifications. <i>(Jan 1992,North & South,61</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> My own house and paying it off on the Rehab. Loan at three percent. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,117</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> A house may be bought with a rehab loan or a State Advances loan. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,172</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> As soon as the land was ours, Charlie applied for a Rehabilitation Loan to build a house. <i>(1984,Bread and Roses,74</i>)

### rehab man/rehab cocky
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a returned soldier farming a rehab block in a soldier settlement areas.

>  <b>FEARON</b> His land was not much good for dairy cows however, and when he sold out, the land was subdivided between Engal and two other 'Rehab' men from the Westmere - Bannister and Wyeth. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,183</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> I'd have been farming today on my own, and there's not many Rehab. cockies that've gone broke. <i>(1990,Between the Rivers,108</i>)

### Rehab officer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Value of Maori Rehab. Officers. <i>(Dec 5, 1945,People's Voice,3</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> It was the Rehab officer in charge of this settlement and others. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,53</i>)

### rehab scheme
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a policy that allowed returning servicemen to purchase farms, usually undeveloped and from larger holdings.

>  The original owner, who had acquired the farm under the rehab scheme after World War II was still running the property. <i>(Nov 16, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

### rehab settler
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer settled on the land through a Returned Soldiers' Rehabilitation Scheme.

>  John and Margaret Thomas came to Stronvar as rehab settlers 35 years ago .. When you started as a young rehab settler in the days when the main aim was to quickly become debt-free and stay that way, it's not easy to add even more to your debt load.. <i>(May 27, 1982,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Perhaps because so many of our clients were Rehab settlers who, with wives and young families were working hard to establish themselves ... <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,77</i>)

### rehabilitation
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Rehab](../R#rehab))

>  <b>BOYD</b> The way in which the executive committee of `diggers' immediately opened its ranks to `kiwis' and gradually made way for them ensured the RSA strong, continuing support. A postwar rise in membership of `diggers' indicated their willingness to help with rehabilitation. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,312</i>)

### Rehabilitation Department
 
 <i>n.</i> [also Rehab Department below] (See [Rehab](../R#rehab))

>  Rehabilitation Department n. N.Z. a government department set up after World War II to assist ex-servicemen. Often shortened to rehab. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,968</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 641; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 411]]

>  <b>CASEY</b> I might have known there was something fishy when he said he'd been so messed up by the Rehab. Department that he wasn't going back to them. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,110</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> He'd had to bum around for a few years before approaching the rehab - [for a loan]. <i>(1969,Green River,39</i>)

### rehe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Names - The general name is upokororo (`head full of brains'), derived from the fatness which characterizes it. The small ones are also called rehe, and the large size tirango. <i>(1926,TrNZI,58/638</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

### rehia
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of seaweed [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  A few also of the sea-weeds were eaten; such as, the Karengo, (A tidal species of <i>Laminaria</i> found plentifully from the East Cape to Cape Turnagain), the Rehia, the Rimurapa (<i>D'Urvillea utilis</i>, and some others . <i>(1869,TrNZI,I/III/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)..]

### Reinga
 
 <i>n.</i> (usu. as the/Te Reinga) the northernmost place in NZ, where the departed spirits take flight

>  This area includes all the most northern portion of New Zealand from Te Reinga Point (where the spirits of the old Maoris were supposed to plunge off into the unknown) down to Matapai, on the east coast. <i>(Aug 1, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  From the far-off Ruapuke's coast / To Reinga's haunted height, / The warrior Clans, a countless Host, / Are gathering in their might. <i>(Apr 17, 1858,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>COLLINS</b> In his chart Too-gee has marked an imaginary road which goes the lengthways of Ea-hei-no-mawe, viz. from Cook's Strait to the North Cape, which Too-gee calls Terry-inga. While the soul is received by the good Ea-tooa, an evil spirit is also in readiness to carry the impure part of the corpse to the above road, along which it is carried to Terry-inga, whence it is precipitated into the sea. <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,345</i>)

>  Whether in Niu Tireni or Te Reinga/ Now you live, or in countries over the ocean - <i>(1949,Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse,110</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Currie Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse (`Epilogue') 110]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> R|einga, s. The place of flight, a descent on the side of a rock near the North Cape ... where it is supposd that the departed spirits of the natives take their flight; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,202</i>)

>  I am told the spirit hovers about the body and place of the deceased until the third day, when the priest prays and directs the spirit to the reinga, a place at the North Cape where the natives say the spirits go to and descend. <i>(Aug 2, 1822,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1822 Aug 2 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 254]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> She spoke of the excellency of the Reinga as having conversed with many from thence. <i>(Dec 23, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,269,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> They had much to say about Te Uarahi having died and after taking a survey of the Reinga returned to deliver his account. [ed fn] Te Reinga, the abode of the dead. It was the Maori belief that the dead travelled to Te Reinga [lit., `the leaping place'] at the northernmost tip of the North Island, and there jumped over the cliff into the place of the dead. <i>(Jun 23, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,320,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>W.G. PUCKEY</b> The whole body of New Zealanders firmly believe in the Reinga as the only one place for their departed spirits. As soon as the soul leaves the body it makes its way with all speed to the western coast; if the spirit of a person who resided in the interior, it takes with it a small bundle of the branches of the palm tree as a token of the place where it resided; if one who lived on the coast, the spirit takes with it a kind of grass which grows by the seaside, which it leaves at different places on the road to the Reinga. <i>(Dec 1834,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1834 Dec W.G.Puckey Journal in 1863 Maning Old New Zealand (1948 edn) 318]

>  <b>YATE</b> to the Reinga, a place of torment <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),140</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> When a great man dies, it is customary for a slave to be killed, whose spirit, it is said, departs with him to the Reinga to minister to him. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,67</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Nov 2 in WILY & MAUNSELL Robert Maunsell (1938) 67]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The chiefs are accounted of divine origin by their followers, whose spirits after death are supposed to reign in an abode of the gods called Te Reinga, whose entrance is situated at the gloomy caverns beneath the sea-worn cliffs of the North Cape, (Muri wenua. ) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/58</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> A singular circumstance was related of this fish [Hinanga], that it comes from the Reinga - (Hades) that there are many openings in the side of Tongariro ... <i>(Jun 26, 1846,Journal</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We were off the Reinga this morning when I went on deck, <i>(May 22, 1847,Journal,V/166</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The abode of spirits, they ascribed to a place called Te Reinga, at the north cape. <i>(1883,Forty Years in NZ,201</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/250</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> This cliff was called the Re-i-nga, i.e., the leaping-place. Some who lived near this spot used to affirm that on certain nights they could hear the rush of the spirits as they passed on their way. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,79</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The time was opportune for another feat-day. ... there had not been a marriage ... for months now; and among the long-lived little community, neither had the occasion for a tangi arisen, altough it was evident that poor old Honi Tawa was not far now from the road to the Reinga. [134 ed. note] 2. `not far from the road to Reinga.' [sic] A traditional Maori way of saying close to death, when the spirit of the dead was said to join its ancestors in the mythical homeland of the Maoris, Hawaiki, across the Pacific Ocean, departing fom New Zealand at Cape Reinga. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,3, 134,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Wedding gift)]

>  <b>HULME</b> Love of dolphins, love of whales/ show me the way to Te Reinga/ <i>(1982,Moeraki Conversations,38</i>) [NOTES: 1982 Hulme The Silences Between (Moeraki Conversations) 38]



 
 <i>n.</i> (as the/Te Reinga)

>  <b>MANING</b> One of the ancestors of Haurake ... hearing, even in the R[ei]nga, (the Maori hades) of the warlike renown of one of his sons, became jealous of his fame, . <i>(1862,War in the North,22 fn</i>)

>  All were alike firmly believed to be remembrances of what they had seen in the reinga, or unseen world (or place of the departed,) whither the spirit (Wairua) was supposed to have been during the sleep of the body. <i>Ibid.</i> I/III/42 ... this haunting spirit, or phantom, (Kehua,) which haunted its former place of residence, when in the body, ... differed widely from the sensible intellectual spirit (Wairua), which had departed to the reinga and which was not feared. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/41</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  To `te Reinga', or into the darkness under the earth, went the spirit of the ordinary Maori. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,159</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The spirits of dogs were supposed, like those of men, to pass to the World of Shadows (Te Reinga) but they travelled by a different path than that taken by the souls of human beings. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,169</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Her warrior brother Hitiri ... was not long in following her to the Reinga. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,115</i>)

>  Reinga: spirit world. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

### rekamaroa
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a variety of kumara. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The group is seeking "the varieties of kumara in Aotearoa be restored to the control of Maori" and full commercial rights to the hutihuti, rekamaroa and taputini varieties ... <i>(Feb 1, 2007,NZ Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The parts of the [milking] machines are named, the cups which attach to the cow's teats, the rubber inflations inside the cups, the pulsators which regulate the vacuum and the releaser. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,149</i>)

>  Releaser: A mechanism for removing milk from under vacuum and discharging it to atmospheric pressure. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,42</i>)

### relief
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) esp. in phrase on relief: doing relief work

>  Jackson could not get regular work and in the end he went on "relief". <i>(Jan 1937,Tomorrow,3 (5): 154</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> You can always pick the new chums on relief. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,207</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> The Depression was knocking at the door ... half the people on Relief, ... <i>(1959,No Remittance,190</i>)

>  Then the Slump came. The builder went broke [153] and for months I was on relief. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,152</i>) [NOTES: 1970 Middleton `The Loners' in 1984 Some Other Country 152-153]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> With the leak days of the Depression only a little behind men who had known relief camps and pannikin bosses had no zest for more mud and for Army sergeants; ... men still on relief had little urge to fight for the country that had given them so little. <i>(1986,Home Front,1/72</i>)

### relief camp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The methods adopted by the Public Works Department in several relief camps is not one that commends itself as an argument for registration. <i>(Mar 6, 1930,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Tom was in a relief camp, somewhere south of Hamilton, digging ditches and felling trees for the Government. <i>(1991,Always the Islands of Memory,95</i>)

>  Unemployed men on relief works marched to the Town Hall at midday to interview the Mayor and to protest against the relief camps. <i>(Apr 13, 1932,Auckland  Star</i>) [NOTES: 1932 Apr 13 Star (Auckland) in 1974 Edwards Riot 1932 14]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Wicked, I call it, taking men away from their families and sending them to those relief camps, leaving [296] the women at home. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,295</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The unemployed were put to work draining swamps, making roads-or golf courses-planting trees, and were paid a miserable sum party raised by special taxation. In many of the rural `relief camps' conditions were extremely primitive; ... <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,250</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> You remember what it was like in the depression? All the single men being shipped off to relief camps? <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,140</i>)

>  <b>RENEE</b> If you go with them you might as well give up and go to the relief camp. <i>(1985,Wednesday to Come,39</i>)

>  `There's a Public Works Relief Camp a bit along the road,' he said. `A few bob a week and tucker, better than nothing.' <i>(Aug 1986,Islands,37</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> With the leak days of the Depression only a little behind men who had known relief camps and pannikin bosses had no zest for more mud and for Army sergeants; ... men still on relief had little urge to fight for the country that had given them so little. <i>(1986,Home Front,1/72</i>)

### relief work

>  <b>NORRIS</b> ... a scheme for creating some work for the unemployed was developed at Te Kauwhata ... The men on relief work cleared the ground of scrub and 810 acres were then ploughed by contract. nf P8: [but relief was general US term for govt work scheme 1930's : also covered non-work aid on relief] <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,210</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  CHRISTCHURCH, June 15. The City Council discussed the unemployed trouble last night, and resolved to start relief work for the most necessitous cases of married men, provided permission is obtained from the union secretary to pay incompetent men less than the minimum wage. <i>(Jun 15, 1909,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  WAIKATO RELIEF WORKS. <i>(Feb 27, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Relief works have occupied the energies of more than one New Zealand writer who, by using brain instead of pick, shovel ... <i>(1934,Journalese,45</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Then he was on a scheme they had going [c.1930], what they called relief work. <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,23</i>)

### relief worker
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a scheme 13 worker

>  <b>BOOTH</b> He had worked with shovel and wheelbarrow building that road he pointed out with such pride and yet with such bitterness, its pattern snaking up the Wainui-o-Mata hill above Wellington Harbour, where he had worked with hundreds of other Relief workers from rough, cold camps spread across New Zealand in those first years of the 1930s. <i>(1997,Deadline : My Story,32</i>)

>  Should investigate conditions of relief workers employed by his department. <i>(Apr 3, 1930,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  Can anyone explain why the same amount of road work done by the relief workers at 10s a day is done by the "Cockie friends" of the Reform Party at 18s per day or more on contract in the summer time? <i>(Oct 14, 1922,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,16</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> But the first of these people with a purpose was a fellow relief worker, and I will call him X. <i>(1975,More Than Enough,42</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> Frank used to pick up groaning loads of scoria from a quarry in Onehunga and lumber them out to the road's end, wherever that was: groups of `relief' workers lived under canvas, a sad army separated from wives and families in pursuit of the only work available to them, moving on as the road moved. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,63</i>)

>  So he often was [a man of leisure], between leaving Auckland Grammar School without matriculation in 1921 and returning to Auckland as a married man in 1933, to go on the roads as a relief worker. <i>(Feb 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### religious Rawleighs people
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Just how the religious Rawleighs people [sc. door-knocking evanglists] face up to enough rejection to flatten a therapist is the big question - along with why they even bother. <i>(Mar 10, 1999,Evening Post,23</i>)

### religo
 
 <i>n.</i> a religious objector to war service (See [cf. hoon](../H#hoon))

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> They reckoned he'd stuck to his religion all through and the Hoons [fn Non religious groups.] gave me the impression that he took his religion seriously and to them he was a bit of a joke. You couldn't take much notice of the Hoons of course, not about a thing like that. Most of them just lumped the religoes together ... <i> Ibid.</i> 164 There was a sort of line drawn in the [detention] camps betwen the Hoons and the religoes. ... The religoes were divided again into God knows how many sects and amongst the hoons were absolute pacifists, pacifists, Communists, Anarchists, humanists, and plain dodgers. <i>Ibid.</i> 215 The third group [in conscientious objectors' detention camps], the religoes, could hardly be called a group at all. It was a mixed bag of Methodists, Presbyterians, Brethren, Church of England and some Catholics. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,154</i>)

>  <b>CRYER</b> People who were conscientious objectors on other than religious grounds were known to the military as hoons (the other were called religoes). <i>(2001,Curious Questions,102</i>)

### relish
 
 <i>n.</i> in New Zealand, a sweetened savoury preserve made of chopped and grated vegetables or occasionally fruit

>  Relish can also be used in a generic sense to mean a spoonful of something tasty served to enhance a plain food, although in New Zealand a relish is usually a sweetened savoury preserve made of chopped or grated vegetables - occasionally fruit - in which the vegetables retain some firmness of texture. Therefore, you could say that a New Zealand relish is a type of pickle, or that a New Zealand pickle is a type of relish. <i>(May 15, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### rellie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a relative [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  Basingstoke in Hampshire is the buckle on England's computer belt, and was our base for the excellent reason that there we could sponge off rellies. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 50/4</i>)

>  Some of our best friends and rellies studied the law and they seem to be perfectly nice people. <i>(Jun 12, 1992,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Last Saturday Manuel deftly polished balding heads and passionately embraced diners he had mistaken for rellies from Barcelona. <i>(Mar 3, 1992,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  When the rellies left, he went to a particularly grotty tattoo parlour in an old whaling port and said "Pierce the damn ear." <i>(Oct 30, 1999,Dominion,18</i>)

>  "If we go away for a holiday we don't stay in flash pubs, we stay with rellies. <i>(Jul 1991,Metro,51</i>)

>  For Jake and Beth Heke there's nothing that can't be solved by an all-night drunken party, their "reles" and friends crowded round the beer crates, singing sentimental songs into the wee small hours. <i>(May 14, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Artistic Connie.. has come on her own to the family hut in the Wairarapa to avoid the rels and her 50th birthday. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  Where else are they so big on hypocorisms? ... They're those dinky little words we make up, like these: kiddies (children), cardies (cardigans), boatie (person who sails boats), yachtie (person who sails yachts), drinkies (drinks), bikies (people who get together to ride motorbikes), wrinklies (old people), rellies (relations), wellies (Wellingtons, as in gumboots), wallies (variation on wankers), littlies (children). <i>(Sep 1988,North & South,41</i>)

>  Those strangely infantile condensations - `prezzies' and `rellies' (for presents and relatives) were bandied about on the radio quite a bit recently. It looks as if they are going to join veges, ciggy, brekky, cardy - and all the rest as OK idiom. But there is one antipodean expression that charms people internationally ... That is the word `naughty', for sexual intercourse. <i>(Jan 10, 1989,Evening Post,6</i>)
