### remit
 
 <i>n.</i> a recommendation sent by a branch or by an affiliated organisation of a body to the main body (often an annual conference) for consideration, and possible adoption and action. [(1921) ?d. B. Sutton-Smith A History of Children's Play [TS] 277 [footnote] quoting 1921 Feb Journal of Education -->18th Meeting NZEI Remit 12 (Southland) -->ca1930's (mid) R.Mason]

>  A number of remits referring to classification and salaries were withdrawn. <i>(Jan 6, 1902,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  A further recommendation to the Directors was then carried "That they forward a remit to the next Conference of the South Island Dairy Association, pointing out the advisability of bringing down a standard set of articles for use in the South Island." <i>(Sep 13, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Mr. Johnson moved the Taranaki remit, That the desirableness be urged upon the Minister of having a uniform set of text books in use in the schools throughout the colony. <i>(Feb 1, 1902,NZ Journal of Education,3/247</i>)

>  A `remit' proposed by Mr Johnson was adopted. <i>(Oct 1, 1902,NZ Journal of Education,4/177</i>)

>  To Shear and Expert `That Remit No. 7 be struck out, i.e. - ... `That no shearer be allowed to shear and expert on the same board.' ... `That no shearer expert and shear on a board of more than five shearers.' <i>Ibid.</i>. 15 Mount Watkins Remit No. 3: `That an allowance in the case of daggy sheep be arranged for,' was dealt with; ... it was decided: `That all members refuse to dagg [sic] sheep during shearing except the dagging rate be added to the shearing rate, as we are of the opinion that dagging is not shearing. <i>(1912,NZ Shearers (Report Third Annual Conference),11</i>)

>  Some N.Z. Remits. [heading] The following are some of the remits proposed for discussion at the Empire Conference by the N.Z.R.S.A. <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,40</i>)

>  REMIT PROPOSED / LABOUR DAY CHANGE ... the Wellington Labour Day Committee has again discussed the desirability of having this statutory holiday changed to a more seasonable time of the year. <i>(Nov 10, 1947,Evening Post,8/8</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The noun remit ... means a directive from a branch to a central organization for consideration. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,174</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> remit: Resolution. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  remit ... 7. proposal from branch put up for central decision <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,412</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; NZPOD 644]]

### remittance
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACKIE</b> It is frequented by young men of some education with a little money, and whilst their money lasts they give themselves up to riot and excess. ... they remain in obscurity until their remittance brings them again to the Wakatu. <i>(1852,Journal</i>) [NOTES: c1852-53 Frederick Mackie Journal in 1973 Traveller Under Concern p? in 1979 Noland Bad Old Days 43]

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> The other two ... had just finished their `cadetship,' that is, they had been learning sheep-farming under a settler, and were waiting for a remittance from their friends in England to begin on their own account. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,20</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> This inn - the Wakatu - is the most disorderly and uncomfortable house that we have seen in the colonies. It is frequented by young men of some education with a little money, and whilst their money lasts they give themselves up to riot and excess. They are probably such as their friends in England are glad to send to a distance and as if to confirm their ruin supply them with a small income. As soon as this is squandered they go to the Wairau as shepherds ... Here they remain in obscurity till their remittance brings them again to the Wakatu. <i>(Apr 26, 1853,Traveller under Concern,102,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> A few days later he came, and told me his story, common enough in Australia, but at present much less so in New Zealand, where we have fewer of those failures at home who are sent out to the Colonies, in the faint hope that a man who is a ne'er-do-well in England will succeed elsewhere, and in too many cases sent out with the promise of small remittances, to be out of sight and out of mind. <i>(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,61,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> They [swell loafers] swear they'll tip without fail,/ And gammon with wondrous anxiety,/ They expect a remittance next mail,-/ It's an ancient dodge... ./ <i>(1862,Auckland Vocalist,2</i>)

>  Rumor gave out that a small annual remittance from the old country kept the Squire's head above-water <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,25</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `Oh! bother my uncle, though he did send me a small remittance the other day,' was Arthur's rejoinder; ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,145</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> ... in a low but displeased voice he led the way outside. `What the devil brought you here? Did I not tell you to keep to yourself and not cross my way, except when you come to my office for your remittance. ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,126</i>)

>  Take the `jackaroos' on a station; ... They get a remittance which keeps them in clothes, l[e]ggings and riding breeches, and an occasional trip to town. ... They are practically banished from the old country to save the family from being disgraced, and this place is being made a dumping ground for these wastrels. <i>(Jul 2, 1910,NZ Truth,262/6/7</i>)

>  ~ n.-remitt'ance man n. a person living on periodical remittances from his family. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> No good record attached to this young sprig of the nobility who (said rumour) had been cashiered from the Hussars and squeezed by anxious relatives into an infantry regiment sailing for the colonies, the relatives promising that he would receive a handsome remittance so long as he stayed there. <i>(1938,Promenade,135</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 1982,Metro</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Remmers Remuera, in the trendy nickname Remuera-ites have given what they regard as Auckland's - and New Zealand's - most exclusive suburb. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,92</i>)

### remittance day
 
 <i>n.</i> the day a REMITTANCE MAN'S allowance is paid

>  When remittance day comes round furtive townspeople hang around the post-office to waylay if possible the Englishman, and if waylaid he is the soul of honour, and squares up all his debts. <i>(Oct 14, 1905,New Zealand Free Lance,12</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Fill it up [with whisky] it's remittance day. <i>(1944,Shining with the Shiner,76</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> Each remittance day they used to get very drunk indeed, often got the D.T.s and had to be taken into the Auckland Hospital for treatment; . <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,185</i>)

### remittance man
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc] a station cadet from England.

>  station cadet from England: remittance man. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a victim of the British class system paid to live and work in New Zealand to avoid bringing shame to his family in the home country [usually employed in rural areas as shepherd, boundary keeper, musterer etc.]

>  Socially, the better type of remittance man - for there are grades even in remittance men - is a great favourite. <i>(Aug 14, 1912,Otago Witness</i>)

>  We learn from the Wellington Post that one of the first uses made of the Australian cable was by a remittance man. He telegraphed to his friends in England "Lend me GBP20." The answer received was "Paid for your telegram and this - balance per mail." He is now waiting for the mail that will bring him about enough to acknowledge the receipt by post. <i>(Mar 4, 1876,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Some slackness had been apparent, especially amongst the "Remittance" or Imported Men. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,68</i>)

>  It was explained by the police that Jones is a "remittance man." <i>(Oct 20, 1938,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  He gave evasive answers to the police and others who inquired the whereabouts of a remittance man, who subsequently was found drunk in the hotel. <i>(May 21, 1901,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  There were the Remittance Men. Rather colourful characters, the so-called 'Black Sheep' of some well to do family in far away England. The 'R'Men received a regular allowance from home, accompanied by a polite note suggesting that it would be better for all concerned if they would remain out in the colonies. <i>(Jun 2002,NZ Memories,No. 36/16</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> The Remittance Man. The chap whose people used the colonies as a wastepaper basket. <i>(1966,Black Beech & Honeydew,203</i>)

>  These young men were soon known as "remittance men" and in some cases remained so until the end of their lives. <i>(Feb 8, 1956,Weekly News,44</i>)

>  See that house? A remittance man from Ireland lived there. Got his sister in the family way. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,72/25</i>)

>  There were the remittance men, rather colourful characters, the so called "black sheep" of some well to do family in faraway England. The "R" men received a regular allowance from home. <i>(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> A Remittance Man meant of good family, maybe a lord's family even, but they didn't want him at home where they could see him, so the black sheep was shipped off to New Zealand, well out of sight, and money sent out regularly to support him. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,82</i>)

>  How often the remittance man would get to the letter-box, only to get the reply, "No letters." <i>(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  He was a drinker, as were, I believe, a number of other remittance men. <i>(Aug 1994,Quote Unquote,16</i>)

>  Remittance Man 'Black sheep' of often upper class family sent to the colonies and paid a remittance. <i>(1989,Chosen Place,104</i>)

>  Just on the edge of The Settlement there lived a person called Sam, who it was said was a remittance man and from a well to do family in England. <i>(Jan 2004,Onslow Historian,No. 34(1/2) : 6</i>)

>  ....There was the litle chap who wore shorts,.. 'Jack', the remittance man from England who lived at Waiheke and came up on the boat to hob-nob with the trammies waiting to change shifts down town... <i>(Aug 2003,NZ Memories,No. 43/45</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> He ultimately settled down into the position of a 'remittance man' - living, that is, upon a pittance sent out from home and doled out in weekly instalments by a solicitor. <i>(1888,New Zealand Of Today,171</i>)

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> Speegles was what was known as a remittance man. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,83</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Remittance men were so out of place - like a giraffe amongst the manuka. <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,27</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> One of Southland's earliest landowners was a remittance man. <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,79</i>)

### remittancer
 
 <i>n.</i> a remittance man

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> [Colonialisms] A `remittancer' in Australia is a ne'er-do-well who has been sent out from England by his friends and receives a monthly remittance as long as he [170] keeps away. No one will give a `remittancer' work if he can obtain anybody else to do it, and the remittancer generally spends his monthly allowance in three days, living very badly on credit till his money next arrives.) <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,169</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Another memory that lingered on the trip, was that from Napier to Gisborne, to inspect a property known as `Te Rapa' and nearby was a `pubbery' (certainly not a hotel, for anybody would wonder why the Licensed Victuallers Association ever granted a licence for meals or liquor to be dispensed), and was typically designated `The Trap'. Truly, this pubbery was just short of shanty, typical of the `Outer Barcoo', but where Maories, their Wahines, or broken down `Remittancers' could drown their sorrows, wander into the `arms of Bacchus' or chase pink elephants. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,157</i>)

### Remmers
 
 <i>n.</i> the Auckland high-rent suburb of Remuera -

>  "We don't go: 'Yo, I grew up in Remmers, I had it so bad!' she laughs. <i>(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,8</i>)

>  Come on Remmers, Fendalton, Khandallah, pull your socks up! <i>(Sep 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  He may be slick at baring tits and bums in his trashy <i>Daily Phews</i> but commercially he lacks that touch of class that distinguishes the real Remmers socialist from the brash Sydneysider or Melbournite. <i>(Jun 1999,Metro,25</i>)

>  New National candidate Richard Worth is an elegant, traditional Remmers Tory figure, a partner in a major law firm, a Northern Club type. <i>(Nov 20, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Simply everyone's yapping about D'Argento's - an ultra ritzy little boutique in Remmers. <i>(1982,Metro,13</i>)

>  No I could not book a table for 7pm at scorching hot new Remmers bistro Banque, I was told firmly. <i>(Nov 2005,North & South,134</i>)

>  Any alliance that combines unwashed lentil munchers with Remmers blond bobs deserves to be stopped. <i>(Feb 16, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  ...she insists she is no "Remmers girl", pointing to one of Ms Hasler's blue-and-white billboards. "Look at that billboard. So who looks like a Remuera girl?" she snipes. <i>(Jul 20, 2002,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  The Parn-hell post office.. has been sold to.. a man.. who.. has operated out of humble and unassuming Remmers. <i>(Apr 1993,Metro,24</i>)

 <i>n.</i> suckerfish

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SUCKERFISH or REMORA [<i>Remora brachypterus</i>] ... Shark-sucker is an alternative name. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,52</i>)

For many, they are now the family vehicle of choice, replacing the sedan and the stationwagon. <i>(Jul 1998,AA Directions,60</i>)

### rengarenga
 
 <i>n.</i> also called renga lily, rock lily a drooping-leaved lily with branching flower-stems, found in open rocky situations from Cook Strait northwards [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The coastal lily rengarenga is replacing agapanthus and nikau palms and cabbage trees are much in vogue. <i>(Oct 14, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Seek ye the rengarenga that holds the drifting spray, / And bring your offspring forth likewise in numberless array. <i>(Oct 21, 1897,Otago Witness,46</i>)

>  Rengarenga roots were eaten by Maoris after being either roasted or cooked in the steam oven, and said to taste like potato. <i>(Dec 3, 2005,Dominion Post Indulgence,23</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> R|enga r|enga; A plant so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,202</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> ... or the root of the Rengarenga (<i>Arthropodium cirratum</i>), scraped and applied ... to bring forwards any abscesses or tumours, where matter is forming. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),89</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i>, Br. ... Nat. name, `Renga Renga', in Northern Districts, and `Maikai Ka', in Southern, Colenso. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 254</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rengarenga, <i>Col[enso]. Arthropodium cirrhatum </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  The blanched bases of the leaves of the harakeke (<i>Phormium</i>), and the roots of the rengrenga or maikaika (<i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i>), were sometimes roasted and beaten to a pulp and applied warm to ... abcesses. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/1-58</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Colenso TrNZI 1/1-58 (On the Botany of the North Island) in 1981 Brooker et al. NZ Medicinal Plants 15]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> .. the roots of the New Zealand lily (<i>rengarenga</i>: Arthropodium cirrhatum) had to be cooked in the oven before becoming fit for food. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,99</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>A[rthropodium] cirrhatum, R.Br. ... Rengarenga. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,719</i>)

>  On the rocky knoll near the middle of the [Karewa] islaet the rengrenga, or rock-lily (<i>Arthropodium cirratum</i>), grows in profusion ... <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/187</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden)]

>  <i>rengarenga</i>, a lily (Arthropodium cirrhatum), likes open rocky situations. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,66/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Rengarenga (New Zealand lily) .. .. rengarenga .. .. <i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i>(Forst.f.) R.Br. Common name: <i>Rock lily</i> Maori names: <i>Rengarenga, maikaika, maika ka</i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,63</i>)

>  The [cliff-hanging] plants chosen were the poor knights lily (Xeronema callistemon) and ringaringa, also called the New Zealand rock lily (Arthropodium cirrhatum). <i>(May 10, 1973,Dominion,5/1</i>)

### rengarenga lily
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Arthropodium cirratum</i>

>  The rengarenga lily edges part of his long sweeping drive, and he says the plant is a "landscaper's dream. <i>(Dec 21, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

An alternative Maori name for the plant, maikika, is shared with two native orchids that have similar, starchy, edible root-like tubers. <i>(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,44</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [(librarians')]

### reo, te reo
 
 <i>n.</i> (the Maori) language [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The growth of kohanga reo and kura kaupapa has created families where the youngest children are comfortable speaking te reo, but their older siblings, born too early, are less so, and their parents can hardly speak their own language. <i>(Jul 24, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66</i>)

>  It is hard to imagine anyone outside the tiny Te Reo Maori clique wasting time watching Tuku Morgan's million-dollar series on Pacific migration. <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  [Hdg] $11 million for te reo teachers. The Government yesterday announced it would spend $11 million over three years to help teachers in te reo Maori (Maori language). <i>(Nov 25, 1997,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  IN THE PRESS AND WILL SHORTLY BE PUBLISHED, TE HIKOI TUATAHI KI TE REO MAORI: or, First Step towards the Maori language; with Conversational Phrases, and a short Vocabulary of easy words; arranged as an Anglo-Maori School Book by R.D. <i>(Mar 30, 1861,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  Says Kiwitea to Makino, / I'll run into you clean O. / Says Makino to Kiwitea, / (i te reo Maori) - Pea. <i>(May 10, 1895,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  E KORERO KOE I TE REO MAORI? (Do You Speak Maori?) <i>(Jun 13, 1927,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  "This will have people talking," noted Wells happily, "possibly in te reo." <i>(Dec 18, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  I really want to be fluent in te reo. I love the language .. The Maori language is exquisite. <i>(Nov 27, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  We would like to see it reach everyone, so there is a holistic, committed approach to making te reo Maori accessible to all New Zealanders. <i>(Jul 4, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  We need at least 100,000 speakers of Te Reo if we want the language to survive. <i>(Jun 28, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  So that puts to rest the theory that here was another young sportsman (like Adam Parore) with a Maori name and a tendency to mispronounce the reo. <i>(1995,Mana,10 : 9</i>)

>  I had lost my reo big time ... <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,15,Autumn ed.</i>)

>  The film stars Maori actors speaking te reo, subtitled in modern English. <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  And given the tedious showing-off that occurs every time Maori is spoken in Parliament .. the whole house could do with a course in both te reo and common good manners. <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Reo - while the word is often translated as 'the language', this explanation hides the fact that the Maori language is revered as a living record of its people, and enshrines the ethos of the people. No one can fully know `te reo' unless one is acquainted with the proverbs, legends, histories and knowledge of all tribal groups and areas. Yet it is a lifetime work knowing 'te reo' of one's own tribal area. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,22</i>)

>  And to top it all off, the Royal New Zealand Navy has allowed Timothy to regain his reo rangatira. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,15,Autumn ed.</i>)

>  [Miria Simpson's] contribution was such that she was heralded simply as "Te Reo" by the hundreds who gathered to farewell her at Victoria University's Te Reo Waka Marae on Tuesday and yesterday. <i>(Jun 13, 2002,Evening Post,9</i>)

### rep
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of a representative sports team [ORIGIN: abbreviation, cf P: politician, athlete, cricketer representing a State: Austr ca 1920; (?) cf WF n. (3) since c 1900]

>  Nothing daunted, this ex-Southland and ex-Wellington Rep. (who, by the way, appeared in a Wellington jersey and long pants) secured from the next line-out to break through and dribble 25 yards in an astonishingly expert manner. <i>(Jul 19, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  A paragraph in last night's Herald, <i>re</i> the football match on Saturday "Reps" v. "All-comers," concludes thus - "It is to be hoped that every endeavour will be made toward the inclusion of the Turakina contingent." <i>(Aug 25, 1891,Wanganui Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> Everybody knew him as a rep. in the Otago [rugby] team, ... <i>(1943,Pure Gold,36</i>)

>  rep 3 ... 2. N.Z. inf. a rugby player selected to represent his district. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,974</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact]



 
 <i>adj.</i> representative (often collocated with match, player, etc.) [some noun uses in citations below (see also rep, reps noun)]

>  Western District football enthusiasts should feel proud of their two representatives in the Southland Rep. team. <i>(Aug 23, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  RUBGY REP MATCH. <i>(Sep 13, 1920,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  THE "REP" TEAM. <i>(Aug 25, 1891,Wanganui Chronicle,3</i>)

>  ... junior rep. match. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/2/8</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> Colin Gilroy, rep. footballer and Rhodes scholar ... <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,39</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> Didn't you play wing-threequarters in such and such a `rep' match. <i>(1916,Wowser, The,270</i>)

>  <b>INGRAM</b> ... the afternoon was spent in watching the 1st. and 2nd. Batalion football reps. in action against each other. <i>(Sep 16, 1917,Anzac Diary,44,1987 ed.</i>)

>  `Rep.' Rugby Matches [heading] A representative Rugby match between Auckland and Canterbury ... <i>(Sep 2, 1929,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  Peter, survey chainman, matriculated, rep-footballer: ... <i>(Jun 1953,Here & Now,29</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Family came from up river early in the summer - plays rep. tennis and hockey and handles a horse better than most men. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,140,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> [see Maul] <i>Ibid.</i> 174 The rep games when the log of wood has gone. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,50</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> `The rep selector is here today. ...' And that's what the reps need too, a few good country forwards who know how to `get stuck in.' <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,13</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Rep.- Sometimes shed r. Under the arbitration award it is laid down that `the shearers should elect a representative' who deals with the employer whenever any dispute or question arises. If the r. is a sensible man of good will, shearing goes through with hardly a hitch. (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> REP: In a shearing gang a man is elected as a representative in the event of any disputes. He is known as the `rep.' - <i>(1949,High Country Days,197,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> REP. Shearers' voted representative who speaks for all the shearers in the gang in regard to complaints, decisions, etc. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Rep. In the terms of the 1894 Industrial Conciliation and Arbitration Act, shearers were to elect a representative to negotiate any disputes or grievances directly with their employer. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

### repatriation farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a training-farm where injured or weakened ex-soldiers learned the practice of light farming and horticulture.

### reperepe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Egooree, <i>A dog</i>. Teyka, <i>Fish.</i> Hewhai[a-grave], <i>A skate.</i> Eraperape, <i>A fish called Chimaera.</i> Hepaooa, <i>Ear-shells</i>. Hekohooa[a-grave], <i>Small ear-shells.</i> [...] <i>(1770,Journal,126</i>)

>  CALLORHYNCHUS MILII (Bory). <i>Elephant-fish; Reperepe</i>. In Christchurch large numbers are sold under the name of `silver trumpeter.' ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (rarer in the north). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Callorhynchus milii</i> Bory. ELEPHANT-FISH; REPEREPE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,11,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Callorhynchus milii</i> Bory. Elephant-fish. <i>Reprepe. </i> <i>(1938,TrNZI,68/401</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> ELEPHANT-FISH (REPEREPE) <i>Callorhynchus milii</i> Bory de St. Vincent <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,84,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ELEPHANTFISH ... <i>Other names: Callorhynchus millii</i>; reperepe (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,194</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> ELEPHANTFISH [<i>Callorhynchus milii</i> ] ... Other names are elephant and reperepe; the latter is Maori and seldom heard today. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ELEPHANT FISH Only one species of this oddity appears in New Zealand waters, <i>Callorhynchus milii</i> , which also bears the Maori name `reperepe.' <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,63</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ELEPHANT FISH Only one species of this oddity appears in New Zealand waters, <i>Callorhynchus milii</i> , which also bears the Maori name `reperepe.' <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,63</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> This bizarre, silvery fish, known to the Maori as <i>rerepe,</i> [sic] a trunk-like snout and occurs mainly along the east coast of the South Island. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,73</i>)

### repertory
 
 <i>n.</i> In England a repertory theatre is usually one with a professional cast who perform a repertoire of plays, changing plays, say, once a week. In NZ it is merely the name of an amateur theatre group, undistinguished from other such groups except (in Wellington at least) in the more popular appeal of the plays performed. (J. Wright). 1963 (Also, as General English, the Rep).

### repile
 
 <i>v.</i> [Jim writes: DNZE records the special NZ use of <i>pile</i> and <i>block</i> [or post] of wood or stone [or concrete] on which the underfloor beams of a building rest. It does not have entries for <i>repile</i> or <i>reblock</i>. These terms are in common use and often seen in ads for old houses e.g. "recently repiled", "needs some reblocking". "Repiling" is a heading in the Yellow Pages.] (See [reblock](../R#reblock))

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal that augments a herd or flock after culling; usually home-bred.

>  He carries 4000 Corriedale ewes and 1800 hogget replacements. <i>(Oct 22, 1953,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  For sale by public tender, finishing land - Motukarara, Strong summer finishing country for lambs and steers/replacements. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Press, The,59</i>)

>  The property is situated 25 km from Whakatane, finishing 1200 bulls, 500 other cattle and carrying 3000 ewes and replacements. <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Dominion,50</i>)

### reremai
 
 <i>n.</i> also called basking shark a large (up to 13 m) plankton-feeding shark, found in New Zealand waters.

>  <b>KEENE</b> reremai: basking shark, New Zealand's largest shark, has blunt teeth about 1/4 in. in length; harmless; eats crustaceans. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CETORHINIDAE contains the enormous basking shark or reremai (<i>Cetorhinus</i>) ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,198</i>)

### rerepari
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Papaka, the crab: the largest is about two and a half inches across the carapace; a small crab, called the rerepari, abounds in the salt marshes. There is also a small crab found in the mussel, which has a very round red body and short legs. I have met with a little fresh-water crab, seventy miles inland; it is not above half an inch across the shell, delicately formed, of a dusky green color. There is no lobster in New Zealand. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,415</i>)

>  The base of the section comprises ridges of pumice sand gravel mantled by Okareka Ash, Rerewhakaaitu Ash and Rotorua Ash now truncated by lake waters when the lake level was at an elevation of 284 m as against the present day elevation of 279.7 m. <i>(May 1975,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,39/25</i>)

### reserve
 
 <i>n.</i> a piece of bush or land `reserved' by statute for scenic, recreational or conservation purposes [also Maori reserve ca1935 Going up to the reserve today for a picnic.] (See [also Maori reserve](../A#also-maori-reserve))

>  Allow me through the medium of your journal to call the attention of the Board of Education, or whoever has the management of Educational Reserves, to the injustice they are doing this would-be township, by not leasing the reserve. <i>(Jan 5, 1878,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  The members of the Christchurch City Council made their annual inspection of the cemetery and other city reserves yesterday. <i>(Mar 9, 1900,Star, The,3</i>)

>  These grounds and premises (presently occupied by Mr. A. Millar, who is about to leave the settlement for a time) being situated on the reserve outside the town, any occupier is not likely to be disturbed in the possession for some time. <i>(May 8, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,1</i>)

>  There are six reserves containing 60 acres that have been set apart as sites for country schools, two of them have been confirmed, the others will be forwarded for the recommendation of the Council. <i>(Oct 21, 1868,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  The directors wish that, in forming the plan of the town, you should make ample reserves for all public purposes; such as a cemetery, a market-place, wharfage, and probable public buildings, a botanical garden, a park, and extensive boulevards. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... a town-site [for Wellington] was laid out, consisting of 1100 sections of one acre each, besides reserves for public purposes, and there were also laid out 1100 rural sections of one hundred acres each ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,94</i>)

>  ... Kite and Jim went mustering the reserve for stragglers. <i>(Mar 1, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Mar 1 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 128]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> An instruction to Mr. Kettle when commencing his surveys was to lay off certain portions of land for public use, not open for sale, and termed `reserves.' <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,100</i>)

>  <b>KOEBELL</b> A patch or two of `reserve' bush <i>(1911,In Maoriland Bush,97</i>)

>  <b>81</b> How can a man fancy that a job as sawdust boy [in a sawmill up Taihape way] for a week or two can help him locate cattle in Mount Egmont Reserve beats me, ... <i>(1927,Me and Gus,(1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm) ed.</i>)

>  The tip of Point Chevalier was a park known as `the Reserve', ringed with pine trees and macrocarpa which led to the Piper Rocks, where boys and men ... caught sprats and piper, which were excellent eating. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  RESERVE. An area of land legally set aside for some given purpose. e.g. Education Reserve, Roas Reserve, Scenic Reserve, and earlier days, Forest Reserve. - <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### Reserve Bank (of New Zealand)
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There shall be established in New Zealand in accordance with this Act a bank to be called the Reserve Bank of New Zealand.<i>Ibid.</i>56 There shall be established in New Zealand in accordance with this Act a bank to be called the Reserve Bank of New Zealand. <i>(1933,NZ Statutes,24 Geo. V 11/53</i>)

>  An act to amend the Reserve Bank of NZ Act, 1933. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/7 It shall be the general function of the Reserve Bank.. to give effect as far as may be to the monetary policy of the Government, as communicated to it from time to time by the Minister of Finance... The bank shall regulate and control credit and currency in New Zealand, the transfer of moneys to or from New Zealand, and the disposal of moneys that were derived from the sale of any New Zealand products and for the time being are held overseas. <i>(1936,NZ Statutes,1/4</i>)

>  The great temptation to any Government is.. to use Reserve Bank credit. <i>Ibid.</i> 1843 If money is in credit, we buy back securities from the Reserve Bank. <i>(1952,NZ Parliamentary Debates,298/1223</i>)

>  Sir Otto Niemeyer, however, in such a report, can only give us the technical framework of a Central Reserve Bank, leaving us to clothe it according to our own particular requirements, provided we do not violate the accepted principles which should govern such an institution. <i>(Sep 29, 1931,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In the same year [1933] the Government set up the Reserve Bank of New Zealand so that it could secure cheaper credit for the state(...) and, more important, so that it could determine general monetary policy. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,254</i>)

### reserved
 
 <i>adj.</i> of land, retained for public use

>  Her Majesty has, in the most decided terms, withheld from the Governor of New Zealand the power to grant any land in that Colony gratuitously, to any person whatever. This prohibition does not, of course, apply to the case of grants of land which it may occasionally be convenient to make to persons in trust for the public of tracts or pieces of land which it may be found convenient to dedicate to purposes of general utility; as, for example, roads, fortifications, public pleasure grounds, cemeteries, and the like. It is proposed that, with the exception of lands reserved or granted in trust for the purposes before-mentioned, all the lands of the Colony should be sold progressively, as surveys shall be completed ... <i>(Apr 19, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  And all persons are warned not to clear, fence, or cultivate, or build in or upon any portion of the belt of reserved land surrounding the town. <i>(Sep 11, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

### reserved bush
 
 <i>n.</i> [(cf. bush reserve)]

>  The reserved bush is within two minutes' walk of the house, ... <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### Residency
 
 <i>n.</i> district officers of M.O.W., viewed collectively

>  <b>KEN SMITHYMAN</b> Have you noticed the M.O.W's resurrection of an old term? - their district officers are now designated as their Residency, reflecting the Indian origin (particulary Madras) of our survey system? <i>(1975,Personal Communication</i>)

### Resident
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs) Bay of Islands]

>  There are 40 Europeans and their families residing at the Bay, independent of the Mission, and of the British Resident and his family. <i>(Aug 9, 1834,NZ Independent,30,1979 ed.</i>)

### Resident Magistrate
 
 <i>n.</i> an official stationed in various towns and settlements, having minor judicial and (particularly in frontier areas) administrative functions, especially in relation to Maori matters. [Jim Cameron writes : Historical 1846-1893. Shorn of administrative duties, resident magistrates were metamorphosed into <i>stipendiary magistrates</i> in the 1890s and legal qualifications were required. The term is not unique to NZ; there were resident magistrates in Ireland and elsewhere, but never in England. They played a significant part in NZ History which is recounted in Aland Ward <i>A Show of Justice</i> (1974). Not recorded in DNZE or other dictionaries, except perfunctorily (without definition) in NSOD.]

>  Resident magistrates, especially in frontier districts, were agents of government as well as having judicial functions. Towards the end of the nineteenth century resident magistrates lost their administrative functions and their office was metamorphosed into the purely judicial one of stipendiary magistrate. Generally known simply as magistrates (or SMs) they were legally qualified and exercised increasingly important judicial functions in magistrates courts <i>(1999,NZWords,2(1) : 1</i>)

### resin
 
 <i>n.</i> kauri resin

>  THE KOWRI. .. This tree yields a large quantity of resin of a white and amber colour, very transparent, burning with a bright flame, and having a very agreeable smell. <i>(Oct 6, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  The Imperial Institute's Technical Department speaks favourably of Queensland resin. It hitherto was believed to be kauri resin, but probably it is derived from a species of callistris. <i>(Jul 7, 1904,Southland Times,3</i>)

### resleeperer
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Railways coinage) R. Mason noted a Railways Dept advertisement in the 1940's for resleeperers to renew railway sleepers.]

### resource consent
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes : This is not recorded in DNZE but is in NZPOD. There are no entries in that dictionary for the different types of resource consent - <i>land use consent, subdivision consent, coastal permit, water permit, discharge permit</i>. See Resource Management Act, 1991, s. 87 for definitions.]

>  Timberlands is seeking resource consents.. to log 64,000 cu m initially and eventually up to 100,000 cu m. <i>(Sep 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  If you want bigger, deeper dams you need a Resource Consent, and the dam must be constructed to specific standards. <i>(Feb 2001,Growing Today,6</i>)

>  Some obsolete terms (e.g. entail, foreclosure, probation) are not noted as historical only.<i>Ibid</i> 2 Even the <i>Dictionary of New Zealand English</i> (DNZE) (1997) is patchy in its coverage of legal terms, having for example no entry for disputes tribunal, family protection, incorporated society, Maori option, protection order, resource consent. Resource consents, defined in NZPOD, are of several types - land use consents, subdivision consents, coastal permits, water permits, and discharge permits. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

>  Resource consents, defined in NZPOD, are of several types - land use consents, subdivision consents, coastal permits, water permits, and discharge permits. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### Resource Management  Act
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If the Timberlands' hearing had been allowed to go through the Resource Mangement Act process, the application would probably have been granted, albiet with stringent conditions. <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,82</i>)

>  The Resource Management Bill - Rembo for short - is basically the old Town and Country Planning Act on steroids. <i>(Sep 17, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### Resource Management Act
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a controversial natural resource protection act of 1991, that gives instruction to landowners on how weeds, pests, water, mineral, topographical, and other resources on their properties are to be managed. [aka RMA]

>  <b>ACKROYD</b> That review [of the Land Act 1948] culminated in the Resource Management Act 1991 that has the single purpose of promoting the sustainable management of natural and physical resources .. Land management policies for the high country are now inextricably linked to the highly politicised policy and planning process underpinning the Resource Management Act 1991. <i>(1993,Politics, Economics & Pastoral Land,38</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW & WILLIAMS</b> The majority of those [farmers] interviewed do not see the Resource Management Act as a regulation requiring sustainable land management practices. <i>(1998,Information and Communication Needs,20</i>)

>  The RMA provides the opportunity for people who do not own land to tell land owners how they should manage their properties. <i>(Sep 24, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,Farming Today Supplt 4</i>)

>  They [farmers] are head butting Resource Management Act issues all the time. [RMA] <i>(May 2002,Growing Today,17</i>)

>  Protection of New Zealand's historic heritage has taken a major step forward with the passing of amendments to the Resource Management Act. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Heritage,No. 90/9</i>)

### responsible
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We have been asking a long time to have a branch railway brought to this town, and a big body of responsibles paid a call on Sir Joseph Ward; but he promised them nothing satisfactory. <i>(Oct 30, 1909,NZ Truth,227/4/8</i>)

### rest area
 
 <i>n.</i> a designated area off or alongside a road where vehicles may stop, sometimes with tables, seats, etc. [Jim writes: Approx BritE equivalent <i>layby</i>. In NZPOD but not NZD or DNZE. Not in NSOD. Collins tags it as <i>Austral and NZ</i>. Noted in Orsman database to check.]

### restaurant
 
 <i>n.</i> Up to the late 1950's at least or early 1960's more commonly applied in New Zealand hyperbolistically to any cheap eating-house serving fries, grills etc: HASH-HOUSE, HASH FOUNDRY qqv. (cf HOTEL). In the 1950's more expensive a la carte restaurants became commoner, a few to be finally licensed in 1962.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Quoting Dougla's recollections] The shanty keeper is a cross between the store and the pub [on the early West Coast]. ... He depends upon whiskey and card playing, and sometimes calls his place an Accommodation House or restaurant. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,14</i>) [NOTES: [1860's?] 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 14]

>  [The Minister of Agfriculture] did not think any member would wish to see restaurants commonly referred to as `hash-houses' with a [liquor] licence. <i>(Oct 31, 1962,Dominion,14</i>)

### restaurant glass
 
 <i>n.</i> a standard wine glass

### restorative justice
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Restorative justice urged... A senior district and youth court judge has called for the youth court principles of restorative justice - in which victims and offenders meet to "make matters right" - should be applied to all levels of the criminal justice process. <i>(Sep 15, 1999,Dominion,15</i>)

>  Restorative justice is by no means a new concept but it is gaining a foothold in New Zealand and overseas. <i>(Mar 6, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  Most people would agree that restorative justice is a wonderful idea, but do they understand the reality of the schemes presently in place or being trialled? <i>(Jul 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  A successful commumnity-based restorative justice programme in Wanganui has managed to extract something positive from this otherwise tragic event... The Wanganui Restorative Justice Trust was set up three years ago by a group of people keen to develop a restorative justice process in the city... A contract is worked out between the offender and the victim, which is then presented back to the court. <i>(Jun 2002,Wel-com (Wellington),No. 186 : 13</i>)

### restraining order
 
 <i>n.</i> a court order aimed at preventing the harassment of persons who are not in a domestic relationship with the harasser. (The NZOD definition is confusing and the example incorrect).]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a reinfestation of sheep by flystrike.

### resultancy
 
 <i>n.</i>

### retail
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of "retail therapy"

>  "I'm off to do a bit of retail." "Retail's always good." <i>(Oct 16, 2001,TV2</i>) [NOTES: <i>Shortland Street</i>]

### reti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANNERING</b> The Maoris used to fish there [mouth of Tukituki River, Hawke's Bay ca1897] with `reti' ... The reti consists of a canoe-shaped board about a foot long with a spinning bait attached with about four feet of strong line. The board is thrown into the current on its edge and is held against the current and allowed to drift out into the breakers, generally on about 100 yards of line. The moment a fish is struck the whole affair is brought in. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,156</i>)

### retire
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [land] to allow to revert to its natural state.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I had gone out alone on skis to look for sheep on the Burnt Face, our old wether block soon to be retired from grazing by the Forest Service. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,50</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Retiring back valleys and land above the snowline (900m or 3,000 ft) is part of a soil conservation scheme to protect mountain lands. On Makarora 12,525 ha (31,000 acres) have been retired. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,179</i>)

>  <b>BARNES</b> I was determined that we had to do something, so the Catchment Board agreed that if we retired certain areas of our higher altitude country they would assist financially to put in protection banks. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),22</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> A run plan based on the report was drawn up by the Catchment Board, calling for compensation for loss of grazing land to be retired from use ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,108</i>)

>  We retired the area from the gorge to the farm boundary - which was an awkward corner and difficult to get to anyway. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Autumn 22</i>)

### retired
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [ high country station land] closed to grazing animals.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> To assist in providing grazing for sheep from the retired lands, certain subsidies are available. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,2</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The sheep displaced from the retired summer country would be found grazing on the more extrensive hill country below 4,000' ... <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,8</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> Now no mountain sheep are on Spear Peak to muster, they've shut up such land - it's retired, so they say ... <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,227</i>)

### retirement block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large area of high country land that closed to stock-grazing.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> In the summer period there is a marked decline in both the retirement blocks and the extensive blocks. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,24</i>)

### retirement fence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country fence that acts as a snow boundary for stock, also allowing regeneration of natural flora.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> A 100% subsidy is available for a boundary fence on such an area - i.e. a retirement fence. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,2</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The catchment board and the station have together put in a twenty-mile retirement fence at 3,500 feet to prevent stock from using potentially dangerous snow country. <i>(1971,Historic Sheep Stations SI II,30</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> One of the main restrictions on burning is that the snow's got to be down to the retirement fence. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,179</i>)

### retirement plan
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a conservation-motivated station plan to retire portions of high country land from grazing.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> There are two kinds of benefit generated in a retirement plan of the kind discussed here. Firstly, there is the physical change in the land .. there are financial returns .. of the cultivation and improvement programme associated with the retirement plans. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,17</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Retirement plans include subsidies to develop other areas of a station so that production lost from one area can be replaced by developing other country lower down. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,179</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> Land retirement plans and the surrender of Crown pastoral lands have decreased the area of back country used for sheep and cattle grazing. <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,169</i>)

### retireti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> There are the native spinach or Renga-renga (<i>Tetragonia expansa</i>), the Pana-pana, or cress (<i>Cardamine hirsuta</i>), and the Reti-reti, or sorrel (<i>Oxalis [211] magellanica</i>), which do for salad and green vegetables. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/210</i>)

### retread
 
 <i>n.</i> a nurse or teacher returning to their profession; a retired person returned to work;

>  "The group of us returning to the workforce were dubbed the 'retreads', and the name has stuck." <i>(Dec 2002,NZ Nursing Review,12</i>)

>  n. soldier who re-enlists; superannuated public servant who re-enters the Service as a casual officer. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  retread n. soldier who re-enlists; superannuated public servant who re-enters the Service as a casual officer. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>BREAM</b> Mr Willis had taught for forty years, and then retired. He had now returned to the school ... to help relieve the teacher shortage. He was a very nice person, as were all our retreads, ... ?ca1980 <i>(1974,I'm Sorry Amanda</i>)

### retro-personed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  ...Courtenay Place is set to become retro-personed - seething with rugged types not afraid to burp in public or masticate over a platter of bulls [sic] testicles while ruminating on the vagaries of life on the ponderosa. <i>(Jul 15, 2004,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

### return
 
 <i>n.</i> a second helping (esp. at a restaurant) [ca1945 `No returns']

>  New Zealand has developed many phrases that depend on local interpretation: e.g. `The Limited', `the six oclock rush', `off the bitumen', `motor camp', `to farewell', `to pencil in', `to check up on', `return (at meals)'. All these are immediately understandable to New Zealanders, but not always to others. <i>(1950,Post Office English Course [TS],14</i>)

### return-box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> Return-boxes: The hydraulic classifiers returning the plus-200-mesh through under-flow nozzles to the tube-mills for further grinding. The overflow from these boxes, being minus-200-mesh, went to the Wilfley tables for separation of the concentrates. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),330</i>)

### return-race/return-race shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an internal-race model of woolshed where sheep go to a pen behind, and at the same level as, the board, once shorn.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It is an eight-stand board with return shutes [sic] to which are added automatic counters. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,77</i>)

>  Return-race sheds may use space inefficiently but they do have certain advantages. Shorn sheep go to the back of the shed by return races which are on the same level as the pens and board. The return races can also be used for footrotting, inocculating and drafting. Unwelcome draughts are avoided in an internal-race shed. <i>(Nov 27, 1975,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  Variations include centre-board, side-port, across-the-board, U-board, raised board and return race. [return chute] <i>(Apr 24, 1980,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> (meaning as in returned serviceman)

>  Bas Tubert is an ornament to the broadcasting scene. I have never met him personally, but I had assumed he was a mere stripling probably crowding 50. Now, I learn from the Evening Post that Mr Tubert is 63 and a returned man. <i>(May 2, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. to the rural area] a rehabilitated serviceman who is allotted land.

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Senney's father, who saw only his own great guilt anatomized in Thrumbull's wheelchair to prove he wasn't a Returned Man, a Returned Soldier. <i>(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,72</i>)

>  Today the supervisor is a man of Maori blood, a returned soldier of the last war, .. . <i>(1948,Our Own Country,53</i>)

>  The intellectual is as snobbish in his attitude to books and writers as other New Zealanders are to many things, notably to returned soldiers - whether they had been `cocoanut bombers' in the Pacific or had been `really overseas' to Africa, ... <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/223</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

### revert
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [land] to regress to growing scrub or bush rather than pasture.

>  Where the soil was poor and the rainfall high the tendency to revert to second growth was so strong that pastures could not be maintained. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This block, now 10,000 acres, has reverted badly over the years ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,88</i>)

### reverted
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] over-run with original cover, second-growth or bush.

### rewarewa
 
 <i>n.</i> NZ honeysuckle; also called New Zealand honeysuckle a native forest tree with reddish flowers, poplar-like in appearance. [Maori] Knightia excelsa =BUCKET-OF-WATER-WOOD, =HONEYSUCKLE (N.Z.), a forest tree and its timber [check honeysuckle]

>  tall tree. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The trees which the natives chiefly make subservient to their purposes, are [besides the pines cowrie, totarra, towha and zarida mentioned above] ... the henow, from which they extract a black dye, the towha, a tree resembling the sycamore, the <i>river river</i>, the grain of whose wood is similar to that of the beech, a species of cork-tree, called by these people vow, a large tree named eckoha, and another named kycata, a tall and beautiful tree, ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/245</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> R|awa r|awa; A tree so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,201</i>)

>  <b>M'DONNELL</b> A great variety of hard-wood grows at New Zealand, admirably adapted for the timbering of any sized ships; among them is the boride[e-acute], rattar[as-acute], taraide[ase-acute], mai[a-acute], totara[a1-acute], koi katoa[a2-acute], toa[a-acute] toa[a-acute], tani raha[as-acute], to wai[a-acute], reiva[a-acute] reiva[a-acute], tana[as-acute], and many others <i>(1834,Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Rewarewa (<i>Knightia excelsa</i>) - This tree is found in dry forests, and where the soil is loose and gravelly in its texture. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),44</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other trees abounded in Pamaki [a's-acute], such as the kaikatea [a2-acute], towai, rewa-rewa, totara [a's-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], kahika [a2-acute], tipow, tanikaha [a2-acute], rimu, and various kinds of akkas, all of which will be found described in the Appendix (Note 7). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/396 The Rewa rewa [a's-acute], (pinus rewarewa,) is a handsome grained wood, very serviceable to the builder and joiner. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  The Titoki (<i>Alectryon excelsum</i>), the Akeake (<i>Dodonaea viscosa</i>), the Ngaio (<i>Myoporum laetum</i>), and above all the Rewa Rewa (<i>Knightia excelsa</i> ), yield woods, out of which some very beautiful pieces of furniture have been made. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/14</i>)

### rewena bread
 
 <i>n.</i> (Cuisine) leavened bread [ORIGIN: Maori, often sp. rewana]

>  Accompanying rewana bread was also excellent, having all the sticky, yeasty, potatoey sponginess you could desire from this post-contact sourdough loaf. Aptly, this rewana bread was the centrepiece of a separate appetiser ... <i>(Feb 28, 2009,Dominion Post Indulgence,11</i>)

>  The menu features traditional Maori food such as pork, puha and dumpling boil-up, along with some New Zealand inspired dishes. Rewana bread is served at the table covered with a woven flax hood and the waitress offers a <i>karakia</i> or blessing before eating. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 : 162</i>)

>  The partners plant to ply the hungry with an eclectic assortment of dishes - seafood mornay, raw fish, lambs fry, whitebait fritters and rewena bread. <i>(May 25, 2000,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  I would have loved to have try the Paua Bar's rewena (wheaten bread risen with yeast brewed from flour and potatoes) but as our waiter explained, their baker was away and they wouldn't try to make it themselves. <i>(Aug 10, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  <b>CARKEEK</b> The Maori word for bread is paraoa which is a trans-literation of the word flour. Various kinds of bread were made by the whalers, traders, and missionaries and some of them survive today and are regarded by present-day Maoris as non-Pakeha items of diet. One of these is called rewena (leaven) bread, another is the panakeke, or pancake-type bread. <i>(1966,Kapiti Coast,52</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> I've a big rewena bread for lunch, made in the camp oven, brown and crusty. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,29</i>)

>  Taro, poke, tarua, green bananas, yams, red island mayonnaise and doughnnuts - and hangi food, pork bones and rewana bread. <i>(1997,Mana,Autumn : 67</i>)

>  Nesian Mystik is basically a tropical fruit salad with rewena bread on the side. <i>(Apr 27, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),20</i>)

### ribbonwood
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Hoheria papulnea </i> [aka Lacebark. The tallest of three main ribbonwoods / lacebarks (12 metres), natural distribution just north half of north island.]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Ribbon-wood of Otago. <i>Hoheria populnea. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Hoheria populnea</i>, A. Cunn. Hohere, Ribbonwood of Dunedin.--An ornamental shrub-tree, 10-30 feet high. Bark fibrous and used for cordage <i>(1883,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),130</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> HOHERIA POPULNEA (<i>A.Cunn.</i>) ... The `HOUI' or `WHAU-WHI'.- ... grows abundantly in both Islands ... Owing to the chaste [snow-white] appearance of its flowers, it has not inaptly been termed by some the `New Zealand orange Blossom'. The inner bark is peculiar, and consists of layer upon layer of laced fibre, ...From this peculiarity it is called by the settlers the `Thousand Jacket', `lace-bark', or ... <i>Ibid.</i> 177 Houi [Maori name] .. Ribbon-wood [Settlers' name] .. Hoheria populnea .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,55</i>)

>  <b>NAIRN & SON</b> (And) <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ribbonwood <i>(1912,Catalogue (Nairn & Son)</i>)

>  houhere, a tree (Hoheria populnea), ribbonwood, lacebark, thousand-jacket. One of the three or four deciduous Maori trees. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>ribbonwood n </i>1 : a New Zealand shrub or small tree (Hoheria populnea) of the family Malvaceae having bark that is used for cordage and a profusion of small snow-white flowers in close axillary clusters - called also houhere 2 : RIBBON TREE <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1951</i>)

>  Ribbon-wood or lace-bark tree ... Common on the central ranges and West Coast. <i>(1868,TrNZI,1/33</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Overhanging the tossing creek [at Lennox Falls, Otago] bend Ribbon-wood trees, [fn <i>Hoheria populnea</i>] with masses of soft, white blossom ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,285</i>) [NOTES: 1892 Jan 29 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 285]



 
 <i>n.</i> names given to plants with a fibrous, lace-like inner bark espec. <i>Hoheria</i>, and <i>Plagianthus</i> spp =HOUHERE, =WILD CHERRY, =LACEBARK, =MANATU, =THOUSAND-JACKET [General; aka ribbon wood, ribbon-wood] (See [houhere](../H#houhere),[mountain ribbonwood](../M#mountain-ribbonwood),[lowland ribbonwood](../L#lowland-ribbonwood),[marsh ribbonwood](../M#marsh-ribbonwood),[saltmarsh  ribbonwood et al](../S#saltmarsh--ribbonwood-et-al))

>  No imported tree surpasses in lavishness of white blossom the New Zealand hoheria, variously called ribbonwood and lacebark, nor equals in beauty of shape or colour its intricately dentate and translucent green leaf. <i>(Oct 12, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,14</i>)

>  The woods, it appears, which struck the most were the Rewarewara [sic], Mangias, mottled Kauri, Ribbonwood, Totara, Mairi, Tanekaea, Matai, Rimu, white tea-tree, and white Kauri, the latter being much in quest by ship builders. <i>(Jan 13, 1863,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> `Plants of both this [<i>Hoheria</i>] and of the next genus (<i>Plagianthus</i>), are popularly known as <i>Ribbonwoods</i>. The bast or inner bark is perforated by the medullary rays, and this gives to it a characteristic, ribbon-like appearance.' <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,270</i>)

>  ... in some cases, such as the Ribbon woods, plants of different genera are included in the one group..Ribbon Wood (<i>Plagianthus betulinus</i>). A very ornamental tree, especially when in flower, being covered with small white flowers... Ribbon Wood (<i>Hoheria populnea </i>var.<i> angustifolia</i>)... Ribbon Wood (<i>Pennantia corymbosa</i>). This tree and the two former [<i>Plagianthus</i> and <i>Hoheria</i>], are often confounded, being very similar in general appearance, in soft, white, easily splitting, worthless wood; in the season, being covered with masses of small white fragrant flowers; ... Ribbon Wood (<i>Hoheria populnea</i> var. <i>crataegifolia</i>)... Ribbon Wood, or Lace-bark tree (<i>Plagianthus Lyalli</i>) A very ornamental shrub tree, with large leaves and flowers. Common on the central ranges and west coast... Ribbonwood. <i>Plagianthus betulinus</i>, A. Cunn. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 Ribbonwood. <i>Hoheria populnea</i>, var. <i>angustifolia</i> ... Ribbonwood. <i>Pennantia Corymbosa </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  [Native name] Houi .. [Settlers' name] Ribbonwood ..[Family] Malvaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>WHITE</b> Its vernacular names are `Ribbon Wood' and `Lace Bark,' for when the bark is pulled off and cut into strips it reminds one of insertion lace. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,90</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> The Maoris apply the names <i>hoihere</i> or<i> houhere</i> to varieties [of<i> Hoheria populnea</i>] indifferently; the European settlers usually call all the forms `ribbon-wood' or `lacebark', names which are, unfortunately, also used for <i>Plagianthus betulinus</i> and <i>Gaya Lyallii. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,79</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The varied names given by the Maoris in different districts to this plant [<i>Hoheria</i> spp.], are all said to spring from the same root. <i>Whau</i> [252] signifies <i>wrapped about, netted, - like lace</i>, and the treeis so called from the character of its inner bark. Thesettlers name it lace-bark, ribbon-wood, [1940 edn, no hyphens] or thousand-jacket. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,251,1940  p. 264 ed.</i>)

>  <b>175</b> <i>ribbonwood</i>: hoheria. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.</i>)

>  A small New Zealand tree of the mallow family; also called `lacebark'. [both Hoheria and Plagianthus are mallows] <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),344,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... picturing the start of the track and lacebark or ribbonwood in flower bringing thoughts of white glimpses of miners' sudden canvastowns. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,131</i>)

>  <i>ribbonwood n. </i>a small evergreen malvaceous tree of New Zealand. Its wood is used in furniture-making. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,990</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 655]]

>  On the whole the beech forest is dark and gloomy, but here and there it may be lighted up by a tuft of scarlet or yellow native mistletoe [fn Species of<i> Loranthus</i>.], or in spring by the gay garlands of white clematis; while in the more open places grows the beautiful ribbon-wood [fn <i>Gaya ribifolia</i>, slightly different from the more western <i>Gaya lyallii</i>.], with a profusion of white flowers resembling pear-blossom. <i>Ibid.</i> Masses of Ribbonwood will give a pale green hue to a portion of a hillside, ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,186</i>)

>  On account of the fibrous nature of [<i>Plagianthus Lyallii's</i>] inner bark, it is popularly known in the Middle Island as one of the `Ribbon Wood' or `Lace Bark' trees, as is the <i>Hoheria populnea</i>, and its varieties in the Northern Island. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Whauwhi [Maori name] .. Ribbon wood [Settlers' name] .. Plagianthus Lyallii .. Small tree `Ribbon-wood'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,54</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods, ... miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others, ... and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Ribbonwood, n.</i> All species of <i>Plagianthus</i> and <i>Hoheria</i> are to the colonists Ribbonwood, especially <i>Plagianthus betulinus</i>, A. Cunn., and <i>Hoheria populnea</i>, A Cunn., the bark of which is used for cordage, and was once used for making a demulcent drink. Alpine Ribbon-wood, <i>Plagianthus lyalli</i>. Hook. Other popular names are <i>Houhere, Houi</i> (Maori), <i>Lace-bark</i> (q.v.), and <i>Thousand-Jacket</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,386</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Plagianthus betulinus</i>, A.Cunn.] Ribbon-wood of Europeans; manatu of the Maoris. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,78</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Ribbonwood .. .. manatu .. .. <i>Plagianthus betulinus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  Houhere (lacebark or ribbonwood) head piece. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,182</i>)

>  The pillars and styles of this are of rimu, the panels of ribbonwood, and the mouldings of riwariwa. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> Another tree which sheds its leaves is the ribbon-wood... [Note.] <i>Gaya Lyalli</i>. <i>(1911,They Led the All Blacks,83</i>)

>  Their names have been burnt onto stakes identifying them as the planters of nikau, akeake, hoho, mahoe, and ribnbonwood, all Chatham Island natives. <i>(Nov 2002,Footnotes,No. 18/7</i>)

>  Over a dozen species and varieties new to the Chatham islands have been obtained and these include.. a ribbonwood (Plagianthus cymosus). <i>(Mar 4, 1924,Otago Witness,70</i>)

### rice-paper plant
 
 <i>n.</i> [CC Ransom comments: a variety of ginseng. Dictionary of Gastronomy (Andre L. Simon and Robin Howe (Deitsch, London 1978) under `RICE-PAPER made from the pith of a ... tree and edible]

### rice-plant
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> We sucked honey from various spring flowers, but the most prized was a low-growing and a very prickly native heather with flowers about half or three quarters of an inch long that grew on the stony plain. There were masses of it which we called rice-plant because its masses of sweet, juicy white berries spread out near the ground resembled polished rice in appearance. A plant that we called `wild birch' (because its leaves resembled birch leaves, I suppose) had red astringent berries that we enjoyed. It grew among the manuka scrub. ... (1900; Lower Moutere) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,115</i>)

 <i>n.</i> synonym for dickhead

 <i>n.</i>

### ricie
 
 <i>n.</i> a Japanese motorbike

>  Japanese bikes were once known disparagingly as "ricies", but this prejudice seems to have disappeared, possibly because of their dominance of racing for the last thirty years. <i>(2000,Directions,Summer 2000 No. 46 : 56</i>)

### rick-ma-tick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Alison Nevill, of Dunedin, asks about the fascinating phrase "the whole rick-ma-tick". She says her Scottish grandmother employed a maid in the 40s who used it constantly, meaning something like "that's all there is to it". The maid came from Stirling, and the family always thought it was one of her home-town expressions, so when Alison got to New Zealand, she was surprised to hear it being used in the same context of finality. "The whole rick-ma-tick" has a long and honourable history as a Scottish version of "the whole kit and caboodle", an expression from the United States east coast that is widely understood here as an ornate alternative to "the whole lot". <i>(Mar 19, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### ricker
 
 <i>n.</i> a young kauri in the pole stage [(? forestry or sawmillers')]

>  <b>6</b> The fifty-feet kauri rika goal posts excited much admiration. <i>(Jul 29, 1930,Northern Advocate</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Kauri rickers made up the first cargoes. <i>(1973,Kauri,16</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The young tree is known as a "ricker", a name derived from an old Anglo-Saxon word applied to a pole used in making hayricks, or to the spar of a boat. The name was probably first applied to the young kauri by the men of the Royal Navy in search of ships' spars. <i>(1964,New Story of the Kauri, The,44</i>)

>  At a given signal they gave one strong pull altogether to see if everything was safe; and the tall kauri ricker trembled and quivered from top to butt. <i>(Aug 23, 1884,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  "I would advocate the reproduction of the kauri tree, as I am credibly informed that a kauri ricker when 20 years old exudes that white resinous matter which is the kauri gum in its primitive state. .." <i>(Nov 12, 1902,Otago Witness,34</i>)

>  A COMPACT FARM, situate in Soldier's Bay, on the North Shore, containing 21 acres 6 roods; it is well adapted for Dairy and other purposes, a considerable quantity being covered with grass of 3 year's [sic] growth; there are seven acres of fire-wood, and abundance of rickers for masts; bark for the tanner, various dye woods, and an overflowing stream of fresh water. <i>(Apr 7, 1849,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  In the Waitakere hills across the valley, kauri rickers are starting to poke their heads above the other trees. <i>(Apr 27, 1997,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> [=a native pine] ... the roof [of the station buildings, Canterbury c1859] were of round pine framing covered with rickers (young pine plants) and thatched with snow grass. <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,24</i>)

>  ... a vigorous second growth interspersed with young kauri rickers soon appeared. <i>(1949,NZ Geographic,57</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The sapling [of kauri] is known as a `ricker', sometimes incorrectly spelt `rika' It is not, however, of Maori origin, but an old Anglo-Saxon term applied to a pole used in making hay-ricks, or to the spar of a boat. The name was probably first applied to the young kauri by the men of the Royal Navy in search of ships' spars. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,61</i>)

>  Ricker: (b) A kauri tree in the pole stage, N.Z. <i>(1956,NZ Timber Journal,4/9/56</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Rikka, the bushman's word for a young kauri, long supposed to be a Maori word, turns out to be an English dialect word for a pole, apparently introduced by workers from England in the kauri timber trade as long ago as the 1790s. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> Out from the valley we came upon a company of young kauri ...; tall rickers fit for the early sailing ships. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,80</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> ... the spire-like young [kauri] tree is called a `ricker'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,212</i>)

>  ricker n. young Kauri tree <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,418</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Then it would be necessary to use the smaller kauris, the young trees known as rikkers. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,150</i>) [NOTES: (Joe Gasparich, Northland kauri country)]

>  There was a long bridge on the main road,.. the piles of which, totara rickers, had rotted away. <i>(Sep 13, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Then it would be necessary to use the smaller kauris, the young trees known as rickers. Ibid. 162. They used kauri rickers as cranes. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,150</i>)

>  In April and March the Moriori lashed rickers to the tree boles to climb up by. <i>(Aug 10, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Virtually all the gentler lower slopes are now covered in regenerating kanuka-kauri ricker forest 95-130 years old. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> In those days, when there were several in one tent, we used to make what was called a community bunk, as there was not enough room to erect ricker (small round rails or saplings) bunks for four men. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,48</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a pole or spar cut from a long, slender tree-trunk; the trunk of such a tree; (occas ?) such a tree [?d. Ed Fairburn Makaratanga 29 [cut] long, thin `Rickers' and made them into ladders. (Auckland bushmen's and forestry general) (occas. rika) a kauri sapling; a kauri-tree in the pole stage]

>  <b>McNAB</b> [Cargo from NZ] 10 logs of timber, 890 rickers, 165 handspikes, 13 casks of black whale oil, ... 3 tons flax, 86 bundles coopers' flags, ... [consigned to George Weller]. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,102</i>) [NOTES: 1834 Apr 26 Log Lucy Ann: Cargo in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 102]

>  1 RICKER, 53ft x 13in. at the butt. <i>(Nov 24, 1847,New Zealander, The,1</i>)

>  CONNELL & RIDINGS Will Sell by Auction, THIS DAY, 20th inst., at 11 o'clock, on the Beach, Mechanics' Bay, FIFTY KAURI SPARS and RICKERS ... <i>(Jan 20, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  May 31 - River Chief, brig, 158 tons, James H. Horseley, for Tahiti, with 15 spars, 13,600 feet of timber, 25 rickers, 7,700 feet sawn timber, 20,000 feet ditto. <i>(Jun 4, 1851,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... I was employed collecting a quantity of spars, for ships' masts, ... &c., logs, for sawing into plank, and rickers or trunks of trees, of narrow diameter but great length. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/93</i>)

>  <b>J. WHITE</b> ... cutting rickers for Jess. <i>(1847,Journal,74</i>)

>  <b>3/88</b> The noose made of a slender strip of flax leaf attached to the end of a ricker or rod. <i>(1871,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Potts) [Canterbury]

>  ... he picked up a ricker and twisted in the [fence] gap mechanically. <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/256</i>)

>  On many farms in this colony `rickers' can be got for the cutting [for fence-making]. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,94</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> That the walls are formed of posts sunk into the ground, covered with a network of rickers, overlaid with tongai (dried flax blades) toe-toe, or raupo ... <i>Ibid.</i> 253 It was an appetising display ... a mound of potatoes, haangi steamed, ... alternated with stacks of eels enclosed in wrappings of `raurekau' leaves (coprosma grandifolia), baked on a framework of rickers over a clear fire, ... delicate of flavour. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,7</i>)

>  Having provided ... ricker saplings and other materials of wood, he [the Moriori] gathered dry flower stalks of Phormium ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 363</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 363]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... there was not enough room to erect ricker (small round rails or saplings bunks for four men. <i>Ibid.</i> 97 The bunks were made entirely from rickers (young birch saplings), while from several packing cases we constructed cupboards, making them mice-proof. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,48</i>)

>  Ricker: (a) A long pole or sapling used for handling floating timber at the docks. <i>(1956,NZ Timber Journal,2/9/56</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> <i>(1949,It Was So Late,14</i>)

>  <b>CHEVASSE & JOHNS</b> The dangerous job was then to roll the logs down to a stream or on to a skid road made of "rickers". <i>(1983,NZ Forest Parks,27</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### ride
 
 <i>v.</i> to loaf on, to let somebody do part of one's share of work. (cp CARRY) [ca1951 Foxton Freezing Works.]



 
 <i>v.</i> usu. ride in, sc. stock, esp. cattle

>  <b>FITTON</b> I have seen here clergymen ploughing, ... officers of the army ... riding in stock, and no one thought the worse of them, but the contrary. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),354</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec J.R.Godley's Address in 1856 Fitton NZ 354]

>  <b>W.H.S. ROBERTS</b> At Macfarlane's the men saddled fresh horses and rode in some cattle which we had a great bother to yard, ... The yard was divided into three - ... but as there was no crush pen, or drafting race, we had to head rope those requiring brands, and ... legroped and threw them ... <i>(Dec 18, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec 18 W.H.S. ROBERTS Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 43]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher</i>) [NOTES: [see quot COVE.]]

>  <b>BOOTH</b> I learned to ride stock, shoe horses, shear sheep ... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,30</i>)
