 <i>phr.</i> (See [boundary](../B#boundary))

### ride one's hobnails
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to walk (See [by hobnail express](../B#by-hobnail-express))

>  <b>STAPLES</b> Peterson rode his horse and Shiny rode his hobnails. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,42</i>)

>  They do not walk, they ride their hobnails, and on horseback they ride the pigskin. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### ride out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to separate cattle in a mob, with the use of a stockhorse.

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Once the calves had mothered up again a few days later, they were quietly ridden out of the mob and sent to Waihora. <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,8</i>)

### ride round
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to block or inspect stock, especially during lambing or calving.

>  Rode round Raw paddock. <i>(Apr 7, 1889,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> 'Riding round' the ewes. <i>(Nov 23, 1906,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> .. the shepherd spends several hours of each day in 'riding round the sheep', long the most popular job of any sheep farm. The job of riding round the sheep can be done by a schoolchild, the owner's wife or a land girl. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. if any further argument against riding round ewes was needed ... <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,69</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> In the meantime my main job was to ride round the paddocks for cast sheep and keep an eye on growing lambs. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,96</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> But Jim Clews had a theory that we did more harm than good in riding round the ewes in dirty weather. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,112</i>)

### ride the block
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [shepherding or lambing] to 'ride round' or inspect ewes and lambs.

>  <b>DREAVER</b> The job of 'riding the block' in the spring was great. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

### ride the gummy-gummies
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [cattle] to walk astride a small tree (thought to be Nothopanax colensoi), eat the particularly glossy, palatable leaves, and on finishing, leave it to spring back.

>  <b>BUCKINGHAM</b> The cattle used also to 'ride the gummy-gummies' which were small trees with wide shiny leaves - good feed, they loved it. They learnt to straddle these trees and push them over so that they could get a the tasty leaves. <i>(1969,From Hethersett to Quarry Hills,44</i>)

### ride the pine
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Sport) to sit on the substitutes' bench

>  England's bovver boy hooker Richard Cockerill jeered at Norman Hewitt about Hewitt riding the pines in Dunedin. <i>(Jun 28, 1998,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  [Caption] Sitting it out: Lock Keith Robinson rides the pine after injuring his calk on Friday night. <i>(Aug 2, 2004,Dominion Post,D8</i>)

### ride work
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  T. Buddicomb, after a long absence from the saddle, has once more commenced to ride work. <i>(Dec 3, 1902,Otago Witness,47</i>)

>  .. and I am of the opinion that jockeys disqualified for minor offences, such as "suspicious" riding or disobedience at the post ought to be allowed to ride work on the training tracks during the time of their suspension. <i>(Jun 4, 1891,Otago Witness,26</i>)

>  A.J. M'Flynn, who was unfortunate enough to receive a nasty crumpling right on the eve of the big winter fixtures, has now recovered sufficiently to be able to ride work and hopes to be fit enough to take a let up in public at Avondale next month. <i>(Jul 22, 1913,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Jockeys are supposed to attend in the morning to ride work, and their licenses are issued on that understanding. <i>(Oct 10, 1925,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  When Woodville finished, Hec. jumped aboard the Limited that night and hurried north to be at Takanini to ride work the next morning. <i>(Dec 18, 1930,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  <i>(1950,Landfall,4/1: 19</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> <i>(1959,Short Stories (various authors),271</i>) [NOTES: 1959 Gee in 1966 Short Stories 271]

### ridge fence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenceline on the outside of a ridge.

>  <b>LEVY</b> When there are ridge fences on a property, and there is good access to the tops of the hills for stock on each side of the fences as a result of bulldozing the ridges before the erection of fences, the cleaning of the country is greatly facilitated. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,202</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Ridge fencing is needed to control stock. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,260</i>)

### ridge-board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The ridge-boards are carved to represent a number of ngarara, or lizards, running along the roof, and the maihi, or gable-boards, have carvings of the mythological animal known as the manaia - probably a kind of taniwha. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/674</i>)

### ridge-pole
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) the head

>  He.. told me several amazing anecdotes of his younger days - "days before the hatch began to blow off the ridge pole," as Bob observed, stroking the bald part of his head. <i>(Dec 23, 1890,Otago Witness,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> of a Maori meeting-house

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> The church [at Otaki] is quite original ... the sides and ends are lined with panels of plaited flax ... these are continued as high as the ridge-pole. <i>Ibid.</i> 35 After a smoke a corrobery* ensued, then we began to think of taking some rest. The whare was built of toi-toi, about 12 feet by 8, and 6 feet to the ridge-pole; ... [fn] Conversation. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,27</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Kimble Bent, the pakeha-Maori, related to me that when Te Ua was in Otapawa pa ... a ruru flew from the forest at dusk and perched on the ridge-pole of the house in front of which the prophet was sitting. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/7,1983 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Mr White then said, that was his object too; why then should the Wanganui tribe be divided! it [sic] was one house, let there be but one ridge-pole. <i>(Mar 28, 1863,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> The whanau was my home. The love and affection they held for each other were the ridgepoles of my heart. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,107</i>)

### ridge-pole rafter
 
 <i>n.</i> praying mantis

>  <b>BARLOW</b> Another curious insect found here is the `Mantis,' commonly called, on account of its shape, `the ridge-pole rafter.' <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,186</i>)

### ridge-riding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GEELEN</b> Like other experienced topdressing pilots he selected this ridge because it lay parallel to the sowing direction and he kept as close as possible to it thereby taking advantage of the added lift provided by the air rising off the ridge face. This procedure, known as "ridge riding", meant the aircraft was flown about five metres above the crest of the ridge and slightly to one side of it. <i>(1983,Topdressers, The,111</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the flying of an aircraft close to the top and one side of a ridge, in order to catch a lift or upwards draught.

>  <b>GEELEN</b> This procedure, known as "ridge-riding" meant the aircraft was flown about five metres above the crest of the ridge and slightly to one side of it. <i>(1983,Topdressers, The,111</i>)

### ridgeline
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Much of the Town Belt is on ridgelines close to the city's urban areas... However, protection of some ridgelines is not always guaranteed... Do you want to help to develop criteria to manage activities on ridgetops and hilltops. <i>(Dec 2000,Dominion,unpaged</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] the horizon of a grazing hill ridge.

>  Their 87ha block rises some 305, bounded below by stream bed and above by a ridgeline. <i>(Jul 2001,Lifestyle Farmer,12</i>)

>  They graze a strip of grass up near the ridgeline with an electric fence to keep them out of regenerating bush. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,14</i>)

### ridging-plough
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a plough, used in swampy areas, to raise ridges upon which plants can be established.

>  It [the land] was again ploughed in the spring and worked into a fine tilth, being then drilled with a ridging-plough. <i>(Jun 9, 1898,Hawera Star,4</i>)

### ridgy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere, but significant in NZ hill country]

>  Country ridgy and broken, approaching to mountainous <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,239</i>)

>  Even on the ridgy lands the crops are better than for many years. <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,154</i>)

### riding
 
 <i>n.</i> a territorial subdivision of a county

>  He considered his road the worst in the riding. <i>(Jun 7, 1930,Northern Advocate,2</i>)

>  The following are the divisions of [t]he Auckland counties into ridings so far as the work had progressed up to yesterday afternoon. <i>(Nov 30, 1876,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The electoral roll for the County of Whangarei, on which the poll will be taken on Wednesday next, contains 802 names, distributed as follows:- Hikurangi riding 156, Kiripaka riding 110, Manaia riding 244, Maunu riding 203, Otonga riding 89. <i>(Nov 4, 1899,Northern Advocate,5</i>)

>  The voting is taking place for the County Council under the new riding system, whereby eight ridings return one member each, instead of three ridings three each. <i>(Nov 10, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The main road through the county is supposed to be constructed by the County Council, which is composed of representatives from the several ridings or districts forming the county, each riding electing a councillor every three years. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,95</i>)

>  When the nine provinces were abolished in 1876 the colony was divided for local government purposes into counties, regard being had to boundaries of existing road districts, and the counties were subdivided into ridings, each riding being empowered to elect a certain number of members of the Council of the county. The ridings comprise one or more of the above three districts. They have no governing body - they are simply the electoral divisions of the county, and their maximum number is nine. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,142</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> The term `riding' as part of a county would have little meaning today. <i>(1970,Way Up North,75</i>)

>  riding ... NZ a sub-division of a local government authority (Kapiti riding). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,656</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 419]]

>  He noted that GBP400 had been allocated to the Waverley riding. <i>(Jul 9, 1918,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

### RIFA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> red imported fire ants

>  Some insects, instead of risking the hazards of riding the wind, take the safer option of hitchhiking on aircraft. This seems to hasve been the case with a nest of red imported fire ants (RIFA) found at Auckland Airport in March, 2001... RIFA originate in the swampy Pantanal area of Brazil, Paraguay and Argentina. <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/18</i>)

### riffle
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>BLYTH</b> Riffles: The diagonal, lengthwise or cross pieces or grooves in sluices which catch the heavier metallic particles allowing the clay and sand to pass. The value of the wash up is determined by the amount of gold or other metal caught in the riffles or other devices. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> RIFFLE OR RIPPLE: both terms were used for the grooves cut across the bottom of a sluice-box or for the much more common wooden bars fixed at right angles, obliquely, or lengthwise, to the bottom of a sluice. The `riffleboard' was detachable and formed a `false bottom'. `Riffle' was probably a Californian term; `ripple' seems to have been more usual on the goldfields of Australasia. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Riffle: shallow stretch of a stream or river, between pools, where the water runs fast and undulating. Also, probably by derivation, a raised wooden or metal crossbar in a sluice box. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,113</i>)

### riffleboard
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [riffle](../R#riffle))

### rifflebox
 
 <i>n.</i> [(goldmining) 1868 [get quots from discards]] (See [also ripple](../A#also-ripple))

### rifle game
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Thomson's stalking was not limited to red deer. He has followed most introduced rifle game in the South Island. <i>(Apr 8, 1953,Freedom,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (historical) (usually pl.) [cf. (bush-)ranger]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The ranks of our Rifle Rangers, our Volunteer Corps [in Taranaki] <i>(1865,England's NZ War,17</i>)

### rifle wren
 
 <i>n.</i>

### riflebeak
 
 <i>n.</i> =RIFLEMAN ?misunderstanding or error

>  <b>HULL</b> I do not know to what to attribute the scarcity of small birds, ...such as crows, saddlebacks, thrushes, tuis, canaries, wrens, riflebeaks, fantails and many others. <i>(1987,Richard Henry of Resolution Island,88</i>) [NOTES: 1897 Apr 5 E.H. Wilmot Exploration and Topographical Survey in Fiord Country in 1987 Hill Richard Henry of Resolution Island 88]

### rifleman
 
 <i>n.</i> a tiny New Zealand bush bird, <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>, [probably from green and bronze plumage resembling an early military uniform] (See [also thumb bird](../A#also-thumb-bird))

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 15. XENICUS STOKESII. Gray. Grey Wren. Stripe-faced Wren. Rifleman. ... <i>a broad white streak from each nostril over each eye to the back of the head; ... </i> <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,7</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ACANTHISITTA CHLORIS \{ACANTHIDOSITTA CHLORIS\}. (RIFLEMAN.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Titipounamu, \{Kikimutu,\} Kikirimutu, Pihipihi, Piripiri, Tokepiripiri, and Moutuutu. [(1873) 113] The Rifleman is the smallest of our New-Zealand birds; .... It is very generally distributed. [f.n. explaining Acanthidositta as more correct Greek] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,112</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... to say nothing of Korimako and Pitoitoi ... we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The <i>Zosterops</i> was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and ... we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; ... and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Rifleman</i>, n. a bird of New Zealand, <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>, Buller; Maori name, <i>Titipounamu</i>. See quotation. The name is sometimes applied also to the Rifle-bird [? in Australia] (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,388</i>)

>  <i>Rii.fle-man</i> (r&imacr.i.f'l-man), <i>n. [Add.] </i>2. (Zoo.].) A small passerine bird of New Zealand (<i>Acanthidositta chloris</i>); so called from its green and bronze plumange, which is thought to resemble the uniform of the early British volunteer rifle corps. <i>Buller</i>. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  Amongs[t?] the creepers there are the bush-wren, the rock-wren, the rifleman and the yellow-headed native canary. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,195</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Houtuutu, n. Acanthidositta chloris, bush wren.</i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,75</i>)

>  <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i> Sparr. Rifleman. Owing to the uncertainty regarding the species denoted by the term `wren' as used by the average observer, many records which may apply to this species have been left strictly alone. In the Wellington District the grey warbler (<i>Maorigerygone igata</i> Quoy & Gaim.) is often referred to in popular parlance as the `native wren.' <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/87</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>POWELL</b> RIFLEMAN (<i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>). This is a wren and our smallest bird, being only three inches in total length. There are two forms, typical chloris from the South Island and <i>A. chloris granti</i>, the North Island rifleman....The Maori name is Titi-pounamu. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  <i>rifleman</i>: smallest forest bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The robins, riflemen and tomtits tamed very soon, the robins being an easy first in this respect. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,108,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Houtuutu, n. Acanthidositta chloris, rifleman, </i>a bird. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,63</i>)

>  2. A popular name for a New Zealand wren also called `bush wren' Its green plumage suggests the uniform worn by riflemen long ago. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),345</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Bush robins, parakeets, tomtits, grey warblers, riflemen, pigeons and the bush hawk are still to be seen. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,129</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> RIFLEMAN <i>Acanthisitta chloris Other names</i>: Titipounamu, Thumbie (Nelson). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,191</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Rifleman .. .. titi-pounamu .. .. <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> diminutive New Zealand bird. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Our tiniest native bird is the rifleman ... , which lives in beech forests of the North and South island, from sea level to high country ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,277</i>)

>  <i>rifleman n.</i> diminutive N.Z. bird. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <i>rifleman</i> ... 2. a wren of New Zealand. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,992</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 656]]

>  <i>Rifleman Acanthisitta chloris</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) ... <i>South Island Rifleman A.c. chloris</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787)<i> North Island Rifleman A.c. granti </i>MATHEWS & IREDALE, 1913. ... OTHER NAME: <i>Titipounamu. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,264</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> A pair of dainty mountain riflemen hopped onto a tree within arm's length, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 79 ... maybe a pair of mountain riflemen and a bellbird, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,77</i>)

>  239.a. <i>Acanthisitta chloris granti </i>Mathews and Iredale, 1913 <i>North Island Rifleman </i> <i>(1973,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,63</i>)

>  239.b. <i>Acanthisitta chloris chloris</i> (Sparrman, 1787) <i> South Island Rifleman (Titipounamu) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,63</i>)

>  It's a titipounamu, and you often find him in the ti-tree scrub... It is the rifleman, very shy, and a friend of the scrub-cutter. <i>(Feb 5, 1995,NZ Tablet,7</i>)

>  I knew the tiny hole in the ribbonwood or the beech into which the riflemen crept. <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

### rig
 
 <i>n.</i> a small commercially fished shark; also called gummy shark, spotted dogfish (See [dogfish](../D#dogfish))

>  One commercial fisherman was setting rig nets up to 800 metres long in the inner Sounds - a "wall of death" for fish - yet there were no rules in place to stop it, he said. <i>(Jan 16, 2002,Dominion,9</i>)

>  Year in, year out, Muriwai fisherman Nathan Adams sets his nets off the west coast of the North Island. Mostly he catches rig, the fish formerly known as dog fish, now exported to Australia for good money. <i>(Mar 30, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>: Common dogfish, pioke (Auckland), rigg (Canterbury). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  `But many wholesalers have told us they are moving away from marketing school shark,' Mr Gradwell said. `They prefer rig, elephant fish or spotty shark.' <i>(Nov 8, 1977,Dominion,1/7</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>SMOOTHOUND</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Small Flake Fillets (restricted to this species); Smoothound. (c) Other common names: Kini; Pioke; Lemons; Lemonfish; Dogfish; Rig; Silver Strip (fillets); Sweet William; Flake. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,44</i>)

>  <b>AYL.ING</b> <i>SPOTTED SMOOTH-HOUND Mustelus lenticulatus </i>(Gummy shark, spotted dogfish, rig, pioke) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,56,1984 ed.</i>)

>  It seems mystery surrounds the fish commonly served...in our fish and chip shops - the rig, or spotted dog fish. <i>(Apr 2, 1983,Dominion,5</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Simon made the acquaintance of barracouta, ling, trumpeter, and rig, and red cod, kelp cod, and rock cod. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,251</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Rig <i>Mustelus lenticulatus </i>... Other names include spotted dogfish, gummy shark, smoothhound and pioke. Often listed as <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>, but this name now applies only to a similar Australian species. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,30</i>)

>  [species name] rig .. [scientific name] <i>Mustelus lenticulatus</i> .. [other name(s)] spotted dogfish, gummy shark, kini, pioke, smooth hound, deepsea bream, lemonfish, spotted gummy shark, sweet william, dogi <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] rig 214 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> cf notes OED sb. slang and coll. cf EDD 1. chiefly in run (one's) rigs(s) upon (another). =to make sport, game of : now dial. [c1840 -] 2. trick; swindling racket 3b. run a (or the) rig, run (one's) rigs =play pranks, run riot : 1782-1886 ?dial - Nth? [prison slang]

>  <i>(1865,Punch in Canterbury,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [?Otago-Southland]

>  <b>SHAW & TARRANT</b> At that [c 1860] time the land was ploughed in `rigs' five and a half yards in width, and each man when sowing or harvesting kept to his allotted strip. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,218</i>)

>  <b>SHAW & TARRANT</b> At that [c 1860] time the land was ploughed in `rigs' five and a half yards in width, and each man when sowing or harvesting kept to his allotted strip. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,218</i>)

### rig shark
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rig](../R#rig))

>  <b>BROWN</b> Not too many flat fish ... a good proportion of cod and elephants with a few rig sharks ... thrown in for good measure. <i>(1986,Weaver's Apprentice, The,91</i>)

### rigger
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> orig. SQUARE-RIGGER, a square-gin bottle; thence any large flagon or demijohn for holding esp. draught beer. (or, Havelock, vinegar) cf PETER @n2 [(Jack Columbus 1980 Aug 25) ca1930's (Canterbury) for a square rigger; 40oz square green bottle of square (HJKZ) gin; also for stone jars, a gallon.]

>  A rigger (the modern plastic flagon) of Black Magic from the Sunshine Brewery in Gisborne won't set your mouth on fire but it will leave the taste of burning behind it. <i>(Apr 25, 2001,Capital Times,17</i>)

>  Any bloke who's had to carry a half-dozen riggers.. all the way.. to New Lynn could simply laugh at forced marches as child's play. <i>(Dec 14, 1955,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A quart bottle of beer is a rigger, and a pint bottle a marine; doubtless neither of these terms is peculiar to New Zealand, but they are rarely, if ever, heard in England <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,89</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A quart bottle of beer is a rigger, and a pint bottle a marine; doubtless neither of these terms is peculiar to New Zealand, but they are rarely, if ever, heard in England <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,89</i>)

>  We were just getting back to normal again when he produced an old-time rigger which, he said, contained wampo. <i>(Jun 1957,NZ Truth</i>) [NOTES: 1957 June J.R. Sheehan in Truth in $72]

>  <b>McGILL</b> rigger quart of beer, precursor of the half-gallon jar; short for `squarerigger'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,92</i>)

>  This drew a crowd round, and he tried to humor [sic] her by first getting her home, and then getting a jar and two riggers of beer from the hotel. <i>(Dec 16, 1911,NZ Truth,338/5/5</i>)

>  I'd always bring a couple of riggers [of beer]. <i>(1943,NZ New Writing,5</i>) [NOTES: (Sargeson Episode)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> riggers: large bottles of beer. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> flagon quoting Sargeson That Summer <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,137</i>)

>  <b>C.R. FRANCIS</b> I notice that the word `rigger' [in HNZD P.936] does not include the sense which used to be common, of a 1 litre JDKZ gin bottle filled at the pub with draft beer. I distinctly remember the notice at `His Lordships' a very scruffy tavern in St Asaph St.Ch-ch `No jars or riggers filled after 5-45 pm.' This was deciphered by an elderly habitue for the innocent student.The word is apparently a contraction of the term `square rigger' used to describe a JDKZ bottle... -->P: 5. `Quart of draught beer in square-faced gin bottle': Austr: ca 1918: (B 1942) <i>(Feb 19, 1983,Personal Communication</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a 1.25 or 2 litre plastic bottle of beer

>  Many quality New Zealand beers are never bottled and are only available on tap from the brewery or a bottle store in 1.25 or 2 litre plastic bottles (called variously riggers, flagons or PETs depending on where you are from). I headed off to Regional Wines, and Spirits with a bag of empty riggers to sample some of their beers on tap... While beer in a rigger will keep fresh for a short time, ideally it should be consumed within a day or two of purchase. <i>(Apr 6, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),6</i>)

### right as a bank
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Don't hang on to me, Bill. I'm all right I tell ya. Right as a bank .. I' not drunk drunk I tell ya. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,165</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `How are you, Ted?' I asked. `Right as the bank, boy.' <i>(1976,Pacific Star,121</i>)

### right as a daisy
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> right as a daisy all is well; local variant of many `right as ...' colloquialisms like `right as rain', denoting security or safety. [Shepard] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

### right as pie
 
 <i>phr.</i> good, well [right as pie is the usual form]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> "Are you all right now, do you think?" she asked. [140] "Right as a pie," I replied, "When can I get up?" <i>(1936,Follow the Call,139</i>)

### right as rain
 
 <i>phr.</i> too right; most certainly

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... right as rain <i>(1946,That Summer,178</i>) [NOTES: [cf also `right as rain, I'll hit him. ...'] [=too bloody right; most certainly] nf OED, EDD]

### right of way
 
 <i>n.</i> -

>  <i>Way (w&amacr.), n. [Add.] Right of way. [Add.] (b) (Pl., RIGHT OF WAYS.) A narrow street; a lane. [Australia] </i> This use of the legal term in a concrete sense originated in Melbourne. In laying out the city, passages ten or twelve feet wide were left between the large rectangular lots. These passages ran from one broad street to another and were intended to afford a <i>right of way</i> between the lots. Hence the term came to be applied to the passages themselves. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2034</i>)

### right out of the book
 
 <i>phr.</i> indicating a `classical formal' nature of something done

>  Free kick be damned ... It was a good forward's try. Right out of the book. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,6</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Gaskell in 1977 N.Z. List Short Stories 6]

### right-of-way
 
 <i>n.</i> a lane, an access-way

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Right-of-way, n. a lane. In England the word indicates a legal right to use a particular passage. In Australia it is used for the passage or lane itself. <i>(1898,Austral English,388</i>)

>  `The main body of the men was located in the right-of-way, which is overlooked by the side windows of the bureau.' <i>(Feb 3, 1893,Argus, The</i>)

>  right n.-right' of way n. a lane. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  Right-of-way n. lane or track. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  right-of-way n. lane, track. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

### right-whale porpoise
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The right-whale porpoise (<i>Lissodelphis peroni</i>) can be at once recognized by the absence of a dorsal fin. It possesses a distinct beak, and about 44 pairs of small pointed teeth. The colouring is distinctive: the back is black, and this colour is sharply marked off from the under-parts, which are white, the line between the two passing above the tail, flippers, and snout. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/140</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

### righto
 
 <i>exclam.</i> used in NZ as elsewhere to express affirmation, agreement, etc; =GOODO(H) spot on; correct

>  `I say Bill, let's have a week off' `Right-o!' <i>(Oct 24, 1903,Bulletin, The (Sydney),8</i>)

>  He's a lovely kid, a righto dinkum oner. <i>(1917,Tiki Talk,8</i>) [NOTES: [ca1920-30 Very frequent in novels: e.g. Rosemary Rees.]]

>  `Righto! Shove them in your valise ...' <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,79</i>)

>  <b>WESTON</b> <i>(1918,Three Years with the NZers,78</i>)

>  `Right-oh!' said Bill, `bring him in [to the bar] and we'll give him a wet.' <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,21</i>)

>  `Come for a walk round the esplanade, Matilda. I can't stand this any longer.' `Right-o. I'll put on my ulster. Isn't it an awful day!' <i>(1920,Stories of Katherine Mansfield, The,193,(1984 Alpers) ed.</i>)

>  <b>GREY</b> `'Right-o,' as these boatmen say,' I replied. <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,53</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> `Why not call me Marigold? ...' `Bless her heart! Why not indeed? Righto.' <i>(1929,Riven,30</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> `O right-o. Only I thought you might like to hear the latest society spice.' <i>(1931,Such Is Life,54</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Shall we?' and he turned to Maisie. She nodded encouragingly. `Right-o!' <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,37</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> `Sh-----' `Right oh.' `Don't let them see you.' <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,121,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Righto, coming out [on strike].' Out of eighty quarry-men, three stayed on the friendly road, and had to take to the bush for the day. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,171</i>)

>  Goodoh and rightoh are used by everyone [in New Zealand and Australia] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `Righto, start up!' Onward, men of the Second New Zealand Expeditionary Force; onward, as our fernleaf badges say, on against the foe; ... <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,17</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> All jokes aside again, he said. `Right-oh then Paul.' <i>(1963,Coal Flat,22</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A form of assent, a token word in parting, a signal to proceed with the next sequence in the operation, or, an acknowledgment that the speaker has been understood. .in 4;`Righto, then I'll leave Jack with you next trip, like you said.' 'I've got everything I wanted, I think. Righto, then, see you next week.' `Wait till I give the word you let the tray go. Righto! Now!' `Righto! We're to leave any cleaning up till later, and load up and get back to the depot. O.K....' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],916</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust. and NZ English [TS] 916]

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Righto. Righto.' [161] The man motioned with his hand. But the curious townspeople who were pressing up against the truck moved back a mere half inch, so he spoke again with quiet, lazy authority. `Come on. Let your mother see the races.' <i>(1965,Outcasts,160</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The words righto and goodo are not confined to Australia and New Zealand but are so prevalent here that they deserve mention. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,135</i>)

>  <b>McDERMOTT</b> `Right oh,' as they say in New Zealand. <i>(1976,How to Get Lost & Found in NZ,165</i>)

>  `Righto, I'll be in touch.' A man of few words, obviously. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,29</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (informal) a person (MP) on the right wing of a political party

 <i>n.</i> patched-up old jeans (from "originals") [US?]
