### riko
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of eel

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>(1926,Tutira,87</i>)

### rill board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> Rill boards: Nine-inch-by-three-inch boards (10 ft long), used when extracting the last of the ore from a shrinkage stope to bring the ore back under the standing sets so that it could be safely handled. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),331</i>)

### rill stope
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> Rill stope: A filled stope worked on a 45-degree angle from one end progressively towards the other. It had the advantage that less shovelling of both quartz and filling was required and the material could be quickly and easily placed on the rill. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),331</i>)

### rilling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [soil erosion] the formation of small gullies in cultivated hillsides.

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> On the more gently sloping land, especially where it is cultivated, puddle erosion, the loss of fine particles in muddy water run-off, and rilling, small gullies in cultivated hillsides, occur. <i>(1961,Agriculture,251</i>)

### rimrod rock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) rock, of an extremely hard type.

>  <b>TAIT</b> The soil is of a pumice formation, running into clay and papa on the lower country where there are outcrops of rimrod rock. <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,159</i>)

### rimu
 
 <i>n.</i> an important native timber tree, especially prized for furniture manufacture. Also called red pine. [RED-PINE (Cook's <i>Spruce</i>) a forest tree, and its timber: see also IMOU Rimu is used in North Island, and in Nelson and Marlborough and Westland. <i>Red-pine</i> has been used chiefly in Central Otago (for the tree) and Southland, and (for the timber) in Otago and Canterbury. <i>Rimu</i> seems to be replacing <i>Red-pine</i> as a tree-name even in the Southern South Island. Rimu is universally known, but not universally used. Morris (1896) remarks that <i>Rimu</i> generally used in North Island, <i>Red-pine</i> more generally in the South.]

>  Totara, Rimu, Matai, and White Pine supplied in any quantities. <i>(Oct 14, 1882,Feilding Star,1</i>)

>  FELLING A GIANT RIMU. <i>(Dec 23, 1908,Otago Witness,37</i>)

>  The Committee recommends the disuse of the terms `red-pine', `white-pine', and `black-pine', in favour of rimu, kahikatea and matai, in order to prevent their being confused with timbers having similar names in Europe <i>(Jul 17, 1896,AJHR,h-24</i>) [NOTES: ) Interim Report No.3 (i) of Timber Conference Appendix Journals H.24]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Rimu, n. Maori name for a New Zealand tree, Dacrydium cupressinum, N.O. Coniferae; also called Red-pine. Rimu is generally used in North Island; Red-pine more generally in the South. See Pine. <i>(1898,Austral English,388</i>)

>  <i>Rii.mu</i> (r&emacr.i.mu),<i> n.</i> [Maori.] (Bot.) A New Zealand coniferous tree <i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>) and its timber; - called also <i>red pine. Mountain rimu (Bot.)</i>, the tree <i>Dacrydium laxifolium. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  The timbers of commercial value are totara, rimu, miro, matai, rata, and kamahi, in mixed bushes; but <i>Fagus fusca</i> and other beeches predominate on the high lands between the lakes and the fiords on the west coast. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,30</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>D[acrydium] cupressinum, Soland. ... Rimu; Red-pine</i>. ... The wood is deep-red, strong, hard, and heavy, but often twisted in grain. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,654</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Rimu (Ree-moo)</i>: Red pine. <i>Ibid.</i> Shingle Short 54 <i>Kauri and Totara, Rimu,</i> and <i>Matai</i> and <i>Maire</i>,/ Red-as-blood <i>Rata</i>, and bright-as-blood <i>Pohutukawa</i>,/ <i>Manuka</i> dark-ey'd, Convolvulus star-ey'd - / The glittering of you that morning! [f@n2 attached to the italicised names] 2. <i>Kauri</i>, etc. (<i>Kow-ree,</i> To[acute]-ta-ra, Ree[e acute]-moo, <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,13</i>)

>  rimu n. a tall tree; the timber of the tree, valuable for cabinet work, etc. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>), Kahikatea or white pine (Podocarpus dacrydioides), rimu or red pine (<i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>), totara (<i>Podocarpus totara</i> and <i>Podocarpus Hallii</i>), Miro (<i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i>), Kaikawaka or mountain cedar (<i>Libocedrus Bidwilli</i>), yellow pine or silver pine (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> and <i>D. intermedium</i>), red and hard beech (<i>Nothofagus fasca</i> and <i>Nothofagus truncata</i>), silver beech (<i>Nothofagus Menziesii</i>), black beech (<i>Nothofagus Solandri</i>), black maire (<i>Olea Cunninghamii</i>), northern and southern rata (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i> and <i>Metrosideros lucida</i>) <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Rimu is the red pine, and for the miro I never heard an English name. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACK</b> Rimu or Red Pine. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,75</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACK</b> Rimu or Red Pine. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,75</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> <i>rimu</i>, a fine tree (Dacrydium cupressinum), red-pine; the young tree, a fountain of weeping foliage considered one of the most beautiful trees in the world. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>)

>  <i> rimu</i>: red pine. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> White pine, pukatea, tawa, rimu, rata, matai, totara, hinau, rewarewa, kamahi, and tree ferns, with some beech on the ridges, were typical of the primary forest. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,16,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> In addition the boys have cut down rimu, totara, matai, and pinus growing on the property and built a 4-stand woolshed without help from outside. <i>(Dec 22, 1954,Shepherd's Calendar,202,1961 ed.</i>)

>  The Maori name of a tall New Zealand pine which yields valuable timber, also called `red pine' (Pronounce .us;reemoo]). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),345</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> I grew to awareness rejecting as alien our scrubby backblocks farm, the thick bush of tall kauri, rimu and kahikatea ..., the mangroved tidal creek, ... the cabbage trees in the playground ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,10,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <i>rimu n</i> -s [Maori] 1 : a tall New Zealand timber tree (<i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>) with a small head, drooping terminal branches covered with tiny keeled linear leaves, and an ovoid terminal nut with a fleshy red receptacle 2 : the wood of the rimu tree used for furniture and general construction - called also imou pine, red pine <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1957</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>rimu</i>: tall, massive tree, 60 ft. to 110 ft. high, found throughout New Zealand; a good timber tree. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Rimu (red pine) .. .. rimu <i>Dacrydium cupressinum </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> New Zealand tree, the red pine. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  rimu ... n. N.Z. tree, red pine. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <i>rimu n</i>. a New Zealand tree. Also called: <i>red pine</i>. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,993</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 658; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 420]]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  ...there's how he was buried alive in pumice, playing the part of a male rimu who died a nasty volcanic death in front of his female rimu lover. <i>(Sep 10, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Inside, the building has been tastefully furnished in figured rimu and smooth white plaster. <i>(Apr 16, 1929,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  OAK OR RIMU EXTENDING DINING TABLE, soundly constructed of Seasoned Timber. <i>(Dec 23, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,9</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> A large Torch is maid [sic] from the Red Pine (Remu) which will last for about 3 hours. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>)

>  <b>CRUSE</b> ... we found ourselves at the edge of a very extensive wood abounding in cowry, and another very fine species of timber called demo; ... <i>(Jul 3, 1820,Journal,122,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1820 July 3 in 1823 Cruise Journal (1957) 122]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ko d|imu d|imu; A certain tree. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,165</i>)

>  <b>McRAE</b> Here we found the trees ... of that kind called by the natives Demo and Totara. The former resembles the pine and the latter has a leaf like the yew tree ... <i>(1820,Journal,20,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Rimu (Dacrydium cupressinum) - This elegant tree comes to its greatest perfection in shaded woods, and in moist rich soil. <i>(1835,Account of NZ, An,40</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Rimu, (cupressinum,) is one of the most graceful trees of the country. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/394</i>)

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> Maree, a fine large tree, produces a mohogany-coloured wood. Rema, a beautiful, high, and majestic tree, growing upwards of 80 feet in length ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,27</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> The Rimu [fn Dacrydium cupressinum] (the most beautiful tree in the world when young) has branches very slender and pendulous, ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,35 and fn,1952 ed.</i>)

>  Remu mentioned by Captain Cook as `Spruce Fir' <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/31/81</i>) [NOTES: (G. Bennett)]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> spg remo ... the prevailing species of trees are remo [rimu], totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of inferior soil.... The bush-like woods on the east side are of white birch, likewise no recommendation of the soil, but the chief objection to the district is, that the prairie soil affords scarcely any food for cattle ... it grows only the wiry tree grass [tussock] ... and Phormium tenax [flax], no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couch grass. <i>(Aug 16, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 16 Tuckett's Diary in 1898 Hocken contributions 223-224]

>  <b>MONRO</b> [rima] In the woods [near Molyneux Bay, Otago] there are not many red [?=totara] and white pines, but a large proportion of rima. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey,249</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 3. Rimu, (Dacrydium cupressimum.) This beautiful tree comes to its greatest perfection in shaded woods and in moist rich soil. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,141</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The leave[s] of the `manuka' (leptospermum scoparium) furnished Captain Cook with a substitute for tea ...; and ... he used the small branches of this shrub and of the tree `rimu', (dacrydium cupressinum,) in equal proportions, to make a decoction from which he brewed spruce beer, frequently referred to by him as a valuable anti-scorbatic. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,128</i>)

>  <b>TORLESSE</b> Kaikatea, (white pine), mahi, black pine, remu, (red pine) <i>(1851,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,6</i>)

>  The timber in [the groves] is chiefly Kaikatea, (white pine), Mahi, (black pine), Remu, (red pine), Pokaka, Kowai, and Totara. ... That in the other forest [ie. not Harewood or Alford] is principally tawai, (black birch), the best timber for boat and ship building, but sometimes unsound: ... <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Dacrydium cupressinum, Don. ... Nat. name, `Rimu' <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 233</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... at work in the forest, sawing up Rimu, i.e. Red Pine, a finely grained timber which literally seemed to bleed under the saw, as its red sap flowed out. <i>(Nov 4, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,45,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Valuable ...furniture planks are sawn from the Rimu, Kakikatae, or white pine (Dacrydium excelsum), Matai, Mairi, and Manuka (Leptospermum scoparium). <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/20,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rimu, Bidwill. Dacrydium laxifolium.. Rimu, Col[enso]. Dacrydium cupressinum. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Red Pine, or Rimu (Dacrydium cupressinum), another valuable building timber, found abundant everywhere; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/III/49 (Buchanan Botany Otago) Rimu. Dacrydium cupressinum, Sol. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  [Native name] Rimu .. [Settlers' name] Red-pine .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  Rimu (red-pine) .. Dacrydium cupressinum .. A strong, handsome, reddish coloured timber, grain beautifully marked and figured; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Beneath the upper canopy of forest trees - such as the <i>rimu</i> pine, the <i>kaikatea</i>, the <i>totara, kahikatoa, rata</i>, and many others ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/245</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Rimu-tree ..... O. Coniferae; G. Dracrydium [sic] S. D. cupressinum. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/303</i>)

>  26 Rimu (red pine) figured slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> The natives here preserved the birds ...in a rimu or sea-weed bag. <i>(1847,Great Journey,1952,53 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Thus prepared the [weka] meat is put into a rimu bag filled up with the fat extracted in cooking. <i>(1940,Rakiura,390</i>) [NOTES: 1850 May Acheron Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 390]



 
 <i>n.</i> a kelp or seaweed (cf. RIMURAPA) [Also called rimurimu [Maori]]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> [Fn] When boiled,** Rimu, a seaweed, [Tutu] forms a jelly which is very palatable. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),139</i>) [NOTES: Check original]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> D|imu, s. Sea weeds. D|imu r|apa; Name of a place. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,150</i>)

>  kelp -lim|moo [rimu] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 113 also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 285]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Rimu - the kelp. It is quite edible if properly treated. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>BUICK</b> The Natives here preserve the birds they catch ... in a rimu or seaweed bag. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,194</i>) [NOTES: [quoting Brunner]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [?to distinguish from MOSS, for which rimu can also be used)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>CAULERPA BROWNII.</i> Sea rimu grows in dense patches at or below low tide level on open rocky coasts. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,8</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Caulerpa brownii</i> var. <i>selaginoides</i> SEA RIMU ... Green patches, often dense, the creeping stems giving rise to numerous more or less upright shoots resembling the foliage of rimu sc. red pine]. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,318</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> [check quot] <i>(1962,Edge of the Alphabet,110</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EDDIE ATTWOOD</b> The most curious Scrub here was the Pigmy Rimu or Alpine Rimu almost like big moss but where it gets support among other scrubs it will grow to a length of perhaps 18 inches. <i>(Apr 15, 2002,Diary</i>) [NOTES: Eddie Attwood's Diary in NZ Memories No. 36, June-July, 2002, p. 33]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KIRK</b> (And) <i>Dacrydium laxifolium</i> mountain rimu <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  (And) <i>Dacrydium laxifolium</i> mountain rimu <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

>  <i>mountain rimu n</i> : a prostrate or suberect shrub (<i>Dacrydium laxifolium</i>) of New Zealand with slender trailing branches <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1478</i>)

### Rimu
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Residual Health Management Unit [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  It appears that hospitals will access extra operating funds through the Residual Health Management Unit, or Rimu as it's known in the trade... It also gives a large number of Rimu, Treasury and health bureaucrats a lot of extra work to do, keeping track of the paperwork. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### rimu stone vein
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a `vein' of gravel growing up with a tree

>  <b>LYON</b> To use such [a well-ground] axe on rimu stone veins, as they were termed, on maire, or on certain hard timbers, meant many hours' grinding, and to `knock a shilling' out of the face of an axe brought one to the soft core. ... <i>(1952,Faring South,182</i>)

### rimu suit
 
 <i>n.</i> a coffin (cf U.S. wooden overcoat)

### rimu-wooded
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MYERS</b> Today, they saw only the dense, rimu-wooded hills, ... <i>(1941,Valiant Love,10</i>)

### rimurapa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> And then there's rimurapa, bullkelp, Durvillea antarctica, a huge, magnificent plant that makes undersea forests round most of the southern coasts. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,61</i>)

>  <b>Greig & Haddad (eds)</b> Tutu juice was also boiled with dried bull kelp (rimurapa) to make a jelly. <i>(2000,NZ Cook's Dictionary, The,00</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> D|imu, s. Sea weeds. D|imu r|apa; Name of a place. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,150</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rimurapa, Col[enso]. D'Urvillea utilis. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  A few also of the sea-weeds were eaten; such as, the Karengo, (A tidal species of <i>Laminaria</i> found plentifully from the East Cape to Cape Turnagain), the Rehia, the Rimurapa <i>(D'Urvillea utilis</i>, and some others ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  [Karengo] together with two other species of sea-algae, <i>rehia</i> and <i>rimurapa</i> (<i>Gigartina</i> and <i>Gracilaria</i> sp.), was mixed with the sweet juice of the <i>tutu ..</i> . <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/29</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Rimu-rapa</i>: A seaweed. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,190</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> The kelp used to make [the Moriori canoe] buoyant was of the <i>Rimurapa</i>, or broad, flat, bull kelp. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,11</i>)

>  The accepted tale that he [sc the Moriori] caulked the seams of his crate-like affair ... with `rim-rap',' bull-kelp, and used inflated bladders if this material ... is, to put it mildly, tosh! <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 363</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 363]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Durvillea antarctica</i> (Cham.) Har. Common name: Bull kelp Maori names: Rimurapa, rimuroa <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,21</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Alternatively, it [tutu juice] was boiled with the pith of the pitau fern or pieces of bull kelp seaweed (<i>rimurapu</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 9 Other edible varieties of seaweed commonly used by the Maori include rimurapu or bull kelp (<i>Durvillea antarctica</i>), which was also dried and then boiled with tutu juice to form a jelly, and the beaded Neptune's necklace (<i>Hormosira banksii</i>), which is palatable raw. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,6</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Durvillea antarctica</i> BULL KELP ... Rimurapa ... Native to: New Zealand. Also right round southern hemisphere except Tasmania and Australia. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,307</i>)

>  [Hdg] Bull kelp. The great bull kelp - rimurapa - with writhing slimy arms up to 10 metres or more long, clothes the coasts of many parts of New Zealand. <i>(Feb 22, 1985,Dominion,9</i>)

### rimurimu
 
 <i>n.</i> a moss

>  The material used among the Tuhow natives ... as a menstruating diaper is a variety of moss (generic term <i>rimurimu</i>) known as <i>kohukohu</i> or <i>angiangi</i>. It is probably <i>Hypnum clandestinus. </i> <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/90</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> seaweed <i>Caulerpa brownii</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Rock pools ... are the favourite haunt of several species of <i>Caulerpa</i>, much the most widely distributed and commonest member being the RIMU-RIMU (<i>C. brownii</i>) thought confined to areas south of Castle Point <i>(1961,Flora NZ,8,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> The finer rimurimu was in the still parts of the lagoons and we would feel round for it with our feet and hands, and pick it and put it into our sugar bags. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,43</i>) [NOTES: From Patricia Grace (1975) : Waiariki]

### rimuroa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Neptune's necklace (again, just 'rimu') is tangy when you nibble it, and so are the young tips of rimuroa (bladderweed). <i>(1989,Homeplaces,61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> The Rimuroa is the bluebell. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,502</i>)

### ring
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> It was risky swimming them over the rivers. If the cattle fail to make for the opposite bank the leaders may turn back and start `ringing' in midstream, the cattle in the middle of the ring being forced under water and drowned. At first it is difficult to get a clean swim-over; but the mob soon gets used to it. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,66,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> To drive stock to Auckland it was necessary to cross the river twice ... The danger of swimming the stock across was that they might `ring', that is swim round and round in the centre of the water till exhausted and then drown. <i>(1963,Armed Settlers,198</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to circle in a gathered mob around a stationary centre.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> If the cattle fail to make for the opposite bank the leaders may turn back and start 'ringing' in midstream, the cattle in the middle of the ring being forced under water and drowned. <i>(1875,Adventures in Maoriland,66</i>)

>  The centre of the mob may stand stand still or move slowly forward; but the outside sheep gallop round and round them. This is called ringing. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  The sheep were ringed in 5min 54sec, but got out before the minute was up. <i>(Mar 21, 1907,Star, The,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> To resemble or pass for another, Leverage, 1925: U.S.A.: since ca. 1918. Prob. ex Australian s. be the dead ring of, `to be a person's double'. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,569,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GORDON</b> Pelters: make the arse-cuts, a cut around the ring so that it comes away from the inside. This is also called ringing or arsecutting (ringers and arse-cutters for men who do them). <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Colin Houlbrooke recalled the two main areas for battle with marbles as being the sealed area for 'rings' and the dirt behind the shed for 'holes'. <i>(1987,Alfredton,75</i>) [NOTES: Refers to c1935]

>  <b>HISLOP</b> I suppose at that time [c1860s goldrush] I would be more interested in grabbing my `dumps' out of the ring at school, when some quick eye had noticed that a young pointer had tried to get away with placing a stinkie or a chalkie into play with the rest of his marbles. The `Grab your dumps' was the roar, as we all rushed in for our own, and anybody else's one could get. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> constr. THE RING [(prisoners')]

>  <b>HYDE</b> The Ring is the second-best institution in New Zealand Gaols, the best being the Dummy. The Ring is in the prison yard and about thirty feet in diameter. The game is to try and find the end of it; but you never do, although you are marched around it from eleven in the morning until five o'clock. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,57</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to ringbark

>  During these two months, July and August, let them `ring' the trees, that is cut out a ring of bark and sap 3 or 4 inches wide round all the trees they intend to cut down or `fall' during the next 12 months. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/186</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Ring, v. (1) To cut the bark of a tree round the trunk so as to kill it. The word is common in the same sense in English forestry and horticulture, and only seems Australasian from its more frequent use, owing to the widespead practice of clearing the primeval forests and generally destroying trees. `Ringed' is the correct past participle, but `rung' is now commonly used. [1846] ... (2) to make cattle move in a circle. [1874] ... (3) To move round in a circle [1884] ... (4) To make the top score at a shearing-shed.' See Ringer. <i>(1898,Austral English,388</i>)

>  ring v.t. to kill a tree by cutting the bark round the trunk; to make cattle move in a circle;-v.i. to move in a circle, as cattle or birds; to win in a sheep-shearing contest. -ring'er n. the man who shears most sheep in a day. - ring'-bark v.t. to ring a tree.-ring'-neck n. a newly arrived immigrant, esp. from England; a jackaroo <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to outshear (other shearers), have the highest tally of shorn sheep, usu. in phrase ring the shed or ring the board (cf RINGER n)

>  <b>PATERSON</b> `The man that `rung' the Tubbo shed is not the ringer here.' <i>(1896,Man From Snowy River, The,136</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>(1898,Austral English</i>) [NOTES: see @v1 above to be the RINGER in (Pa. tense rang)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> the terms to ring a shed and to be a ringer, i.e. to be the fastest and most expert shearer in a shed, have been used for so many generations that they are quite orthodox in the Antipodes. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,38</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Jacky Howe.- Sleeveless singlet, cut nearly to the waist under the arms. Named after the famous Jack Howe who rang many of the South Island sheds in the 1890s. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,372</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> The fastest man is called the ringer and there is considerable prestige attached to it, besides the extra profit. Sometimes a man will `ring the shed' in the wethers by sheer courage or brute strength, but when the ewes come in with their softer and less bulky wool, some- one else may come to the fore and displace him. <i>(1951,High Country,29</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> RUNG THE SHED: The shearer with the highest aggregate tally, is said to have rung the shed. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> 6 [Young shearers should] take notice of the man who shears in a quiet, business-like way, and, ... [by] the end of the day rings them [his mates] by quite a few sheep - ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> ... many a tense drama was enacted as individuals vied ... for the honour of `ringing the shed'. For a shearer to be able to say that he `rang the board' at so-and-so station the previous year was as good a testimonial as he could wish for. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,21</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing) [See quot ringer n.]]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> On the shearing board of Otupae,/ Shore six of the world's best,/ And with the best of kai, you bet,/ ... Starting from the end pen,/ Billy Richards rang the board./ <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,98</i>)

>  ring: To outdo all other shearers by `ringing the board' or `ringing the shed'. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/27</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>(1898,Austral English</i>) [NOTES: see @v1 above]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Merino sheep on the flat, especially when coming to a gate, are apt to circle. The centre of the mob may stand still, or move slowly forward; but the outside sheep gallop round and round them. This is called ringing. It is annoying to see the sheep racing past the gate instead of going through it, but the habit may be turned to the shepherd's advantage. By starting the sheep on one side ringing the reverse way, two streams of sheep can be made to meet at the gate, and carry one another through. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of a sheepdog) to turn in a complete circle around the sheep and come back to the handler; to stop after its out-run [sheep farming]

>  Ringing Sheep: Fault in a dog where it goes in a complete circle around the sheep and comes back to the handler. It fails to stop after its out-run, halfway around the sheep, and directly opposite the handler. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,25</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area of a dog trial course in which sheep are yarded.

>  Pulled straight and true and put in some fine work at the ring. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,950</i>)

>  The second dog, Sweep, ran out in a good cast to the right and took charge well. Under very good command, held his sheep well in the ring and yarded in 8 1/2 minutes. <i>(1923,NZ Farmer,951</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> On his way to complete the run by getting a balanced hold in the ring, Pauline's Boy ran rather wide ... <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Anyhow, to give you the fair dinkum guts I put across a beauty when I found the double-headed penny in the ring, and that's how I won 200 francs. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> [(criminals' slang) sc a car, to change identification. (or from RING IN?)]

>  `Ringed' cars and stolen property worth in total more than $25,000 was recovered by the Porirua CIB yesterday. ... He said the operation, which was not yet complete, began about a month ago when a `ringed' (changed indentification) Honda Accord stolen from Wellington in 1981 was found during police inquiries in Porirua. ... Mr Reeve said the arrested man also admitted `ringing' another car, which was stolen in 1980. <i>(Oct 31, 1984,Evening Post,3</i>)

### ring bark
 
 <i>v.</i> to cut away a ring of bark from around a trunk in order to kill a tree [aka ring-bark, ringbark]

>  THE WEEKLY NEWS .. CONTENTS FOR THIS WEEK.. To Ring-bark Trees ... <i>(May 22, 1875,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The idea is to ringbark the trees and bore through the trunk with an augur a couple of holes which are filled with a composition and plugged. The composition soaks into the wood, rots it, and causes it to become inflammable. In about eight or ten months a match is applied, and the tree falls, the stump and roots which have become soaked with the mixture, being burned right out. <i>(Jul 27, 1904,Tuapeka Times,1</i>)

>  The dead tree by the gas works has been cut down, and the gentleman who ring-barked it for impeding his zig-zag progress can now absorb his whisky with a comparatively easy mind. <i>(Feb 20, 1893,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  Trees thus treated invariably die out as if ring barked, and hitherto we have heard of no remedy which has been successful in bringing them back to life by renewing the lost skin. <i>(Apr 6, 1887,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  <b>MacDONALD</b> `The selector in a timbered country, without troubling himself anout cause and effect, is aware that if he destroys the tree the grass will grow, and therefore he `ring-barks' his timber.' <i>(1888,Gum Boughs,204</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Ring-bark, v. Same meaning as Ring (1). <i>(1898,Austral English,389</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> And so, instead of felling these forest giants, he simply selected those parts where the underscrub was lightest, ring-barked the trees, and when they were dead ... burnt out a farm from the stubborn wilderness of forest around him. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,13</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> And so, instead of felling these forest giants, he simply selected those parts where the underscrub was lightest, ring-barked the trees, and when they were dead ... burnt out a farm from the stubborn wilderness of forest around him. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,13</i>)

>  Firewood was procured [by the Moriori] by ring-barking trees and waiting until some gale overthrew the tree at the ring-bark rot. <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 366</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 366]

>  Ringi.barki. (r&itilde.ngi.ba.rki.), v. t. To make a ring around by cutting away the bark; to girdle; to ring; as, to ringbark a tree. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> [figurative; aka ring-bark, ringbark]

>  A New chum Emerald Isler, travelling through some ring-barked country, couldn't make out why some of the trees were "all out in blossom, while the others didn't have the devil of a leaf on 'em at all bedad." <i>(May 12, 1883,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  Secularism in the school has ringbarked morality, the shade of which is vanished and its verdure dead. <i>(Aug 10, 1894,NZ Tablet,6</i>)

>  Which is it better, to ringbark thistles on Sunday, or send a bad one pound note to Peter Campbell or the heathen? <i>(Apr 11, 1883,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Only to be quite safe he should ring-bark the lamp-post at the corner, as a lamp-post, even when the lamp is alight, is a serious menace to the safety of gentlemen who have approached the "cant't-see-a hole through-a-ladder" [sic] stage of obfuscation. <i>(Feb 20, 1893,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  .. or, if [the hair] were too stiff and strong, to ring-bark and use a cross-cut saw. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/1/1</i>)

>  ... barren, ring-barked hills. <i>(Mar 17, 1906,NZ Truth,39/4/6</i>) [NOTES: (but cf Morris 1893 Austr quot: `We were going through ring-barked country ...')]

### ring crutch
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>COOP</b> With August shearing it is customary and generally advisable to `ring-crutch' or `bullseye' the ewes before putting the rams out at tupping. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,28</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> As we completed shearing each mob of ewes, we would follow on by ring crutching their lambs. <i>Ibid.</i> As tupping season was approaching I also gave them a light ringcrutch (a honeymoon shear) before turning them out into a larger paddock <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,98</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to take a small circle of wool from around a sheep's rear end. (See [buttonhole](../B#buttonhole),[bullseye](../B#bullseye),[tup crutch](../T#tup-crutch))

>  <b>COOP</b> With August shearing it is customary and generally advisable to 'ring-crutch' or 'bullseye' the ewes before putting the rams out at tupping .. in those cases where the ewes were normally ring-crutched at tupping and fully crutched in June, the latter operation is eliminated. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,28</i>)

>  One man can ringcrutch approximately 200 per hour. <i>(Jan 27, 1977,NZ Farmer,70</i>)

>  One man can ring crutch approximately 200 per hour ... <i>(May 1981,NZ Journal of Agriculture,79</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> As we completed shearing each mob of ewes, we would follow on by ring crutching their lambs. [ring; ring-crutch; ringcrutch] <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,98</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the removal of a small ring of wool from a sheep's rear end.

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> We continued home with the ewes which we were going to give a ring crutch. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,201</i>)

### ring crutching
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the removal of a small area of wool at a sheep's rear end.

>  The job may be small, such as ring crutching, but those heavy sheep have to be handled. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture,49</i>)

### ring dollar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> In New South Wales, in 1813, this difficulty [of shortage of coin] became so acute that Governor Macquarie, in an effort to check the outflow of Spanish dollars, then the main circulating medium, caused the centres of these coins to be struck out, the centre-piece (about the size of a shilling) being stamped NEW SOUTH WALES, 1813, around a central crown, and on the other side FIFTEEN PENCE, the remainder or `ring' part of the dollar being impressed around the new inner rim with NEW SOUTH WALES, 1813 on one side, and FIVE SHILLINGS on the other. ... [67] 3,008 Dollars (mainly Holey or Ring Dollars) [pounds] 752 0 0/ 725 Dumps (centres of Spanish Dollars) 45 6 3 <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,66</i>)

### ring fence
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to fence off an area of land [originally used to form a boundary between neighbouring landowners.] [aka ring-fence]

>  I understand Mr S. Daniells is going to ring fence his farm. <i>(May 18, 1893,Otago Witness,22</i>)

>  All Highly Improved, ring fenced, and subdivided into 10 paddocks; also Holding Paddocks. <i>(Aug 21, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  This could include ring-fencing ownership of the mainframes themselves by outsourcing them to a third party. <i>(Nov 3, 2003,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

>  Mrs E. P. Fisher wrote stating that she would accept GBP35 per acre for the cottage site on her property, the Council to bear all expenses and ring fence the section. <i>(Jun 14, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)

>  The clearing of forest-lands, ring-fencing and grassing them, will cost about [pounds]3 ... per acre. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,486</i>)

>  <b>WEBB</b> The section we were working on was nominally mine; that is, I was the lucky one who drew it in the land ballot. ... We had got it ring-fenced, because ... the drawers of the adjoining sections had done their share towards that, ... <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,98</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> The first job of the purchaser [of a run], that of ring fencing his whole property could not be done quickly or cheaply. <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,15</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> As soon as possible after sowing, the burn was ring-fenced and later subdivided. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,245,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> ... he decided to take up the other section `as forty pounds was sufficient at slump prices to buy wire for ring-fencing'. ... He then ring-fenced the new land, ramming as many as fifty posts into position in one day ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,93</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The property was ring-fenced and divided into nine paddocks. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,164</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> An area was ring-fenced. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,487</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> As soon as possible after sowing, the burn was ring-fenced and later sub-divided. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,245</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> .. the top [of a stump] yielded valuable battens to ring fence the farm. <i>(1993,Colonial Outcasts: A Search for the Remittance Man,139</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to contain an object, funding or person. [aka ring-fence]

>  MNZ [Merino New Zealand] Chairman John Perriam says it is essential that the monetary reserves held by the Wool Board be 'ring-fenced.' <i>(Dec 7, 2000,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

>  Ms King said she believed that there had been a misunderstanding of what should be ring-fenced, rather than any deliberate misuse of the money .. from the point of view of the Government they have always been very clear that that money was ring-fenced. [ring-fence] <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence that surrounds an area of land for a specific purpose or a boundary fence. [aka ring-fence]

>  With the exception of about 30 acres of bush, this property is all good fern land with a quantity of natural and artificial grasses growing amongst it; there is some fencing erected thereon, and with the aid of the river and stream boundaries, very little more fencing is required to complete the whole in a ring fence. <i>(Dec 1, 1866,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  FREEHOLD SHEEP FARM FOR SALE. .. Containing 6067 acres Freehold and 700 acres Leasehold, enclosed within a ring fence ... <i>(Mar 1, 1873,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  Mr. St. George will sell about 20 Acres ( more or less) of Excellent Land, situated on the BELL BLOCK, price GBP60. Or will Let it on a Term, for a nominal rental, upon consideration that the Tenant will erect a ring fence, consisting of a Ditch and a two-feet bank, planted with furze, with a Rail on the top. <i>(Sep 7, 1853,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  They were living on their purchases, which had a sort of ring fence round them, but as far as witness knew the sheep on the section were Smith and Gellibrand's. <i>(Jun 9, 1883,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  A writer in one of the Auckland papers appears to think that native reserves under the new arrangements will be a source of annoyance; and if he is correct in thinking that they are not to be merely so much land purchased beyond the wants of the purchaser, outside his ring fence, but that they are to be picked bits among his other fields, they certainly will be a nuisance of the first order. <i>(Nov 16, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  <b>A Tramp</b> A ring fence has been placed round the property at the snow-line. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,54</i>)

>  The lessee shall, within two years from the date of his lease, have the land fenced with a ring-fence. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,77</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> When he got his first sheep the only fences were known as 'ring' fences. There was one ring fence around the whole farm. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,283</i>)

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> We put up three-quarters of a mile of this fencing. It was not a ring fence, but it stopped the stock going back into the bush on the south side of the mountain <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,166</i>)

>  Incomplete ring fence; back line fenced. <i>(Jul 4, 1917,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> A ring fence had been placed round the property at the snow-line <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,54</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> RING FENCE: Single fence surrounding a paddock or other enclosure. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,188</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 188]

>  (1.) The lessee shall, within two years from the date of his lease, have the land fenced with a ring-fence; and such fence shall be a sufficient fence within the meaning of `The Fencing Act, 1895.' (Vide `Fencing Act.') <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,77</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> When he got his first sheep the only fences were known as `ring' fences. There was one ring fence round the whole farm. The ring fences were put round the turnip and oat fields. All the rest was one paddock. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,283</i>)

### ring fern
 
 <i>n.</i> = hard fern, <i>Paesia scaberula</i>

>  Although I crush carpet fern in my pastures by feeding out hay to cows during the winter, the fern still comes away in the spring. Total elimination of carpet fern (better known as hard or ring fern) under high rainfall conditions even under topdressing and stocking is not easy. <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,13</i>)

>  <b>EASY</b> A spark would land on a clump of manuka or a patch of ring fern and instantly a fire would spring up. <i>(1996,Looking Backwards,49</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The sweet fern, (also known as hard fern, ring fern and carpet fern) was burned and grass sown on about 250 acres of the hardiest country. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,136</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> HARD FERN (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>), called also pig fern, ring fern, and silver fern, is abundant in both islands, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,19,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Paesia scaberula</i> (hard fern, ring fern, scented fern, carpet fern) is considered one of the most troublesome weeds of the wetter hill farms of New Zealand. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/113B</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Attuned to the geographical region were some particularly troublesome growths, and of these, the hard fern (or ring fern) became a special pest difficult to control and to guard against. ... This hard fern and piripiri were greatly encouraged by set grazing with sheep, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,15,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>ring fern [s] Paesia scaberula</i> (A. Rich.) Kuhn <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,64</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One of the worst of the regrowth problems on this country [near Taumaranui] is the hard fern, or `ring' fern as it is sometimes known. ...Ring fern ... got the name because after burning the regrowth came up in the form of a circle outside the burnout area. <i>(1972,Wake of The Axe,112</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Paesia scaberula</i> LACE FERN ... Ring fern ... Scented fern ... Fronds yellow-green ... strongly scented in hot sunshine ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,428</i>)

### ring in
 
 <i>v.</i> to enter (a horse) for a race under a false name (cf RINGING-IN) [racing slang]

>  But the manipulation of these races has now come to such a pass that local people have very little to do with them, and it is generally found that there are three or four racehorses "rung-in" under false names by the "clever" division, and the public are swindled. <i>(Jan 16, 1886,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The Greymouth Trotting Club also wrote <i>re</i> the "ringing-in" of a certain horse in that district, stating that the club had authorized its secretary to proceed to the North Island with the object of identifying an animal alleged to have been "rung-in" at Greymouth; also suggesting that the association might financially assist the club in its efforts to stop "ringing-in." <i>(Apr 15, 1897,Otago Witness,32</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> He was not `ringing in' the horse. <i>(1924,Heather of the South,90</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> When somebody or other rings in a racehorse even in those days, takes it to Australia with painted fetlocks ... <i>(1962,Hot Water Sailor,59,1981 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to enter into a two-up game [(two up)]

>  <b>REES</b> The fly-manager rings in at a `two-up' school. <i>(1913,Merry Marauders,71</i>)

### ring in a grey
 
 <i>phr.</i> to use an illegal double-headed coin in a two-up game

>  ... too often, he falls a ready victim to the crook and speiler [sic] who is adept in the art of `ringing in a grey'; i.e., using a double-headed penny, and resorting to other devious, and dirty dodges. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

### ring it on to
 
 <i>v.</i> [(?criminals')] (See [cf. to ring in](../T#to-ring-in))

>  This sort of excuses make his Worship tired. `The same old cold,' he remarked. `It is no use ringing that kind of stuff on to me.' <i>(Jan 6, 1912,NZ Truth,341/7/2</i>)

>  After giving the [counterfeit] coin to the cabby he (Croul) repented, and wanted to get it back, but Watson said, `Never mind, it's all right; he'll ring it on to someone else.' <i>(Mar 16, 1912,NZ Truth,351/6/3</i>)

### ring off
 
 <i>v.</i> (transf.) shut up (as imperative)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1940,Man & His Wife, A,27</i>)

### ring the board
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [shearing] to have the highest tally of shorn sheep in a gang or in a particular shed.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Lew was ringing the board by about twenty sheep ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,41</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> A claim to have 'rung the board' at such and such a station last season would be a more influential testimonial than a character reference signed by Angel Gabriel. <i>(1966,Sixty Million Muster,75</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Potae and Horton rang the board with a remarkable tally of 529 each. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,133</i>)

### ring the double
 
 <i>phr.</i> to commit bigamy (?f. a two-up term)

>  Witness then went on to give the itinerary of life's journey up till the time when Nellie had `rung the double' in the marriage line at Parnell. <i>(Nov 30, 1912,NZ Truth,388/6/2</i>)

### ring the sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [heading dogs] to circle a mob of sheep slowly before working them.

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. the dog trotted right up beside the sheep, walked right out in front and continued on round to return to the rear. This is known as 'ringing the sheep' and, out in the open, would cost several points. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,205</i>)

### ring-bark
 
 <i>v.</i> [passive form]

>  [Caption] Puriris have been ring-barked by the cattle in their search for food, and the station management has taken the precaution of painting trees with creosote to prevent their wholesale destruction by the animals. <i>(May 7, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

### ring-fenced
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  FREEHOLD PROPERTY, NORTH TUAKITOTO. .. This property is intersected by the Main South Road, and the whole is ring fenced. <i>(Apr 18, 1874,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  SHEEP FARM. 844 ACRES. 685 acres freehold, 159 acres l i.p., 650 acres in grass, balance in bush, ring fenced, subdivided into six paddocks, carrying capacity 1 sheep and 80 head cattle. <i>(Aug 27, 1906,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  acres in grass and turnips, ring fenced; .. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/26,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1930 May 22 Na To Hoa Aroha (1987) (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 2/26 (Ngata) 280]

>  <b>HISLOP</b> There are plenty of [hares] in that ring-fenced paddock, and as they can't get out of there you should have a lot of fun; .. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,73</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [joc.] [of a person] kept at home.

>  Of a spouse, to be ring-fenced: to be kept at home. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of funding, products or organisations] isolated from others for a specific purpose.

>  One [clear option] was for the reserves to be returned to farmers, the other being for money to be vested in a 'tightly ring-fenced' trust fund. <i>(Sep 3, 2001,Rural News,05</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of an area] surrounded by a fence for a particular reason e.g. a boundary between neighbours.

>  <b>HISLOP</b> There are plenty .. in that ring-fenced paddock, and as they can't get out of there you should have a lot of fun. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,73</i>)

>  Sheep Farm: Good sheep-proof fences, ring-fenced with concrete, totara posts. <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,69</i>)

### ring-fencing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMAS</b> ... he decided to take up the other section `as forty pounds was sufficient at slump prices to buy wire for ring-fencing'. ... He then ring-fenced the new land, ramming as many as fifty posts into position in one day ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,93</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the enclosing of an area with a fence or railings.

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Around 1918 the Bryan brothers did a ring-fencing job for Dick Goodwin on a 100 acre block of recently chopped bush. <i>(1990,Bryans, The: At Home on the Farm,37</i>)

### ring-in
 
 <i>n.</i> a substitute; an outsider

>  ring in n. entering a disguised horse, dog or athlete in competitive sport <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  ring in ... ~ n. ring-in. 3. Austral. & N.Z. sl. a person or thing that is not normally a member of a particular group; outsider. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,994</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 420]]

### ring-in/ringer
 
 <i>n.</i> a fraudulently substituted racehorse

>  There is one thing in connection with trotting I cannot understand, and that is the lack of interest shown in some clubs, epecially in the south, to stamp out the "ringer." <i>(May 21, 1896,Otago Witness,34</i>)

>  THERE is an impression in some circles that the days of the "ringer" are not passed in New Zealand, and keen eyes are being kept on certain horses produced. <i>(Mar 13, 1930,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  As Mrs Hedley and the "old prad" are also out for life, the "ring in" did not do the crowd behind the ex-New Zealander, Rawmire, much, if any, good, when that gelding won. <i>(Feb 22, 1908,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  <b>Scott</b> He fumed at being beaten .... winding up his vituperation by saying that [the racehorse] was a ringer from Australia. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,222</i>)

### ring-keeper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE</b> "Come on, come on, come on!" the ring-keeper would yell as he gave the new chance to spin to the biggest better. <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,99</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> One man takes the kip and places two pennies on it ... He hands [his bet] to the ring-keeper. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,162</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> I'll admit I was stiff when I lost that fifty francs, but my cobber produced another ten; and when the ring-keeper said `Up and do 'em', I collected 200 francs. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

### ring-master
 
 <i>n.</i> [(two-up)]

>  Of course, there are [two-up] `schools' here and there, and only a `head', in other words `a true sport', knows their locale. Once upon a time the free and easy `school' with the `ring-master,' and his `All set - a fair go' broke upon the stilly night. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

### ring-neck
 
 <i>n.</i> a white collar worker

>  You fight among yourselves till your ranks are broken through./ By carping critic `ring-necks' and non-union alienage. ... Get rid of the Union `cuckoo' and the `stool pigeon' as well / The `arrear fiends' and the `ring-necks' ' <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,28</i>)

### ring-raced
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [dairy farms] furnished with a race that encircles the perimeter of a farm, adjoining most paddocks.

>  102 hectare recently converted dairy unit. Mainly flat with balance easy rolling. Ring-raced to 57 paddocks. <i>(Nov 18, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,40</i>)

>  The farm is also ring-raced to enable feeding to occur at the same time as milking. <i>(Sep 20, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### ring-tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JENKIN</b> He walked over to the dressing table where Mrs. Elworthy kept her rings on a ring-tree. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,77</i>)

### ringa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] The arm or hand ... [New-Zeeland] Ringa [i-macron, a-grave] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

### ringa-ring
 
 <i>v.</i> shake hands

>  <b>WEEKES</b> The natives here [in Taranaki] are in fear of their old enemies the `Wicatus' coming, but it appears their visit is intended for a friendly one, or to ringa-ring (shake hands). <i>Ibid.</i> t 68 They [Maoris] were very civil, indeed the habit of shaking hands with every one became at last disagreeable, for if you met them a dozen times a day the ringa-ring must not be omitted, ... <i>(May 2, 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 May 2 Weekes Journal in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 49]

### ringanui
 
 <i>n.</i> a groundwater crustacean [ORIGIN: Maori, The name <i>Ringanui</i> is derived from the Maori words <i>ringa</i> (hand) and <i>nui</i> (big) in reference to the very large gnathopod 1 characteristic of this genus. **The name <i>toonuiiti</i> is derived from Maori <i>toonui</i> (thumb) and <i>iti</i> (little) and refers to the smaller robust seta defining the proximal end of the gnathopod 1 palm... **The name <i>koonuiroa</i> is derived from Maori <i>koonui</i> (thumb) and <i>roa</i> (long) and refers to the very large robust seta defining the proximal end of the gnathopod 1 palm...]

>  The new genus, Ringanui, is a significant step in resolving this confusion. Ringanui differs from Paraleptamphopus on several characters and is most easily recognised by diverse gnathopods, the first of which is enlarged relative to gnathopod 2. The two species are described and illustrated in detail. They differ in size and gnathopod characteristics, but other differences are small. Ringanui koonuiroa is the smaller species and is most easily recognised by the single, very long robust seta defining the palm of gnathopod 1. The second species, R. toonuiiti, is the larger, but its palmar defining spine is relatively short. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.mapress.com/zootaxa/2006f/z01148p025f.pdf Accessed on 14/03 2006]

### ringaringa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rengarenga](../R#rengarenga))

### Ringatu
 
 <i>adj.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  His [Te Kooti's] followers state that he received visions from on high, and here he laid the foundations of the Ringatu Church. <i>(Feb 1, 1933,NZ Tablet,15</i>)

>  He doubts if there are 70 non-Waitotara district natives present, and of the strangers there are some Wanganui and Wangaehu, probably 40, and a few of what are known as the ringatu natives, from Urewera district, some of Te Kooti's old people. <i>(Jan 6, 1897,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>4</b> There were Anglican, Roman Catholic, Mormon, Wesleyan and Ringatu services, and everywhere the singing could be heard. <i>(Dec 30, 1911,Poverty Bay Herald</i>)

>  I thought at first that the Ringatu Church meant a church at Ringatu. If it means a religion I think we ought to pause before we endow a faith which may, for all we know, be most pernicious. <i>(Feb 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

>  Many of the Maoris are followers of the Ringatu religion started by Te Kooti, the prophet, last century. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,55</i>)

>  Ringatu draws on the Old Testament; Rua Kenana, who claimed to be son of Te Kooti and the new Messiah, turned to the New Testament. <i>(2005,AA Directions,Autumn p22</i>)

>  [Caption] The famous temple of Rua, the Ringatu prophet. <i>(May 6, 1950,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

>  Nearly all these people were Ringatus... Not many people know much about the Ringatu faith... I knew that Ringatu was the name for some sort of religion, for on Sundays the people gathered at the carved meeting house which was the Ringatu church and chanted long karakias, guided by one who was their Ringatu Minister. <i>(Apr 20, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,42</i>)

>  At the native meeting up the river the diversity of religions caused some discussion. There are four religions altogether, Protestant (Church of England), Catholic, Salvation Army, and Te Kooti's religion (Ringatu). The latter is a bugbear to some of the natives, particularly the Catholics, but the meeting decided on freetrade in this matter, the main argument being that as Englishmen had hundreds of religions there was no reason why the Maoris should not have at least four. .. The term Ringatu is, we understand, derived from the fact that in place of saying "Amen," as usual in other services, they say "Glory." <i>(Sep 16, 1889,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The 1870-72 Urewera expeditions were driven by a vengeful Colonial Government (also known as the Kawanatanga), desperate to track down and destroy Te Kooti Arikirangi Te Turuki and his Ringatu guerilla force then regrouping within the boundaries of the Tuhoe tribe's forested rohe. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  When she returned here [from treatment in a Pakeha hospital] I heard an old woman ask her, `In what state are you now?' (i.e., `Have you deserted our ringa tu religion, are you noa?' <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/87</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Ringatus always begin and end each prayer by raising their hands with the fingers extended. <i>(Feb 1, 1933,NZ Tablet,15</i>)

>  At the native meeting up the river the diversity of religions caused some discussion. There are four religions altogether, Protestant (Church of England), Catholic, Salvation Army, and Te Kooti's religion (Ringatu). The latter is a bugbear to some of the natives, particularly the Catholics, but the meeting decided on freetrade in this matter, the main argument being that as Englishmen had hundreds of religions there was no reason why the Maoris should not have at least four. .. The term Ringatu is, we understand, derived from the fact that in place of saying "Amen," as usual in other services, they say "Glory." <i>(Sep 16, 1889,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The Missionaries, the Mormons, the Ringatu, and the others, all worshipped the God of the Bible in slightly different ways. <i>(Feb 25, 1887,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  I thought at first that the Ringatu Church meant a church at Ringatu. If it means a religion I think we ought to pause before we endow a faith which may, for all we know, be most pernicious. <i>(Feb 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

>  That's describing the place as it was in the early 1900s, when it was basically a Maori-speaking Ringatu community that didn't have a cash economy at all, just lived off the land, growing their own fruit and vegetables, and taking fish out of the estuary and out of the sea. <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Te Kooti and his main force of Ringatu managed to elude the Kawanatanga. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### Ringatuism
 
 <i>n.</i> [Some tribes have the marae instinct and a psychology that influences the collection of food supplies. Such is Waikato. And you will find the same condition where cults like Ringatuism prevail]

<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> circumcised

### ringbolt
 
 <i>n.</i> a non-paid trip on a ship aided by one or more of the crew;a clandestine passage aided by one or more of the crew;(used by shipgirls, escapees, etc.) [?f. a play on ring `vagina; anus' and bolt `run for it'; or from a play on ringbolt cf Golden rivet; or mere elaboration on bolt

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Because Mata was a seaman, he knew he'd have no trouble getting a ringbolt or a stowaway back to the Islands. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,192</i>)

>  She went to Australia as a "ring bolt", a stowaway, on the <i>Monowai</i>. <i>(Jul 1991,Metro,94</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> ... they would go possum-trapping, ... or maybe even get a whitebait trench down on the West Coast, or do a ringbolt on [16] a ship somewhere. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,15</i>)

>  to ringbolt: to stow away <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> RINGBOLT (n) Clandestine voyage by sea used by escapees. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,253</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> ringbolt stow away, with crew connivance. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

>  Officially Hinckesman was a stowaway, but the Auckland Star said he had done a `ringbolt', paying a crew member to hide him during the voyage. <i>(Jan 11, 1989,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>NORGROVE</b> Maybe she's scared he'll do a ringbolt and take off. <i>(1987,Shoestring Sailors,48</i>) [NOTES: Of a girlfriend scared of losing her boyfriend: used as a synonym of bolt.]

>  <b>NORGROVE</b> Maybe she's scared he'll do a ringbolt and take off. <i>(1987,Shoestring Sailors,48</i>) [NOTES: Of a girlfriend scared of losing her boyfriend: used as a synonym of bolt.]

### ringbolting

>  He vanished ... possibly using a technique known as `ring bolting', catching an illegal passage on a crew member. <i>(Jul 10, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A10</i>)

### ringed dosinia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dosinia anus</i>
