>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Ringed dosinia .. .. tuangi-haruru .. .. <i>Dosinia anus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

### ringer
 
 <i>n.</i> the shearer with the highest tally in a gang, or a shearing-shed; the fastest shearer in a shearing gang, who usually shears on the stand closest to the wool table

>  M'Kay, who was "ringer", put up a decent record with 111 sheep in four hours. <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  Robert Parker has even suggested that [this wine] is good enough to be used as a ringer in a tasting of top Burgundies. <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Dominion,30</i>)

>  Fastest shearer in a shearing gang who usually shears on the stand closest to the wool table. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,58</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> All sorts of characters used to gather there, hunters and miners, ringers from outback stations. <i>(1986,Address to a King,59</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> For some time he had known that his reign as `ringer' would soon be over. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,76</i>)

>  ringer ... NZ & Austral. fastest shearer in a shed. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,658</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 994; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 420]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Ringer. The fastest shearer in a woolshed.The ringer is said to `ring the shed' by shearing the highest tally. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Ringer. The fastest shearer in a woolshed.The ringer is said to `ring the shed' by shearing the highest tally. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies</i>)

>  ringer: The fastest shearer in the shed over the period of shearing, and different from a `gun'. The ringer could be the best of several gun shearers in the same shed, but he could also be a ringer in a weak team and not even be a gun. A possible origin of the term can be found under `quoits' but others are that the ringer `runs rings round the rest' and the ringer in Yorkshire dialect means anyone superlatively good. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/27</i>) [NOTES: Wallace Shearing Glossary-]

>  Ringi.er (-&etilde.r), n. [See RING, v. t., 4.] [Add.] 3. One who makes the highest score in shearing sheep. [Australian bush cant] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  He's often the ringer and even though he's the best on the board, he takes time to give the younger men advice on their blade-shearing techniques. <i>(Jul 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  We had been discussing Billy Blair, the "ringer" of the shed. <i>(1913,NZ Observer,Xmas Annual : 4</i>)

>  The majority of cries of "ringer" that are heard after a hitherto unknown, or poorly performed horse wins a heat in impressive style.. are simply the result of "pipe-dreams." <i>(Feb 28, 1934,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Two of them [sc shearers] were running one another for the title of `ringer' (the one who shore the most sheep in one day) and were cutting the sheep rather badly, necessitating the constant application of the Stockholm-tar brush to the cuts. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,143,1935 ed.</i>)

>  And when each shearing season came,/ ... She'd take the `snips' and put to shame/ The `ringer' of the crowd. <i>(Sep 1889,Sydney Bulletin</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Sept 8 Sydney Bulletin in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 121]

>  And the ringers and the borses will be passed in long review./ <i>(Oct 15, 1896,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1896 Oct 15 Otago Witness in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 131]

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Some [shearers] are so proficient at their craft that they can shear as many as a hundred and sixty sheep in a day, the best and quickest of whom is called the `ringer'. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,242</i>)

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> (`The bloke that ran across a snag') Though the ringer muttered, `Likely'; and Dick Mason sneered, `perhaps'. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,34</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Ringer, n.a sheep-shearing term. See quotations. Mr. Hornung's explanation of the origin (quotation, 1894) is probably right. See Rings <i>(1898,Austral English,389</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Michael, a ringer of an ordinary three hundred <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,81</i>)

>  `Ringer,' of course, persists for the fastest hand in a shed; with about an equal probability of derivation from the man, horse, or ship that runs `rings' round an antagonist, or from the ringer of the bell that has marked success in many games or contests besides that of the sportive `shooting gallery.' <i>(Jul 7, 1911,Maoriland Worker,18/14/2</i>)

>  The fastest shearer in the woolshed. He us said to ring the shed. [1898 Morris Austral-English] says the name was given because he runs rings round the other shearers. <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: 1933 Acland Glossary Press 18 Nov 15]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> [Gloss] Ringer: fastest shearer in the gang. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ringer: The fastest shearer in the gang. Fast men are also known as `guns,' and the slowest man in the shed as the `drummer.' <i>(1949,High Country Days,197,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> The fastest man is called the ringer and there is considerable prestige attached to it, besides the extra profit. Sometimes a man will `ring the shed' in the wethers by sheer courage or brute strength. <i>(1951,High Country,29</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> RINGER: The shearer with the highest aggregate tally of sheep shorn. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> ..., and yet over the years it has developed into a competitive sport and for past years, right back in early blade-shearing history, it was always a race, and an honour to shear for the `ringer's' stand. <i>Ibid.</i> 156 RINGER. The fastest shearer on the board. He usually shears on the first stand next to the wool table. Any rival shearer must shear a bigger tally for three consecutive days before he can take the ringer's stand. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,1</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,90</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,90</i>)

>  Ring the board. Ringer ringie: the fastest shearer in the gang occupying the number one stand. In shed shearing the position of `Ringer' is sought after and competition among shearers has always been keen. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The fastest shearer in the gang. Fast men are also known as guns, and the slowest man in the shed as the drummer. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,189</i>)

>  ~ n. shearer who shears largest number of sheep each day. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  RINGER (Ringie): The fastest shearer in a gang. He occupies the number one position on the board until successfully challenged. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>) [NOTES: [Shearer's language]]

>  ringer n.champion shearer with highest daily tally, who `runs rings round' other shearers. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (marbles term)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> It is important to distinguish these `Action-Terms' of marbles from the innumerable merely descriptive marble terms which have no effect on the course of the action - terms such as Ringer, In, Smug, Dead Stick, Skun, Skinned, Sported off, Scat, Cannons, Dubs, Kills, Funs, Keeps, Pink. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/770</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>Ibid.</i> 176 [Waitara, Taranaki, 1899: reference, game of Little Ring] Many heated arguments arose as to who said `everythings' or `nothings' first. If a player's `taw' touched a marble and lay alongside it, the others would shout `knuckle down' just before his turn. ... `Knuckle down' meant that the player had to hold his taw close to the marble attacked and fire it in that very inconvenient position. ... When a boy lost all his marbles at play he was `scat' [?related to skint ?HWO] . ... A `ringer' was a taw which, when fired at the commencement of the game, lodged in the track of the ring itself; that, and knocking a marble out when he `fired' were the perfect shots. ... To fire a shot while holding the hand nearer to the ring than where the taw had lain, was to `funk', an unforgiveable crime. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a throw that rings the spike (?or object at hoopla) [(quoits)]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> The click of quoits as someone threw a ringer. <i>(1907,Elixir of Life,190</i>)

>  quoits: In the early days when men were isolated at the sheds, quoits was a popular game. Iron rings or old horse shoes were used and the one who beat the rest was the `ringer'. This term `ringer', and `good iron' could have drifted into the shed where it is still used today. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i> ? one who works in a criminal `ring'; ? one who `rings in' stolen cars [(?criminals')]

>  POLICE believe several gangs of car ringers are active in Christchurch. They use identification plates and papers from wrecks to disguise stolen vehicles before resale....The ringers buy a late-model wreck written off by an insurance company and steal a similar car. The identification plates are then changed over and the stolen vehicle is sold as a rebuilt wreck. <i>(Mar 20, 1983,NZ Times,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> Pelters: make the arse-cuts, a cut around the ring so that it comes away from the inside. This is also called ringing or arsecutting (ringers and arse-cutters for men who do them). <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works terms]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> As I was finishing the counting-out, the boss came towards the shed with a couple of towels in his hand and invited me to come for a swim. I said I would join him as soon as I had all the tallies. The two ringer contestants then called to him, `We're going.' <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,144,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> `That second is Maori ringer this year,' said Darien, looking down the dim broad board flecked with light from the little windows, with the flash of clicking blades, ... <i>(1938,Promenade,441</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I was up with the `guns' with a top tally of 163 and had my eye on the `ringer' status, the highest and most coveted accolade in any large shed. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,115</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> from ca1930 for something `really good', superior: (cf BOTTLER, RUMPTY) [I have not heard it much used since 1941 in Wellington or Marlborough. Used only occasionally as an attrib.']



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person in charge of a team of rural workers.

>  The person in charge of a team of farm workers: a ringer. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fastest shearer in a gang.

>  The 'ringer' was J Aberfield with 252 sheep. <i>(Mar 1895,NZ Farmer,91</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> ringer - the shearer with the highest aggregate tally of sheep shorn. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111</i>)

>  <b>BARNES</b> The ganger is the boss and the Ringer is the fastest shearer. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),118</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  I don't stand up to look for work, I come down to make a start; / For I'm classed among the `ringers', and from others stand apart. / Whilst on the job I'm set for life, and I'm doing well I vow. / For I've got the boss behind me, So I want no Union now. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,24</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> The term ringer is now applied to a person who is particularly competent at any manual labour. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,39</i>)

>  The best men on the killing board [ca1914], called the Ringers were Ellis and Pettit. <i>(Jan 24, 1981,Evening Post,7</i>) [NOTES: (Clem Goddard on Gear Meat Co.)]

### ringeye
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [silvereye](../S#silvereye))

>  Mr Buller gives a curious account of the first appearance in the Province of Wellington of the little bird which we call here the Wax-eye, or Ring-eye. <i>(Jul 17, 1869,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  ".. It is the small native bird - the ring-eye I believe it is named - does the mischief here ..." <i>(May 19, 1871,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  [It may here be stated, that the writer of the foregoing would long ago have written in English for publication in the newspapers what he knew of this little bird (Ring-eye), had he not from time to time noticed in several colonial papers the most strange assertions concerning it ...] <i>(Aug 13, 1867,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Is the correct name waxeye or ringeye? <i>(May 19, 1909,Otago Witness,86</i>)

>  The beautiful little ringeye (which is generally considered to be not a native of New Zealand) builds in scrub along the banks of watercourses. <i>(Dec 11, 1890,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The natives distinguish the bird as Tau-hou (which means stranger), or Kanohi-mowhiti (which may be interpreted as spectacle-eye or ring-eye). It is also called Poporoke and Iringatu, names suggested by its accidental or periodical occurrence. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/82 By the settlers it [sc Zosterops] has been variously designated as Ring-eye, Wax-eye, White-eye, or Silver-eye, in allusion to the beautiful circlet of satiny-white feathers which surrounds the eyes; and quite as commonly as the `Blight-bird', or `Winter Migrant'. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/81</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Ring-eye, n. one of the many names for the birds of the genus <i>Zosterops </i>(q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,389</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> <i>tauhou</i>, a wandering bird (Zosterops halmaturina), blight-bird; twinkie; button-eye; wax-eye; ring-eye. Tauhou means stranger and this name was given by the Maori when the bird appeared, in 1856, in the central part of New Zealand, gradually spreading north and south till the islands were occupied. It is an Australian bird, and was blown across the Tasman sea in the year mentioned. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,46, 47</i>)

>  ... but she was big and warm and knew about cats and little ring-eyes, ... <i>(1951,Some Other Country,101</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Frame `Swans' in 1984 Some Other Country 101]

>  <i>(1953,Landfall,7/1/21</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family ZOSTEROPIDAE (silvereyes). <i>Zosterops lateralis</i> ... , called ring-eye in the south, wax-eye, white-eye, silvereye, blightie, etc., elsewhere, is probably in actual fact a blown-away Australian. Not seen here before 1886, it is certainly plentiful ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,282</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... the very first time they tried it they caught a blackbird and two ring-eyes. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,11</i>) [NOTES: (Southland)]

>  This bird is sometimes called the silver-eye, ring-eye or blight-bird. We call them "blighties". <i>(Apr 22, 1924,Otago Witness,63</i>)

### ringie
 
 <i>n.</i> ringer [shearers']

>  <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing) [See quot. at ringer n]]

>  RINGER (Ringie): The fastest shearer in a gang. He occupies the number one position on the board until successfully challenged. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>) [NOTES: [Shearer's lan guage]]



 
 <i>n.</i> the ring-keeper in a two-up game

>  Swift the `ringie' shouts with might. <i>(May 16, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,137</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Ringie, the. The man that, at two-up, keeps the ring, arranges the wagers, and pays out the winnings: Australian and New Zealand coll.: late C. 19-20 <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,700</i>)

>  A `boxer' is a gratuity to the `ringie' at two-up, a game supplying the phrase `two ladies on bicycles,' two pennies showing Britannia. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]



 
 <i>n.</i> a childrens' marble game [ca1964 Auckland, a game of marbles played in a ring]

>  <b>FRAME</b> `Treasure' I played ringie and holie. I played funs and keeps. I had pee-wees and bully taws and changers that weren't made of glass mind you. And I won nearly every game because the kids were so dazzled by the look of my stinkers. ... They came for miles to play marbles with me ... <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,116,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> [Photo caption] Marbles became `in' or `out' capriciously but someome always knew the rules of the various games - `holey,' `liney' and, seen here, `ringey' - which could be played for `funs' or `keeps.' <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### ringing
 
 <i>n.</i> [(kauri bushmen's)]

>  <b>REED</b> Deeper scarves were cut out of all Leaning trees to prevent them from splitting up. After sawing to a certain depth ..... each man alternately cut round his side of the tree almost to the front scarf. The adoption of this plan, known as `ringing', caused the wood to break clean when falling. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,157</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> arse-cutting (from ring = arsehole) [(freezing workers)]

>  <b>GORDON</b> Pelters: make the arse-cuts, a cut around the ring so that it comes away from the inside. This is also called ringing or arsecutting (ringers and arse-cutters for men who do them). <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c.Colin Gordon]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) ring crutching

>  The job may be small, such as ring crutching, but those heavy sheep have to be handled. Ideal for full crutch, ringing and wigging, dagging or sale crutch of prime lambs. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture,49</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] the circling of a mob of sheep.

>  A bit rash when ringing. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,788</i>)

>  <b>WHYTE</b> The fault of ringing sheep is a very serious one. Ringing means completing a circle around the sheep. It is a waste of time and tends to knock up the sheep. <i>(1927,Sheep-Dog, The,48</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> .. if the sheep move forward he [the sheepdog] may come round in front of you to face them, which is known as 'ringing his sheep.' <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,30</i>)

>  Ringing - when a dog heads sheep, doesn't stop behind them and goes round and round them - that's ringing. <i>(Dec 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,45</i>)

### ringing-in
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Greymouth Trotting Club also wrote <i>re</i> the "ringing-in" of a certain horse in that district, stating that the club had authorized its secretary to proceed to the North Island with the object of identifying an animal alleged to have been "rung-in" at Greymouth; also suggesting that the association might financially assist the club in its efforts to stop "ringing-in." <i>(Apr 15, 1897,Otago Witness,32</i>)

>  The Wellington Trotting Club will investigate a case of alleged ringing in. <i>(Jan 28, 1896,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  The hearing of the charge in connection with the alleged `ringing-in' trotting case was proceeded with in the Police Court, Dunedin. <i>(Feb 14, 1924,Auckland Weekly News,24</i>)

>  [Hdg] Alleged "Ringing-In" Auckland Students Charged. <i>(Feb 26, 1924,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  If "ringing-in" did occur, did Tucker have anything to do with it? <i>(May 20, 1924,Otago Witness,24</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> <i>(1952,Houses by the Sea,132</i>)

### ringtail
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Ringtail" rumors are now persistent and the gelding being a "clean-skin" it is believed that it might have hailed from New Zealand. <i>(Jun 20, 1929,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  A "Ringtail" is a colloquialism employed to describe a horse which is raced under the name of another, usually with the desire to deceive and defraud. <i>(Jul 20, 1955,NZ Truth,25</i>)

>  Has the "ringtail" entirely been eliminated from trotting in New Zealand? <i>(Feb 28, 1934,NZ Truth,2</i>)

### ringtail snorter
 
 <i>n.</i> an exceptional person

>  When it comes to hot stuff this man North is out on his own, he's a hummer, a ring-tail snorter. <i>(May 2, 1908,NZ Truth,150/1/1</i>)

### ringtailed snorter
 
 <i>phr.</i> an elaboration of SNORTER, (cf RIPSNORTER), for anything excellent or superior of its kind.

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `She was a real beaut.' `Only cost me fifty ackers.' `Did you see the ringtailed snorter old Shorty got hold of.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,51</i>)

>  He was a ring-tailed "snorter", and the overseer thought the world of him. <i>(Apr 25, 1906,Otago Witness,73</i>)

>  The English colt, Valido, who is reported to be a ring-tailed snorter in the galloping line, will make his first public appearance in the Middle Park Plate to-day. <i>(Feb 3, 1912,NZ Truth,2</i>)

### ringworms
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRASCH</b> It was very bold, among us [at Waitaki], to wear the horizontally striped socks we called ringworms, especially if the stripes were pronounced in colour ... . <i>(1980,Indirections,64</i>) [NOTES: (Waitaki school)]

### rinse
 
 <i>n.</i> the drug gamma-hydroxybutyric acid (GHB) (See [GHB](../G#ghb))

>  Instead, clubbers reached for drugs like GHB, chemical name gammahydroxybutyric acid, which was affordable and easy to obtain, but when mixed with alcohol acted like an anaesthetic and put users in vulnerable situations, "especially where safe sex was concerned". Last year, four clubbers aged under 21 had to be put on ventilators in Wellington hospital's intensive care unit after taking GBH [sic], a class B drug also known as Rinse. <i>(Sep 26, 2005,Dominion Post,A4</i>) [NOTES: electronic record: http://www.stuff.co.nz/stuff/0,2106,3423102a11,00.html#top Accessed 09/02/06]

>  Also found at the house was stolen property, including laptops taken in inner-city burglaries as well as quantities of the gamma-hydroxybutyric acid (commonly referred to as rinse or GHB), cannabis plant and seeds, steroids and a pipe used for smoking methamphetamine (speed). <i>(Jul 29, 2003,Independent Herald (Wellington),3</i>)

>  Some of the people at [his] home that night said he had previously supplied them with drugs, including Rinse, a form of Fantasy. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

### rintin
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> ... what would I wish for anyway? A return of the spirit of joy? ... Maybe, considering this rintin shambles of a night, I should wish something for them ... for Simon, what? <i>(1983,Bone People, The,93</i>)

### riot pack
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAHY</b> "A riot pack," said Richard, meaning that he had bought a large carton holding three litres of wine. <i>(1985,Carpathians, The,112</i>)

>  During the 1980s around 70c of every dollar earned came from three- or four-litre casks, known by some as "riot packs". <i>(Apr 1, 1995,Evening Post,13</i>)

### riovi
 
 <i>n.</i> rowi (kiwi)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Riovi [possibly Kiwi], this bird was never repletely described to me, but is said to be a very remarkable one, and sometimes caught by the natives. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>)

### rip
 
 <i>n.</i> (Wellington coast)

>  <b>GREEN</b> The Rip ... we stopped off the entrance of Port Phillip to take a Melbourne pilot on board, and steaming through the `Rip', as the rough water between the heads is called, we let go our anchor ... <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [?Terawhiti Rip] Stood out of [Port Nicholson] harbour. Off the headland got into a strong race (tide rip), observed the ebb tide set to the Southd. <i>(Nov 9, 1839,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,450,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PUGH-WILLIAMS</b> `When used by seafarers they are generally referring to the tide rips in Cook Strait off Terawhiti. <i>(1951,Letter</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> All went well until we passed Cape Farewell and Stephen's Island and the Brothers and encountered what they called the rip in Cook Strait. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,46</i>)

>  <b>MACKENZIE</b> The Rip (Cook Strait) Terawhiti rip The boat [Tamahine c1941] always seemed to head for that notorious stretch of water known as `the rip', and all the chairs ... anchored with chains would turn over, while a nurse in white would move about among the green passengers with much-needed strawberry boxes. -->OED 1. commotion of seas caused by opposing currents or by a rapid current passing over an ... 1836, 1860, 1875 2. tidal wave or current 1903; CC Ransom =US riptide, rip current <i>(1982,WAAF Book,30</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of a pig) slash (a dog) with its tusks

>  A young man named M'Guiness of West Melton, was badly ripped by a pig at the Addington Saleyards to-day the animal rushing at him and inflicting a gash in his thigh three or four inches in length. <i>(May 17, 1906,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Caught a fine young boar and Jock got ripped by a large boar <i>(Nov 17, 1849,Torlesse Papers,113,1958 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> constr. often the rip a strong tidal current (usually off a headland, point), a TIDE-RIP

>  The first such instance saw me in my novice days towed from a rip by one of the club veterans. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/37</i>)

>  <b>CAPT.J.R.KENT</b> Commences with strong Gales and thick misty weather; a strong ripple, and a heavy cross-sea running, the vessle [sic] shipping a quantity of water - <i>(May 29, 1823,Log</i>) [NOTES: 1823 May 29 ripple =?rip Mermaid log in 1940 Howard Rakiura 342]

>  <b>CARRINGTON</b> the Tide Rips. [? sense] <i>(1841,Journal,29</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> The term [tide-rip] is well-known in New Zealand and especially in Melbourne; I never heard it used in England although I am informed they exist in the Portland firth, and ... near Weymouth. A Tide-rip is caused by a current setting in a certain direction after a gale of wind over perhaps an under surface. <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,125</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> The `rip', or tide, running like a mill-race against us, was terrible. <i>(1863,Incidents of the Maori War,402</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When the water is half to three-quarters out [at Waikouaiti] there is a terrible rip but at full tide it is motionless ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,20</i>)

### rip hell out of
 
 <i>phr.</i> [a person or thing ca1938]

### rip into
 
 <i>v.</i> to berate, tear a strip off (a person); to get stuck into (a job of work) [ca1938 Havelock]

>  <b>McGILL</b> rip into attack with fists or words, often in positive fashion, such as aiming to move a pile of rubble; eg `Let's rip into this digging of the swimming pool, should only take us a few hours.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

### rip off
 
 <i>v.</i> to rush off

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I ripped off down to where the nearest pair of dogs were barking. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,38</i>)

### rip one's nightie
 
 <i>phr.</i> make one angry

>  More than anything, it is the unbearably condescending nature of the bill's wording that really rips my nightie... <i>(Dec 14, 2005,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),9</i>)

### rip up
 
 <i>v.</i> >rip somebody (for arse-wife) to berate soundly; `abuse'; to tear one's character to pieces. (cf rip into) [ca1935]

>  `threats from one of [the toughs] to `rip up' his pursuers.' =(?) swear at ;?? rip into ie. beat up ;? cut to ribbons (ie like ripping up one's stomach)] <i>(1865,Evening Post,1/69/3</i>)

### rip, shit or bust
 
 <i>phr.</i> as an asseveration or attrib, of action or person who goes bull-at-a-gate at anything without thought to the consequences, or the quality of the result. -->ca1930 Havelock. -->ca1940 (Les Cleveland) [also: rip, shit and bust.]

>  Greenwood said too many Kiwi developments were "rip, shit and bust" and he believed foreign buyers took better care of their land. <i>(Aug 15, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A15</i>)

>  Roy Billing was in the original production and reignites his crowd pleasing blustery performance as Tupper the rip-shit-or-bust coach, who truly believes that Tuesday and Thursday down at the clubhouse would do any lad the world of good. <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  The iconic figure of the "rip-shit-and-bust" coach Tupper survives, originally a World War II era throwback, increasingly out of place amid the political, racial and social awakenings of the 1970s. <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Instead of rip-shit-and-bust coaches who talked only about guts and toughness.., John Hart began using the vocabulary of business, people management and monetarist economics, words like "accuracy" and "efficiency". <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  <b>McGEE</b> The days of the rip-shit-or-bust coach are gone. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,85</i>)

>  In a fit of name-calling / they fix blame by brawling / as they go at it rip, shit and bust... <i>(Oct 3, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C12</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> If anything breaks grab our standby truck and keep hauling. It's rip, tip or bust at this game [hauling road metal] and no please or thank you. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,120,1960 ed.</i>)

>  It didn't ever seem to occur to him that a leisurely, measured approach might work better than rip shit and bust. <i>(Nov 11, 1983,National Business Review,57</i>)

>  He'd already picked a name - it would be Loosehead Len and he'd be a right-wing, rip shit and bust Kiwi bloke, opinionated about everything, .. <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,48</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> rip shit and bust a big effort, often sporting; also rip split or bust; eg `Okay, team. If we're to have any chance of pegging this game back in the second half, it's got to be rip, shit and bust!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

### rip, split or bust
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>CHARLES</b> We'll get that old Buster! [a wild boar] Rip, split or bust! <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,49</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) Bromus sterilis, a broom.

 <i>n.</i> an established Asian immigrant

>  Newly arrived Asians, I am told, call their more established counterparts "ripe bananas" - yellow on the outside and white on the inside. <i>(Jul 17, 2002,NZ Herald,A15</i>)

 <i>v.</i> [(whalers')]

### Rippa Rugby
 
 <i>n.</i> an introductory form of rugby for primary school children

>  [Hdg] Rippa Rugby a big hit with primary schools... In Rippa Rugby, a New Zealand Rugby Union initiative, players wear a belt with two velcro flags, passing the ball when a flag is removed and a player yells "ripped". <i>(May 29, 2004,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

### ripper
 
 <i>n.</i> a good bloke

>  The men say things like, "You ripper, mate." <i>(Oct 15, 2003,Capital Times,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Someone who raids a cannabis patch before the growers can harvest the crop

>  The worst dread of every grower, though, is a new breed of "rippers" who raid a patch, sometimes before it is even ready for harvest... Two years ago rippers were scum... There are so many out there now that it's become accepted. <i>(Jun 29, 1998,Dominion,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> good thing

>  "I come finally today to bring you tidings of great joy. At last we have the health policy." And what a little ripper it is too. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> So with huge labour and ingenuity we made that final historic campaign a ripper. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,285</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> good [1984 16 F4 E43 Pakuranga Coll. 8 RIPPER [something/-one good, -->1984 16 M3 E43 Pakuranga Coll. 18 RIPPER liked, delightful] -->1984 14 F E141 Wgton Girls C. 18 RIPPER]

>  ripper (beaut): good <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> ripper! excellent; eg `Got tickets for the game? You have? Ripper!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

### ripper down
 
 <i>n.</i> (in a freezing-works)

>  RIPPERS DOWN (Rotates with shoulder men) <i>(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,42</i>)

### ripple
 
 <i>n.</i> a tide rip (see rip)

>  There was a strong tide ripple on it, and the water was of a light sandy colour. <i>(Jul 10, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  ..mariners waiting the turn of tide, entering or leaving the harbour, are recommended to keep the Point Lonsdale shore aboard, as the tide there runs fairer, and in bad weather small vessels incur less risk on the Point Lonsdale shore from the tide ripples than towards Port Nepean. <i>(Jun 10, 1854,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  After leaving Lord's Harbour, you come to a critical boat passage between 2 reefs: here are two tides meeting at the flood, which causes a dangerous ripple, this instantly subsides at the ebb when the waters recede from each other. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,105,1986 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>WEKEY</b> ... they had the advantage ... of going from one bar of the river to another, ... which, probably for ages, served as ripples on a grand scale, to catch and accumulate the gold-dust brought down by the river, ... <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,65</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> There was a minor rush to Coal Creek ... and I think a little gold was got in the ripples. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,172</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> RIFFLE OR RIPPLE: both terms were used for the grooves cut across the bottom of a sluice-box or for the much more common wooden bars fixed at right angles, obliquely, or lengthwise, to the bottom of a sluice. The `riffleboard' was detachable and formed a `false bottom'. `Riffle' was probably a Californian term; `ripple' seems to have been more usual on the goldfields of Australasia. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

>  Mr. William Beetham ... in Nelson, mentioned the trouble ... occasioned at the erpiodical `clean-up' by the presence of ... lead which collected in the ripples with the gold. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/403</i>)

### ripple board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The tailings escape from the ripple through a row of small holes, half an inch in diameter, in the ripple board, pass through a second ripple, then through the third, on to the more gradual incline of the remaining twelve feet of the ripple table. <i>(Jul 6, 1872,Otago Witness,8</i>)

### ripple plate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  VULCAN FOUNDRY .. cast iron sluice and ripple plates ... <i>(Jun 3, 1882,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  There is a slight fall between each roller, on which are placed copper ripple plates, so that whatever gold is liberated by the crushing of the first roller has a chance to get amalgamated on the first copper ripples, and so with the second chamber, where the material passes through a rough grating before entering the third or last chamber, where it is retained until fine enough to go through a 240 punched grating, after which it is passed over the final copper ripples. <i>(Apr 12, 1894,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  ... the water and debris then ran down a sluice channel into boxes fitted with coconut matting and `ripple plates'; these caught and retained the gold which was collected at the regular `wash-up.' <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,291,1988 ed.</i>)

### ripple table
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The tailings escape from the ripple through a row of small holes, half an inch in diameter, in the ripple board, pass through a second ripple, then through the third, on to the more gradual incline of the remaining twelve feet of the ripple table. <i>(Jul 6, 1872,Otago Witness,8</i>)

### ripsnorter
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  If this doesn't make for a rip-snorter election campaign, then tough. <i>(May 22, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### riripi
 
 <i>n.</i> lilipi spg ripiri [? an error - transposition of sylls]

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> We got a `fiery stick' from some Maoris ... who were waiting for a `Goashore' of `Ripiri' to cool ... The mess was in time cool enough, when they all surrounded the great pot, some with pewter spoons, ... but most with shells from the beach ... . <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,18</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in Outlook in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 18 [reference Wellington 1850s]]

>  <b>CARKEEK</b> Hadfield in his 1839 diary mentions eating a meal which included boiled flour. This is probably the same food which became known to the Maoris later as riripi. It consisted of flour and water, with sugar [53] to sweeten, boiled and served up as a kind of porridge. In the early whaling days it was often served up to a whole village community from a canoe set up like a trough from which the villagers scooped their requirements in large bowls or paua shells. Many of the Ngati Raukawa of Otaki have vivid recollections of having eaten riripi in their childhood. <i>(1966,Kapiti Coast,52</i>)

### riroriro
 
 <i>n.</i> grey warbler, a small native bird, host to the eggs of the shining cuckoo. [Maori]

>  Tuis, mokomoko, riroriro native canaries, wrens, robins, and ringeyes, are fairly plentiful. <i>(Jul 21, 1892,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Even the grey warbler is affected.. riroriro's insistent aching call.. abruptly stops. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,111</i>)

>  Riroriro also establish their territories in late July and August in preparation for nest building. Hearing the riroriro in early July on a beautiful clear winter's afternoon took me by surprise. <i>(Jun 2006,Tumai,76/30</i>)

>  The grey warbler (riroriro) is just as common in the wilds as he is in the town gardens, and delights the ear with his musical little trill. <i>(Jun 26, 1912,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  [His] observations are of the little grey warbler to which he gives its Maori name, riro-riro. <i>(Apr 13, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  First among these indications was the early nesting of the little riroriro, or gray warbler. <i>(Sep 28, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Riroriro</i> - A very small brown bird, with white feathers under the wings and tail. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),58</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>riroriro</i> is a kind of swallow, not much unlike those in Great Britain. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/301</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 19.<i>Certhiparus maculicandus</i>. ... Voy. de l' Astrol., Zool. 1.210 Momohoua [f. French spg]of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy et Gaim</i>. Riro riro of the natives of the Northern Islands. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. ... --as a note to 13. <i>Acanthisitta longpipes</i>, (bush wren)] The bird, with the native name of Didadido, given by M.Lesson, may probably prove a species of this genus. [It did not: HWO] <i>(1842,Travels in NZ,2/189</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/189 [this must be an error of D's]]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The family <i>Luscinidae</i> has five species. The Matata, a small low-flying bird with a shrill cry, and the Riro or wren, the smallest bird in New Zealand, are the only species worthy of notice. The Matata has four long and four short tail feathers, similar in texture to those of the Kiwi. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/25</i>)

>  ... Read the papers & did Maori, went for about half an hour in the Domain & found a fantail nest (young ones) & a riroriro's with two young ones & an egg. I am inclined to think the egg is a pipiwharauroa's (white, with purple spots at the larger end). Splitting firewood in the afn. I found a fine large centipede - a weri - body brown, with the articulations very plainly marked, legs a pale blue .. <i>(Oct 20, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/645,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <i>Gerygone flaviventris</i>. Riro-riro. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 24. GERYGONE FLAVIVENTRIS. Gray. Grey Warbler. Riro-riro. <i>G. assimilis</i>, BULLER. <i>G. aucklandica</i>, PELZ. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,10</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> GERYGONE FLAVIVENTRIS. (GREY WARBLER.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Riroriro and Koriroriro. \{- This is the Grey Warbler, the well -known Riroriro of Maori history and song.\} <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,107,{1888 2nd edn 1/44} ed.</i>)

>  I have got a great many birds since you have been away, which I shot with your gun or a catapult (excuse spelling), and which I stuff and hang up by the beak. ... One of them is a New Zealand bittern ... I have also the mako-mako, silver-eye, riro-riro, miro-miro, fanny etc. <i>(Aug 16, 1875,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/399</i>) [NOTES: (?Bill Atkinson, Nelson, 15 years)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Riroriro (<i>Gerygone flaviventris</i>) is a little warbler seen about in company with the <i>tauhau</i>. [=?tauhou `silvereye'] <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> There is a little bird called Riro-riro in New Zealand from its note. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,125 fn</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> ... rerorero (the rainbird) <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,7</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> a bird. Maori name for the Grey-Warbler of New Zealand, <i>Gereygone flaviventris</i>, Gray. See <i>Gerygone.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,390</i>)

>  <i>Ri&#769;.ro-ri&#769;.ro</i> (r&emacr.&#769;.ro.-r&emacr.&#769;.ro.), n. [Maori.] (<i>Zool</i>.) Any New Zealand bird of the genus <i>Gerygone.</i> See GERYGONE. [Local, N. Z.] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  ~ n. the gray warbler. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> But here, a little stream meandered under cream-plumed shimmer of huge toi-toi bushes, crimson-billed pukekos with thoughtful expressions waded downstream, and a dart of wings meant riri-riro, the Maori's little bird of laughter, adventuring in the sunlit spaces. <i>(1934,Journalese,109</i>)

>  <i>riroriro</i>, a bird (Pseudogerygone igata), grey-warbler; favourite foster-parent for young bronze-wing cuckoo. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> GREY WARBLER (Pseudegerygone igata), Riroriro of the Maoris, is a small brownish grey bird abundant in all parts of New Zealand. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  `A little wren managed to squeeze itself through, and it flew off to Kurangai-tuku, and cried, `Kurangai-tuku, the man is riro, riro!' -that is, gone, gone, gone. And to this day the bird is known as the riro-riro. <i>(1889,Picturesque Australasia,IV/163</i>) [NOTES: >1889. Cassell's `Picturesque Australasia,' vol. iv. p. 163:]

>  <i>riroriro</i>: grey warbler. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <i>riroriro n</i> -s [Maori] : GRAY WARBLER <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1960</i>)

>  ~ n. bird, New Zealand wren, the grey warbler [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),523</i>)

>  <i>riro-riro ... n</i>. bird, N.Z. wren, grey warbler [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),17</i>)

>  254. <i>Gerygone igata</i> (Quoy and Gaimard) Grey Warbler (Riroriro) <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,67,1980 ed.</i>)

>  254. <i>Gerygone igata</i> (Quoy and Gaimard) Grey Warbler (Riroriro) <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,67,1980 ed.</i>)

### rise
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [top the rise](../T#top-the-rise))



 
 <i>n.</i> an upward shaft

>  Rise: A small section shaft formed by working upwards from a level. <i>(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,188</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 188]

>  <b>BLYTH</b> Rise: Rising on the ore body. RISE: Quartz-mining term for working in or driving a shaft upwards. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Rise: A timbered excavation constructed by working upwards, using upper holes. Rising was done on the three-compartment system, that is with a ladderway on one end and a return airway on the other, with a `box' in the centre. This box was kept filled with ore and used to stand on while drilling the roof, the level of the ore being maintained by, drawing off the surplus ore through a chute at the bottom of the box. Rises were about 9 ft by 4 ft in section and might reach from one level to the next (about 150 ft), or to a sublevel, or from a stope to the level above. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),331</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [(fencing)]

 <i>n.</i> (i.e. of a Court)

 <i>n.</i> baking-powder; baking-soda (occas. yeast)

### rito
 
 <i>n.</i> growing shoot

>  Earlier, Ngarimu had sung a karakia and shown me how to cut tussock so that the rito, or growing shoot, wasn't severed. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The erect erito [e-acute], and other umbelliferous palm-[68]trees wave their broad leaves proudly amid the varied foliage of the surrounding trees. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/105 There is scarcely a more splendid sight to behold than a New Zealand forest. In the one we now entered, we did not perceive any kouri, or yellow pine-trees; but the totara [a's-acute], or red pine, grew in vast abundance. ... The rito, and innumerable other palm-trees, were in great quantity. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/143 In addition to the fare set before me at supper, a quantity of rito, or hearts of the palm-tree, were also included. This vegetable, which is eaten uncooked, makes a salad unequalled for its excellent flavour, and is held in much esteem by the people. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/287 rito The heart of the delicious palms, called E' Rito, is highly esteemed. The korou, which the branches of another of these umbelliferous trees afford is equally prized, together with the saccharine roots of the ti, cabbage-tree. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/67</i>)

### river
 
 <i>n.</i> ?mainly Auckland cf CREEK [[see also ?Eaddy GRAVEYARD (?Kaipara)] -->R. Mason notes: in Auckland Province narrow harbours or arms of harbours, mostly on the west coast are often called rivers, and were so named on older maps and in writings. They are in fact formed by the drowning of river systems. [nf in this sense OED, EDD, W3] -->On old maps: The Whangape River, The Herekino River, The Hokianga River; on present-day maps, The Waiuku River. At the present time [1957] the local inhabitants refer to the Hokianga Harbour as `the river', as do the people of Herekino to the Herekino harbour. Jane mander's novel The Story of a New Zealand River refers to the Kaipara harbour, I understand. Various arms of kaipara and Hokianga are still named `rivers' on present maps. In Waitemata Harbour, the Whau River; and other branches also rivers.]

>  <b>KING</b> `1823. May 3rd. - The Rev. J. Butler came and informed us of the wreck of the American schooner Cossack at Shokeehanga (Hokianga) River. <i>(May 3, 1823,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1823 May 3 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 255]

>  []sound, inlet Captain Steine discovered another large river near the entrance of the river William The Fourth, which he named Queen Adelaide River. [McNab comments, p21: The term river was, at that time, applied to what we would now designate a sound, [William IV River] was Tory Channel] <i>(Sep 14, 1832,Log</i>) [NOTES: 1832 Sep 14 Capt. Steine in Courier (Hobart Town) in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 20]

>  <b>POLACK</b> A river in New Zealand, from twenty to forty miles in length in a direct course, often flows in serpentine directions nearly three times that length; the salt-water rivers are joined at their entrances by fresh-water creeks, many of which pursue routes inland, through perhaps eighty miles of country. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/169 fn</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> ... in the vicinity of the Hokianga river of the West Coast. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),143</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> She [sc a tug] had come down the Kawa Kawa (called a river, but really an arm of the sea, like the Paramatta) from the railway ... <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,174</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> ... naturally they cannot know that what with our [sc. North Auckland's] tidal rivers and freshwater creeks, ... we really want no roads ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/233</i>)

>  The usual name for the inlet [on Dunedin harbour] appears to have been `The River' [?c1817]. <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/141</i>)

>  ... the Wairiri Creek - a tributary of the Hokianga River. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/351</i>) [NOTES: Walsh]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... European families had by this time either returned to Sydney or shifted over to `Otago' (the district around the Otago Harbour, or Otago River as the whalers called it). <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,38</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> Most of Whina's earliest memories are of `the river', [38] as they always called the [Hokianga] harbour. <i>(1983,Whina,37</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> as in general English: but in early uses (a) note that the epithet freshwater is often prefixed; and (b) note the contrasting British usage (and possibly Australian), e.g. the River Thames, the River Murray, with NZ, the Thames River

>  <b>HAY</b> Kaipara, a fine basin about eight miles across, having five considerable rivers running into it ... <i>(1832,Notices of NZ,10,1979 ed.</i>)

>  sc a freshwater river On the East side is a small fresh water river. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,54,1986 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  ... close to the River Pots. <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/148</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  River Avon <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/151</i>) [NOTES: (Chilton)]

>  River Waimakariri River Hawdon <i>(1906,TrNZI,33/266</i>) [NOTES: (Cockayne)]

>  ... the River Hutt <i>(1902,TrNZI,34/99</i>) [NOTES: Hocken]

>  The River Maruia could be followed south. <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/396</i>) [NOTES: (Townson)]

>  River Turakina [This seems to be Cockayne's standard style for the TrNZI.] ibid. River Rangitikei p.400 <i>(1908,TrNZI,41/399</i>) [NOTES: (Cockayne)]

>  <b>FRAME</b> When I had returned to New Zealand and wrote The Adaptable Man set in Suffolk, a sharp-eyed critic noted my inaccurate reference to the Orwell River instead of the correct, River Orwell, my usage being the New Zealand [154] idiom - the Rakaia River, the Waitaki River, the Clutha River - and not the English - River Thames, Humber, Orwell. - <i>(1985,Envoy from Mirror City,153</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [aka river-beach]

 <i>n.</i> [see DNZE; aka river-bed]

 <i>n.</i>

>  PUBLIC NOTICE is hereby given that the Opio River Board intends on Wednesday, 7th March, 1923, to make, pursuant to Section 87 of "The River Boards Act, 1908," and of amendments thereof, and Sections 48 and 50 of "The Rating Act, 1908," and its amendments - A GENERAL RATE OF 7/8d in the GBP in Grade One .. on the Rateable Value of all lands within the Opio River District for the year commencing on 1st April, 1922, and ending on 31st March, 1923, and payable in one sum of 14th March, 1923. <i>(Feb 13, 1923,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  PUBLIC NOTICE is hereby given that the Opio River Board intends on Wednesday, 7th March, 1923, to make, pursuant to Section 87 of "The River Boards Act, 1908," and of amendments thereof, and Sections 48 and 50 of "The Rating Act, 1908," and its amendments - A GENERAL RATE OF 7/8d in the GBP in Grade One .. on the Rateable Value of all lands within the Opio River District for the year commencing on 1st April, 1922, and ending on 31st March, 1923, and payable in one sum of 14th March, 1923. <i>(Feb 13, 1923,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  A resolution was passed unanimously - "That the Government be asked to pass an Act forming a central River Board, comprising and amalgamating the existing River Boards Districts, the districts to be divided into wards of as nearly as possible equal rating value, each ward returning one member or more, the rating basis to be the same as now existing, with power to obtain loans." <i>(Aug 10, 1905,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  THE Ratepayers of the Spring Creek District are hereby requested to await the Meeting at Marlboroughtown on the 20th inst., when it is intended to discuss the attempt at enforced amalgamation, now insidiously carried on by some members of the "Lower Wairau River Board," and that in the meantime they be not cajoled into sig[n]ing any petition whatever, hawked about by their emissaries. <i>(Mar 9, 1895,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  The Hibernian (a river claim) will commence paddocking in a few days, and they deserve to be well remunerated for the energy and pluck they have displayed for the last three years in all weathers, and under the most trying circumstances. <i>(Apr 28, 1877,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  The following applications were granted .. James Cowan and others, protection for three months for the river claim on the Molyneux River, Beaumont ... <i>(Feb 25, 1888,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  The following applications were dealt with on May 23rd .. E. J. Craighead, Extended river claim. <i>(May 25, 1912,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  They also applied for Certificate of protection of special dredging claim No 2031, extended river claim No 2044, and special river claim 2073. <i>(Aug 17, 1926,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  In the case, Adams <i>v</i> Pringle and others, an appeal from the Warden's decision over a piece of spare ground between the Golden Run Co's and respondent's river claims at Millers Flat, his Honor upheld the Warden's decision, with costs (GBP10 16s). <i>(Sep 27, 1893,Tuapeka Times,5</i>)

>  At the Warden's Court, Queenstown, on the 11th inst., before Mr. F. Burgess, warden, Jas. M'Mullan, jun., made application for a special river claim of 20 acres, situated at Arthur's Point, one mile below the bridge. <i>(Aug 19, 1903,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  Dunstan, 13th June, - ... At the Kawarau ... all the river claims are submerged, the paddocks and tunnels in the banks are mostly fallen in ... Arrow Township, 10th July. - ... Huts, tents, wheels, sluice-boxes - everything on the beach and river claims have been swept off by the torrent, ... <i>(Jul 18, 1863,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### river crosser
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAAST</b> The man I refer to is a first rate traveller, a keen river crosser, and in every other respect a good bushman, but he is giddy ... and this would be a very great drawback to passing round the coast. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,48</i>)

### river crow
 
 <i>n.</i> shag, white-throated

>  <b>BULLER</b> .. not the least interesting feature to me [by the Huka Falls] was this, that a dozen or more of these little [White-throated] Shags (or `River Crows' as they are sometimes called) kept passing and repassing through the misty spray. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/171,1873 ed.</i>)

### river diggings
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf WET DIGGINGS; cp DRY DIGGINGS; for citation see dry diggings 1864]

>  The Honourable The Colonial Secretary, &c., &c., &c. Kapanga River Diggings, Coromandel Harbour, 4th November, 1852. <i>(Dec 15, 1852,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  Various tracks to the outlying districts were then discussed, the principal of which was that through Waikaia bush to Whitcomb and Upper Waikaia river diggings. <i>(Mar 28, 1873,Bruce Herald,6</i>)

>  All the sections offered in Cromarty, the township near Wilson's river diggings, were sold, but there are complaints of want of publicity of the sales, many who would have been buyers not knowing it was to take place. <i>(Dec 17, 1892,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

### river flat
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere, but widely used and significant in New Zealand's horticulture, agriculture, pastoralism, and viticulture] a level area of land formed from the course of a river.

>  Every Person desirous of establishing a claim to a particular portion of unoccupied ground, by working, in the ordinary method, for Alluvial Gold, may have his claim marked out on the following scale, viz., not less than .. (4) 20 feet square of table-land, or river flats. <i>(Nov 27, 1852,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  It was persisted in with blind prejudice, that the interior would never support more than a handful of back settlers, who might possibly be able to eke out a scanty living by dint of running a few head of cattle here and there over a few grassy patches of river flat. <i>(Nov 20, 1866,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  The country is of various descriptions .. with some river-flats. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,233</i>)

>  On Messrs Donald Bros' farm, close to Masterton, on a river flat, they have just threshed a crop of oats. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,234</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The country above the lake, with its miles of beach, great carpet-grass basins and heavy river-flats, is ideal country for deer. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,169</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> It split along the top ... and rushed across the riverflat towards Charlie Morel's house. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,97</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> ... sheep sometimes went wild, wandering the back country and straggling up the river flats. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,11</i>)

>  Some river flats in front rising to medium hill with some steep faces. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,77</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> As we walked down the Acheron, sheep ran down the Acheron face to join others stringing down the river flats. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,67</i>)

>  It gets quite warm on the river flats and gullies and we get a lot of early morning fog. <i>(Mar 29, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  Te Makahi has about 72 ha of fertile river flats suitable for orchards and 161 ha of clean, steep grazable hills. [riverflat; river-flat] <i>(Apr 12, 2002,NZ Farmer,40</i>)

>  200 acres good land, river flats, near butter factory, GBP1400. <i>(Apr 24, 1903,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  Block IV.: Sections 1, 2, 3, 4, undulating fern, tutu, and grass hills, excellent soil on river flats. <i>(Mar 27, 1880,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>McClymont</b> Thomson, who was a firm believer in the rights of explorers, saw to it that the maps showed the names they gave : Ben Nevis, Nevis river, Lochy river, Roy river and the Devil's Staircase. Ibid. 80 Every creek and every river-flat was prospected. <i>(1959,Exploration of NZ, The,75</i>)

>  4612 acres of fine Pastoral Country, comprising low undulating hills and river flats, a very large portion of which are ploughable: also 197 acres of leasehold (Gordon's Valley) held on perpetual lease. <i>(Sep 3, 1897,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  Despite this, surface water will always be rather slow in disappearing as the whole area is composed of river flat rich soil on a solid clay bottom, which does not draw off the water as quickly as if it were sandy or gravelly. <i>(Aug 11, 1931,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Our road lay now ... across a grassy flat, mostly overgrown with the same dense vegetation met with on the banks of all our river-flats, namely the spiny Wild-Irishman, and the bayonet-like Spaniard (<i>Aciphylla Lyallii</i>). <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,124</i>)

>  The whole valley of the Wangapeka consists of a narrow gorge, with here and there a small river-flat <i>(1879,TrNZI,442</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> It was in his first month, as he passed a river flat one day, that he saw a young man begin the cutting of a sledge track ... <i>(1925,Allen Adair,15,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> The winter months had been long and grim, and had left their marks on mountains, river flats, cattle, sheep, dogs and men. <i>(1964,High Country Family,11</i>)

### river flounder
 
 <i>n.</i> = black flounder

>  The Black or River Flounder of New Zealand (<i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i>). The river-flounder is one of the common edible fishes of Wellington. Though not so common as either the yellow or sand flounder, it is much in demand as the chief delicacy among all species of flat fish eaten by the New Zealand public. <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/368</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Hutton. River flounder (black flounder). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/408</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> River or Black Flounder <i>Rhombosolea (Adamasoma) retiaria</i> Hutton <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,405</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 405]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Patiki mohao, Rhombosolea retiara, river or black flounder. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,271</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BLACK FLOUNDER [<i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i>] ... River flounder and mud flounder are widely used alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,32</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND BLACK FLOUNDER or RIVER FLOUNDER (PATIKI-MOHOAO) <i>Rhombosolea (Adamsoma) retiaria adams</i> Whitley and Phillipps/ It is known to fishermen and those in the fishing industry as the River Flounder as it is caught mostly inside or near the mouths of rivers and enters lakes where it lives chiefly among weeds. To some it is known as the Maori Flounder. I have helped to catch these fish in various rivers in New Zealand, Both in the North and South Islands, from the Bay of Islands, Bay of Plenty, Canterbury and Otago. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,204,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLACK FLOUNDER ... <i>Other names: Rhombosolea retiaria</i>; mud flounder, river flounder, estuary flonder; patiki-mahoao (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,215</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLOUNDER, BLACK More sole-like in shape than his cousin flounders this fellow also has small, brick-red spots on his back and has the ability - unlike the other flatfish - of being able to live in pure, fresh water. Other names are `River Flounder' and `Mud or Freshwater Flounder'. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,72</i>)

>  [species name] black flounder .. [scientific name] <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> .. [other name(s)] blacks, freshwater flounder, river flounder <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  Black flounder <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Restricted to New Zealand. Alternative names include river flounder, freshwater flounder, or estuary flounder, derived from its habitat. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,144</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>RHOMBOSOLEA RETIARIA</i> Hutton. Black Flounder; Patiki-mohoao. This species, known generally as `river-flounder,' is occasionally taken in the sea. Common in the estuaries of most large rivers, and often taken many miles inland. <i>(Jul 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Hutton. RIVER-FLOUNDER, BLACK FLOUNDER; PATIKI-MOHOAO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,29,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flounder, river 2287-90 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### river gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering team working in Marlborough and North Canterbury.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A musterer might state that he is in the River Gang and that again is sufficient. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,50</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Blairich is one of the stations that used to get the 'River Gang' to do its mustering .. like the Flying Gang in the Hanmer Spring district they did just the actual mustering, moving on from place to place as the hill work was completed. They covered a tremendous amount of country, all hard going, and the River Gang was widely known. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,52</i>)

### river gurnard
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Leptoscopus macropygus<i>, stargazer.

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> STARGAZER ... <i>Other names: Leptoscopus macropygus</i>; river gurnard, mud gurnard, flat-head gurnard, mud grubber. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,262</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Most gurnards are brownish-red but larger specimens are often bright red on top and white below. The Stargazer is sometimes known as the Mud Gurnad, River Gurnard or Mud-Grubber Gurnard but is in fact no relation. Similarly the Sea Perch is sometimes called a Rock Gurnard - .. . <i>Ibid.</i> 197 RIVER GURNARD: see <i>Spotted Stargazer </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 208 STARGAZER A distinctive estaurine fish which spends most of its life buried in mud up to the eyes. ... Comes in two varieties - the Common Stargazer and his tubbier cousin the Spotted Stargazer. ... Other names are River Gurnard, Mud Gurnard, Flathead Gurnard and Mud Grubber <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,118</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Stargazer <i>Leptoscopus macropygus</i> Also occurs in south-eastern Australia. Alternatively called estuary stargazer, and mud, river, or flathead gurnard. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,119</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] gurnard, river 1814 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a crossing and recrossing of rivers in mustering or droving sheep.

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] a horse used to cross fast-flowing mountain rivers, often as a pack-horse or harness-horse.

### river-wash
 
 <i>n.</i> [(? goldmining)]

>  2. River-bed Gravels and Sands. - These occupy the beds of the Clutha and Kawarau Rivers. ...Throughout the Dunstan and Kawarau Gorges these Gravels, commonly spoken of as river-wash, rest on the schist bottom. ... The only practicable method of winning gold lying at the bottom of this river-wash is dredging. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No5 (N.S.)/46</i>) [NOTES: (Park: Cromwell)]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the washing of fleecewool on sheep's backs by dipping sheep in river pools.

>  Let us hope to see this system pursued and extended year by year until river washing and greasy clips will be the exception, and not the rule as it is now. <i>(Apr 9, 1869,North Otago Times,5</i>)

### river-working
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Whilst at the Dunstan I for the first time heard of Fox's Rush, and the Shotover River workings; and as I was on the direct route to those localities I resolved to proceed thither for the purpose of ascertaining the true state of affairs. <i>(Dec 27, 1862,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  The river Molyneux still continues high, and river workings are therefore at a standstill; but with the numerous rushes in the neighbourhood there is no lack of employment for the mining population. <i>(Nov 25, 1862,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  Quartz-mining, especially at Macetown and Skipper's Point and neighbourhood, will receive an impetus; river workings, both by dredging and the hydraulic jet processes, will be started in the Shotover and the Kawarau rivers ... <i>(Sep 7, 1888,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  Besides the drawbacks mentioned it must not be overlooked that the season was a most unfavourable one for river workings of any kind. <i>(Nov 12, 1901,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  The road here follows the eastern bank of the creek, and, in addition to the river workings, water races and dams (of which there are several at this spot), a few houses, huts, and gardens, can be seen dotted here and there, filling up and making altogether a very pleasant and agreeable picture. <i>(Feb 5, 1870,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  At the Lower Wilson a few parties have been earning small wages in terrace workings in wet weather and river workings in dry. <i>(Mar 15, 1894,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  Before the close of the year 1862, 70,000oz.were transmitted by escort from the Dunstan Goldfield; but this was not all derived from the river-workings. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,8</i>) [NOTES: 1863 V. Pyke's Report in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 8]

### Riverbed Mould
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of Riverhead Gold q.v.

Alan asked, and when I said I'd never heard of that tobacco, he told me that was what all 'real smokers' like himself called Riverbed Gold, which was named after a famous racehorse and had a drawing of the horse next to the label. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,91</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a brand of tobacco

### riwai
 
 <i>n.</i> a potato

>  Their names have been unchanged, as they are still the same in other islands, whereas the potatoe [sic] is known by different names, as taiwa, riwai, &c., in different districts. <i>(Jul 3, 1847,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  <i>Riwai mau</i> would be potatoes for you to eat; <i>riwai mou</i>, potatoes to be eaten with your flesh, - an offensive curse. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  Then, I'd roll out my threadbare Whaariki/ - to help you remember to take your boots/ off - spread an old newspaper on the floor/ and on it place a steaming pot of puha,/ kamo-kamo, riwai, brisket-on-the-bone and/ dumplings what we call: doughboys. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: (Hone Tuwhare)]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> To others my love is a plaited kono / Full or empty, with chunks of riwai. <i>(1972,Collected Poems,537</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The "men" of one party were represented by small potatoes cut in two, called Riwai; those of the other by peachkernels, called Pititi. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),298</i>)

>  Maori, all t'at pomme-de-terre, t'at riwai, t'at spud, for you. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,163</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> MAHETAU, a potato. Syn. riwai; besides which a variety of other words are used to denote their different kinds. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,309</i>)

>  REWAI. - Potato. <i>Ibid.</i> 163 `Maori, all t'at pomme-de-terre, t'at riwai, t'at spud, for you.' <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,(Glossary) xiv</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> First of all, quantities of provisions were brought to the place, kumara, taro, hire, and rewai, birds preserved in oil, . <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,148,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `We're not going to eat dirt like this,' said Jance. `Who peeled the riwai today?' <i>(1948,Taina,167</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> There on the river flats were our crops of corn and kumara and riwai that we kids had to hoe. And here and there you see the nikau thatch roofs of our maize cribs. <i>(1966,Brown Man's Burden,120,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>141</b> riwai: potato. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> To others my love is a plaited kono/ Full or empty,/ With chunks of riwai,/ Meat that stuck to the stones. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,537,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> riwai potato <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> riwa potato <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a potato

>  She actually had to argue with a young Maori offender who sincerely believed that to be a Maori was to be automatically dumber; that to succeed even, in our society, it is necessary to neglect or even forget taha Maori. To become a riwai, a potato, dark outside, white inside. <i>(Sep 28, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  I have been publicly called a riwai (potato), as in brown on the outside, white on the inside. <i>(Sep 23, 2003,BLANK ENTRY,B4</i>)

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> She calls me her riwai - her potato. You get the idea? Brown on the outside, white and hard and cold on the inside. <i>(1981,Where to Go?,15</i>)

### roa
 
 <i>n.</i> (South Island) the great spotted kiwi. [ORIGIN: Maori]
