Till now scientists had recognised three species - the great spotted kiwi or roa in the northwest of the South Island, the little spotted kiwi on Kapiti and a few other islands, and the brown kiwi, scattered from Northland to Stewart Island. <i>(Apr 23, 1993,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Nine adult great spotted kiwi (roa) were released into the Rotoiti Nature Recovery Project area in which intensive pest control is creating a 5000-hectare protected zone for native species. <i>(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,12</i>)

>  <b>von HAAST</b> It was the same call as that of the Roa, but much louder and deeper ... <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,226</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 226]

>  <b>CAPLES</b> Near the mouth of the Middle Fiord [of Te Anau], they picked up a stone tomahawk and shot a `native emu'. The bird is described as being `curious among the feathered tribe, having neither wings nor tail'. Caples goes on to say that `Other night-loving birds, such as the keewee and green-bird promenaded around our camp all night.' ... [104] However I favour the conclusion that their `native emu' was the large type of kiwi (roa). <i>(1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1863 P.Q.Caples Diary in 1968 Hall-Jones Early Fiordland 103-104]

>  2. <i>Apteryx australis.</i> Roa. South Island Kiwi. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/191</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  Kakapos, kiwis, and roas, are plentiful on the south side of the river, and there are plenty of penguins on the north-east side of it. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/485</i>) [NOTES: (Sutherland)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> While camping on the shore of this lake [Maporika], I heard the cry of the rua, or large brown kiwi, now nearly extinct and very valuable; ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,68</i>) [NOTES: (West Coast)]

>  <i>Roa, n.</i> another Maori name for the largest or <i>Brown Kiwi ... </i> In Maori the word <i>roa</i> means long or big. <i>(1898,Austral English,390</i>)

>  Another remarkable flightless bird ... is the member of the <i>Apteryx</i> family commonly called by naturalists the roa'. The Maoris say that the proper name of this bird is the <i>`tokoweka'. </i> <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/338</i>)

>  <i>Apteryx haasti Potts. Great Grey Kiwi; Roa. </i>Judging from the scanty reports, this is still the rarest of the three species of <i>Apteryx</i>. Mr. R. S. Sutherland gives one South Island locality where it may be occasionally seen. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/82</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>roa, roaroa</i>, a bird (Apterxy haastii), large grey kiwi: South Island kiwi. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <b>POWELL</b> LARGE GREY KIWI (<i>Apteryx haastii</i>), Roa of the Maoris, is almost as large as australis, but is greyist brown, mottled and cross banded with brownish black. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,76</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> At nights, the others ... heard the distant call of a kiwi, `roa' the big Stewart Island kiwi is called, but we saw no trace of the bird. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,74</i>)

>  <i>~ n</i>. the largest kiwi, brown in color [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  3. <i>Apteryx haastii</i> Potts, 1872 <i>Great Spotted Kiwi (Roa)</i> South Island, chiefly west of the divide ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,15,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Great Spotted Kiwi Apteryx haastii</i> POTTS, 1872 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Roa, large grey kiwi. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For less talk and more sense we might begin with the far south of this country, where fishermen see themselves as Stewart Islanders - not roa or tokoeka! <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of tall fescue. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Kara cocksfoot may have an advantage sown as a mixture with either maru or roa. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,30</i>)

>  Cows on the grassgrub-tolerant species, roa tall fescue and maru phalaris milked-on for a further fortnight. <i>(1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,70</i>)

>  The principal advantages of Roa over ryegrass are its slightly earlier spring growth, its markedly greater vegetative growth over summer and the fact that it allows a greater clover component in a sward. <i>(Apr 1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

>  Roa is more persistent than perennial ryegrass over periods of summer drought. <i>(Mar 20, 1991,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

### road
 
 <i>v.</i> (transitive) also road out: to freight by road



 
 <i>n.</i> a log-way [(kauri bushmen's) ca 1951 R Gilberd (Okaihau) Letter an old name for logway.]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> If far from water, the carriage [of kauri logs] is more difficult. A skidded road, six to eight feet wide, is formed of greased logs. Thus a sort of rough wooden tramway is made. The logs are hoisted onto this road by means of `jacks', or dragged by a team of bullocks. <i>(1906,Plants of NZ,64,1940 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> .. a few possibly misleading terms [have been] varied, as for instance [Taylor's] use of `road' indiscriminately for bush trail, bridle track, or formed vehicular road. <i>(1966,Richard Taylor,10</i>)

### Road Board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  .. they had a Road Board composed of Messrs. M'Glashan, Proudfoot, and Reynolds. <i>(Apr 26, 1856,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  WAIAREKA ROAD BOARD. A meeting of this Board took place in the Road Board office, Tyne-street, on the 28th May. <i>(Jun 3, 1873,North Otago Times,4</i>)

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> Another remedy that might be tried with advantage is the abolition of the County Councils and Road Boards. <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,11</i>)

>  Nominations closed at noon to-day for the election of members to several of the district road boards. <i>(Apr 28, 1909,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  Later he became a contractor and many a mile of road in the Selwyn County and under the Rakaia Road Board was constructed under his supervision. <i>(Jul 2, 1929,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  In the local governing body, which began as a road board and developed into a borough council, Knox was always prominent. <i>(Feb 6, 1904,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  In a month or two there will be no Remuera Road Board - but there will be a City Council. <i>(Apr 19, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  The New Zealand Nurses' Memorial Fund has not been forgotten in the holiday season as evidenced by the following subscriptions .. Upper Wangaehu Road Board GBP10. <i>(Jan 1918,Kai Tiaki,19</i>)

>  The Road Board question is beginning to assume very serious dimensions in the County of Wairarapa East. <i>(Dec 9, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  When a member of the Road Board is voted to the chair, does he remain chairman for a year, or for three years? <i>(Jul 23, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Acland came home in the evening having had to attend a Road Board at Shanks when, like other road boards they squashed all their own actions of the last four months. <i>(May 14, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,132,1950 ed.</i>)

### road bongo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Life on the road lends itself naturally to hysteria - put eight people in a minibus for a day and eventually the giggling takes over. Rock groups on tour call it "road bongo". <i>(Sep 21, 1996,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

### Road Code
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Road code, drivers' licences and warrants of fitness were unheard of in those days. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,80</i>)

>  Road Code NZ set of rules issued officially for guidance of road-users. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,659</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### road end hut
 
 <i>n.</i> a tramping hut located near to the end of an access road.

>  Oriwa Biv is in a remote location where DOC does not maintain tracks, and Mt Dagg is a low-use road end hut. <i>(Dec 2004,Footnotes,22/1</i>)

### road gang
 
 <i>n.</i> a road-workers' team

>  A long time ago, going from here to there, we would come across a "road gang". Some would be shovelling, some working tractors and some leaning on shovels. <i>(May 17, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### Road Knights
 
 <i>n.</i> a mostly Pakeha bikie gang

>  Behind one set of palisades are the Devil's Henchmen. Inside the other are the Road Knights. Both are motor-cycle gangs, mainly Pakeha. <i>(May 11, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### road line
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a line cut through bushland or remote areas, to indicate a road to be laid. [aka road-line]

>  The next stage is that of the road-line being ditched on the sides, the stuff thrown upon it, and bridges erected where necessary by post and plank. <i>(Apr 19, 1851,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  It is rumored - but we can't vouch for the accuracy of it - that as soon as road lines are cut through the bush country the land will be thrown open for free selection as was done in Canterbury ... <i>(Oct 6, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  The two farms, with only a roadline between them, form a compact, easily-wrought and highly productive estate. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,50</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> With the discovery of a road-line striking directly inland from the coast, a new era dawned for pedestrian weeds. [road line; road-line] <i>(1921,Tutira,286</i>)

>  ALL that parcel of land containing by admeasurement 1 acre and 7 perches, situated in the Parish of Tuhikaramea, in the Newcastle Highway District, and being a road-line traversing Allotment 31, commencing on the south eastern boundary of Alottment 31 at a point distant 150 links from its southernmost angle. <i>(Mar 27, 1884,Waikato Times,3</i>)

>  With respect to the country sections, no progress has been made, in consequence, it is said, of some difficulty in the road lines on which the settlers are to take their frontage. <i>(Mar 7, 1851,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  NOTICE is Hereby Given, that, in consequence of repeated complaints made to the General Board of Road Trustees of obstruction and damage being caused to road lines within the Province, by parties digging holes in removing turf, and other material from, and otherwise injuring and obstructing said road lines, that proceedings will (without further notice) be at once taken, under the 51st section of the "Roads Ordinance, 1856," against any parties so offending. <i>(May 12, 1860,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  The members for the Papakaio Subdivision were empowered to have two footbridges erected on road lines in Block 3. <i>(Jun 11, 1879,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  An almost level grade traversing a portion of the original road line, and upon which the earthwork would be very light, has been obtained. <i>(Dec 14, 1909,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  A deputation of objectors to the taking of land in Kaiaua Block then waited upon the Council, and stated that their main objection was that the surveyed road line took away too much of their flat land, also that it was land that had belonged to the family for generations. <i>(Dec 3, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,11</i>)

>  A 9in. pipe drain 200 yards in length has been laid through Mr Bayne's land and then across the road line and into the creek to carry the rain water coming through the block cutting at the 10 mile peg. <i>(Sep 27, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Was there a little bridge to build, a small cutting to make, or a portion of an impassable road-line to be improved, there were many to contribute labour, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,160</i>)

>  The burnt area on each side of the road-line ... <i>(1898,TrNZI,399</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> A large clearing opened out on the right, and a little way back from the road-line stood a slab hut - or whare [e-acute], as it is generally called in New Zealand - of a rather better appearance than ordinary, from the iron chimney of which issued the smoke which the travellers had seen. Farther back from the road were yards and a larger building evidently used as a wool-shed. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,8</i>)

>  We were struggling home with a big night's catch [of rabbits] - struggling down to the road-line, along which the rabbit-cart would travel next day. <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,45</i>)

### road mob
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a group of stock driven on the hoof to or from saleyards.

>  <b>GORDON</b> For days, 'road-mobs' had been converging on Matawhero - recognised as the sale centre holding the largest cattle sales in the country. <i>(1990,Matawhero, Elsewhere and Other Things,17</i>)

### road party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAAST</b> Mr. Greenlaw was in charge of the road party, which ... had been sent up to form a bridle-path over the Pass,... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,119</i>)

### Road Services
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ..the Hari Hari team had charted [sic] the Road Services bus to take their team and supporters to the game. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,221</i>)

### road trustee
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  Every Road Trustee or member of a Road Board must have noticed the extraordinary number of excellent suggestions which have been made to him, both publicly and privately, and how utterly unable he is to carry them out. <i>(Jun 20, 1857,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Mr Martyn has always taken a prominent part in all matters affecting the welfare of the district, and has successively filled the office of Road Trustee, County Councillor and member of the South Auckland Cattle Board, besides taking a very active part as a member of the local racing clubs. <i>(Feb 10, 1881,Waikato Times,2</i>)

### road trusteeship
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  There are plenty of opportunites for a young gentleman to take part in the public business of the district in which he resides; there are the road trusteeships, the school committees, &c. &c. <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,5</i>)

### road weed
 
 <i>n.</i> wireweed

>  <b>HIL;GENDORF</b> WIREWEED (<i>Polygonum aviculare</i>), also called road weed, mat weed, knot grass, [pig weed,] and hog weed, is common in cultivated land all over the country. It is an annual, with stems up to 2ft. in length trailing over the ground. The stems have swollen nodes (`knot grass') and are very tough (`wireweed'). <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,61,1967 ed.</i>)

>  road weed [=] wireweed [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,64</i>)

### road-broken
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] accustomed to being driven on roads.

>  <b>MILLS</b> One day I came upon a large mob of cattle, weary and footsore. The leading dog had little to do. They had obviously been travelling for weeks and were well road-broken. <i>(1966,Huntaway,194</i>)

### road-hopper
 
 <i>n.</i> a hitch-hiker [(? obsolete)]

>  <b>GRAEME-HOLDER</b> Did you ever see such a lot of road-hoppers? <i>(1933,Restless Youth,99</i>)

### road-keeper
 
 <i>n.</i> a bushrangers' lookout [(criminals' obs)]

>  He was `road-keeper' in the gang; that is, he preceded the others, and while they were engaged with their victims, stood ready to give warning of danger to his companions. <i>(1866,Maungatapu Murders,11</i>)
